These results are achieved without task-specific image annotation or segmentation-model weight updates.
  </details>

- **[FloMo: 3D Scene Flow as a World Action Model Intermediate for Learning from Human Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05062)**  `arXiv:2611.05062`  
  _Jeremy Collins, Namra Patel, Ayush Agarwal, Shitij Govil, Animesh Garg_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pretrained video generation models distill internet-scale motion priors into robot policies, but full video is a redundant signal for action prediction. We argue that dense 3D motion is the medium through which video pretraining best transfers to robot actions, and that predicting motion rather than video affords better sample-efficiency for action prediction. FloMo is a 5B video-generation model fine-tuned to predict 3D scene-flow and action tokens. Rendering scene flow as RGB and encoding it into the same latent space as video enables a pretrained video generation backbone to efficiently adapt to predict 3D scene flow. Co-trained on action-free human video and action-labeled robot teleoperation data, our model benefits from human-video pretraining and generalizes to unseen objects, motion primitives, and tasks. Replacing motion prediction with a video prediction objective degrades out-of-distribution generalization, while adding a video prediction target alongside motion lowers policy success rather than improving it. When motion, video, and action are predicted jointly, action tokens attend far more to motion than to video, suggesting that predicting video wastes model capacity. Together, these results indicate that motion effectively mediates the transfer from video pretraining to action. More information can be found atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[Bg-Mix: On Using Image Backgrounds for Data Augmentation in Electron Microscopy Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03455)**  `arXiv:2611.03455`  
  _Ankur Srivastava, Joana Sarah Grah, Katharina Ladewig, Nils Körber_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electron microscopy (EM) serves as a cornerstone in virology enabling the visualization of viral particles and is widely used in research, diagnostics and morphological characterization of pathogens. Computer-assisted image analysis can help facilitate and speed up this labor-intensive method, however, typical EM virus datasets are small and sparsely labeled, resulting in a lack of readily available deep learning models due to training limitations. To overcome data shortages and leverage content-rich, diverse sample backgrounds, we employed background-based data augmentation. To this end, we propose Bg-Mix, a form of data augmentation method, which decomposes training images into foreground objects and backgrounds and subsequently rearranges both into novel augmented images. This not only enhances the performance of object detection models but also increases the diversity of the training data. Using Bg-Mix provides an increase between +0.8 and +3.3 box mean average precision (mAP) points over the baseline when tested across two datasets using two architectures, thereby demonstrating its effectiveness.
  </details>

- **[3D Gaussian Splatting within Self-Learned Neural View-Dependent Color Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03114)**  `arXiv:2611.03114`  
  _Yixiao Yang, Yu-Shen Liu, Zhizhong Han_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has become a vital tool in novel view synthesis. 3D Gaussian functions with attributes like color, opacity, and scale are stacked together to explicitly model a radiance field. However, discrete 3D Gaussians usually produce poor depth with few geometry details but lots of floaters, which hinders the effective modeling of smooth radiance fields. To address this issue, we propose to learn a radiance field within a continuous view-dependent color field parameterized by neural networks. With continuous constraints in color, the bias on low-frequency signals of neural networks can highly encourage the geometry modeling to contribute to high-frequency color variations on images, getting rid of solely relying on the color attribute itself. Moreover, we impose a constraint to improve the Lipschitz continuity of the neural color field, making large changes of each Gaussian's color also match with large changes of its position in terms of a ratio. To this end, we additionally introduce novel self-learning strategies to improve the learning of the neural view-dependent color field without any external knowledge or priors, aiming for better generalization on unseen scenes. Our numerical and visual comparisons on widely used benchmarks justify our idea and show better ability of high fidelity geometry recovery in 3DGS. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[PhotoGuider: Composition-Aware Cropping and 360° View Framing with Explainable Photographic Assessments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02897)**  `arXiv:2611.02897`  
  _Yuan-Kang Lee, Kuan-Lin Chen, Chia-Che Chang, Yu-Lun Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traditional image cropping assumes the photograph already contains the composition worth keeping, and asks only where to crop. A full 360$^{\circ}$ scene offers no such guarantee: the viewing direction in a scene has to be discovered before anything can be framed. We present PhotoGuider, a measure-then-frame method that answers both questions with compact learned scorers over one shared set of composition-aware features. The designed features are extracted using foundation and task-specific vision models and structured into four photographic tokens: Subject, Composition, Aesthetics, and Integrity. Our MLLM explainer is fine-tuned to generate composition-related explanations conditioned on images and measured token assessments. To our knowledge, PhotoGuider is the first method to combine explicit composition-based direction selection in 360$^{\circ}$ panoramas with refined framing, camera-parameter output for native re-rendering, and measurement-conditioned explanations. On two standard 2D cropping benchmarks, PhotoGuider achieves the highest average mIoU performance against ground-truth crops. In a blind ranking study, 120 participants ranked three cropping methods over 100 panoramic scenes from the public Poly Haven HDRI library, placing our framings first in 53.2% of cases, against 27.3% and 19.5% for two strong alternatives. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Gnomon4D: Learning Where and When to Focus for Geometric Motion-Grounded World-Action Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02474)**  `arXiv:2611.02474`  
  _Lingwei Dang, Yan Zhou, Juntong Li, Zhenhao Zhang, Yiteng Chen, Xiaoqiang Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-action models (WAMs) predict scene changes to guide robot actions, yet their RGB futures entangle state changes with appearance and lack 3D geometry. Recent WAMs add geometry as a training signal or a control interface, or build native 3D models, but decide what geometry to model rather than how the action should read it. Intuitively, control-relevant cues lie at embodiment--object interactions and their transitions, yet probing existing WAMs reveals that their action attention underweights these interactions in favor of the rest of the embodiment. To focus the action on these interactions, we present Gnomon4D, a WAM that jointly generates future video, robot actions, and GeoDelta, a video-aligned 4D geometric motion field recording the image-plane displacement and depth change of embodiment and object points. With the embodiment and objects in one field, their relative motion, which marks an interaction, can be read off. Guided by this motion and the task, Interaction-Salient Attention (InSAttn) selects where the action reads, and Transition-Anchored Aggregation (TransAgg) selects when, around grasp and release. As GeoDelta encodes surface motion rather than embodiment-specific controls, it serves as a human--robot quasi-action that links action-free pretraining on EgoManip-5000H, which we curate, to robot mid-training on Robot-30H. Gnomon4D leads on RoboTwin~2.0, VLABench, and RoboDojo, and outperforms real-robot baselines, especially under visual shifts. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Exploiting Textual Semantics for Robust Cross-View Object Correspondence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02356)**  `arXiv:2611.02356`  
  _Bing Fan, Yunhe Feng, Yan Huang, Heng Fan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-view object correspondence (CVOC) aims to establish associations of the same target between query and search videos captured from different viewpoints. Existing methods often rely on visual cues for CVOC. Despite recent progress, these methods struggle in complicated scenarios with substantial viewpoint changes and appearance variations, due to lacking sufficient target information. Addressing this, we introduce TeSCo, a framework that exploits rich Textual Semantics of an object, in addition to its visual cues, for cross-view object Correspondence. Our key insight is, textual description of a target, by capturing diverse attributes such as category, texture, and color, provides viewpoint- and appearance-invariant semantics, which are complementary to visual cues and can hence enhance correspondence robustness in complicated scenes. Inspired by this, TeSCo first generates a textual expression of the target from the given query view using a vision-language model, and then extracts textual feature to enhance visual features for localization in the search view. To realize this, we introduce a text-conditioned cross-modal fusion (TCF) module that incorporates the textual feature into visual features with text-guided modulation, producing more robust multimodal target representation for object correspondence. Since not all textual cues are equally compatible with the target in current search-view frame, we propose an iterative textual feature refinement (ITFR) module that progressively adjusts textual feature using search-view information before the TCF module, enabling search-view-aware textual semantics to be fused into visual features and leading to better performance. In extensive experiments on Ego-Exo4D and HANDAL-X, TeSCo demonstrates state-of-the-art results and largely outperforms other models, validating its efficacy. Our code is released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Conditional Diffusion-Based Generation of T2-Weighted Prostate MRI for Clinically Significant Prostate Cancer Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02197)**  `arXiv:2611.02197`  
  _Xavier Rafael-Palou, Alejandro Vergara-Richart, Ignacio Iborra Roncales, Carmen Prieto-de-la-Lastra, Andreu Antolin-Redondo, Juan Morote, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scarce and unrepresentative training data limit the performance of prostate MRI prediction models. Synthetic T2-weighted (T2W) MRI may augment training data containing clinically significant prostate cancer (csPCa) and transition zone (TZ) lesions. In this retrospective, single-center, ethics-committee-approved study, we developed a conditional generative model for T2W prostate MRI extending MAISI-v2 with csPCa class, zonal, and lesion-mask-guided conditioning, incorporating a relative advantage weighted loss fine-tuned on 1,303 patients (1,654 lesions). Fidelity was assessed by incremental ablation, and downstream utility was evaluated with T2W-based csPCa classifiers across five simulated training-data scenarios, each testing 18 synthetic-data configurations. The proposed model reduced Fréchet inception distance (FID) from 13.28 (pretrained MAISI-v2) to $2.04 \pm 0.06$ internally (PI-CAI: $3.83 \pm 0.51$). Synthetic augmentation significantly increased AUC in all five scenarios (+0.027 to +0.088; adjusted $p = 0.016$--$0.043$), though cross-institutional augmentation of PI-CAI yielded a non-significant gain (+0.013; $p = 0.397$). Overall, our model improved T2W MRI fidelity, and synthetic images improved csPCa classification under simulated data constraints, although benefit did not transfer significantly across institutions.
  </details>

- **[Testing concept-level audits of melanoma classifiers with diffusion counterfactuals and matched positive controls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02119)**  `arXiv:2611.02119`  
  _Md Shohanoor Rahman, Md Hasibul Islam, Md Kishor Morol, Dip Nandi, Tze Hui Liew_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Concept-based explanations of melanoma classifiers are rarely tested against controls with known targets. We built a counterfactual audit from a diffusion model conditioned on seven 7-point checklist concepts and tested it with such controls. Colour-calibrated start noise reduced colour outliers from 78.5-84.8\% to 6.6-13.3\%. Four of seven knobs were target-dominant but also changed other concepts. Quartile-based positive controls were confounded, and the audit failed its pre-specified rule. It recovered the targets of all 16 controls matched on the generator's pseudo-labels and of 12 of 12 controls labelled by a second evaluator, for three concepts; concept activation vectors recovered fewer (11 of 16). In 18 permuted-label classifiers, significant sweep responses and target rankings also arose, whereas standardised effect margins separated the controls from these null classifiers. The pigmentation sweep raised the melanoma logit in 15 of 15 ISIC 2020 classifiers (+3.12 on average) and 6 of 6 HAM10000 classifiers (+4.01), about four times the null response in scale-free units; the regression effect did not transfer. An exploratory decomposition, evaluated against the controls, suggested that knob cross-talk partly hides vascular responses. Matched controls and null classifiers are practical checks of concept audits.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Group and Order: Cross-Instance Self-Supervised RL for Vision-Centric MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00892)**  `arXiv:2611.00892`  
  _Songming Yang, Hong-Tao Yu, Xiu-Shen Wei_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training has become an important paradigm for improving multimodal large language models (MLLMs), especially with automatically verifiable rewards. Self-supervised visual pretext tasks provide such rewards without human annotations. However, existing methods mainly exploit within-instance structure, offering limited supervision for cross-instance discrimination and fine-grained comparison. We introduce Group-and-Order Self-Supervised Reinforcement Learning (GO-SSL), a cross-instance jigsaw framework for vision-centric MLLM post-training. Given two visual instances, GO-SSL mixes their local elements and trains the model to group them by instance of origin while recovering the order within each group, coupling instance-level comparison with spatial, temporal, or geometric structure recovery. This paired formulation turns the same unlabeled data into richer verifiable supervision through diverse pairings, shuffles, and a random-to-hard curriculum. We instantiate GO-SSL mainly on cross-image jigsaw tasks and further extend it to 3D depth and video temporal ordering. Across diverse benchmarks, GO-SSL consistently improves over the base MLLM and prior self-supervised post-training baselines, with clear gains in fine-grained perception and spatial understanding. Further evaluations across model scales and families support its generality. These results suggest that cross-instance comparison provides a useful self-supervised RL signal for improving transferable visual understanding in MLLMs. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[GeoFlip Preview: Benchmarking Geometry-Sensitive Physics in Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42002)**  `arXiv:2610.42002`  
  _Yuqi Li, Junming Liu, Bowen Hu, Ruize Niu, Huiran Duan, Ming-Hsuan Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generation models synthesize realistic motion, but can they respond correctly to changes in initial geometry? We introduce GeoFlip, a video generation benchmark centered on physical outcome flips induced by geometry. With shared physical settings and textual prompts held fixed, GeoFlip applies controlled interventions to support extent, object placement, and mechanism configuration, constructing paired scenes with contrasting reference events, such as remaining stable versus falling or tipping. These pairs test whether models translate geometric differences in the first frame into correct physical evolution. The benchmark comprises 482 scene pairs and 964 reference videos across six physical categories, with reference outcomes established through procedural scene construction, physics simulation, and numerical validation and verification of physical events. We evaluate 16 models for image to video generation along three dimensions: event correctness, structural integrity, and similarity to reference videos. We examine evaluation consistency with independent vision language judges. On the 442 conditions shared by all models, the highest event correctness averaged across tasks is 27.98%, revealing substantial limitations in physical generation conditioned on geometry. Model strengths vary across physical mechanisms, and the three evaluation dimensions capture distinct capabilities. Typical failures include ignoring geometric changes, generating incorrect object motion, bending rigid objects, and introducing additional components. Rank correlations between judges reach 0.906 for event correctness and 0.929 for structural integrity. By combining controlled geometric interventions, verifiable physical references, and multidimensional evaluation, GeoFlip assesses progress from visual realism toward physical consistency conditioned on geometry.
  </details>

- **[TokenRepel: Generating Diverse Image Sets Without Compromising Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39913)**  `arXiv:2610.39913`  
  _Qingtao Yu, Zhen Wang, Ning Lai, Boyang Hu, Hongdong Li, Dylan Campbell_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present an inference-time approach that enhances the visual diversity of image sets generated by text-to-image models, without compromising the average image quality. Our method leverages the intrinsic geometry of hidden representations in pretrained models to diversify images generated from the same prompt. First, we introduce a token repulsion mechanism that increases the angle between feature tokens across samples. It takes a negative Riemannian gradient step on a von Mises-Fisher mixture distribution of tokens on the unit hypersphere. Second, to preserve the quality of each output image, we propose a token density preservation mechanism that projects the repulsion-induced prediction change onto a half-space aligned with the model's score direction. This encourages the updates to remain within the high-density regions learned by the generative model. We further extend this mechanism to the output of each intermediate layer, enabling finer-grained iterative guidance. We evaluate our method on three types of pretrained text-to-image models. Experiments show substantial gains in visual diversity under identical prompts, while maintaining a comparable level of image fidelity.
  </details>

- **[From Presence Labels to Bounding Boxes: Can Multimodal Large Language Models Scale Surgical Instrument Localization?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34218)**  `arXiv:2610.34218`  
  _Mohamed Hamdy, Muraam Abdel-Ghani, Fatmaelzahraa Ahmed, Sifna Nasar, Mariam Ahmed, Khalid Al-Jalham, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial localization of surgical instruments through bounding-box annotations is a foundational component of surgical scene understanding, supporting detection, tracking, interaction analysis, and grounded vision-language applications. Yet existing annotations often cover selected frames, video subsets, or dataset variants rather than complete procedures. Manual extension is time-consuming and expert-dependent. Additionally, weakly supervised and detector-based pseudo-labels require task-specific training and may be imprecise. In this study, we design a scalable MLLM-based annotation framework that converts surgical frames and existing instrument-presence labels into accurate bounding boxes, and systematically analyze its reliability, cost, and design sensitivity. We instantiate the framework using six MLLMs and a multi-call configuration on laparoscopic cholecystectomy in Cholec80. Using a carefully designed stratification scheme, two annotators independently label 1,000 frames, and an expert surgeon reviews one annotation set to establish the reference. Results show that instrument-set F1 ranges from 88.2 to 92.7,whereas mIoU ranges from 0.180 to 0.735, showing that comparable recognition can conceal large localization differences. A structured multi-stage annotation pipeline achieves 0.784 mIoU, compared with 0.735 for the best single-call baseline and 0.814 human-human agreement. Using the best accuracy pipeline, we create and publicly release SurgBox, a spatial extension of Cholec80 comprising 285,977 bounding boxes across all 184,498 annotated frames from the 80 videos. The annotations and human references are made available on GitHub
  </details>

- **[From Augmentation to Synthetic Evidence: Evaluating Generated Chest Radiographs at Dataset Intake](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29152)**  `arXiv:2610.29152`  
  _Shaikh Akib Shahriyar, Zinedine Sehili, Adam Arthur, Christopher Schwartz_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While generated chest radiographs can serve as valuable data augmentation when explicitly declared, the same pixels become unsupported evidence when submitted as records of real clinical examinations. We study this dataset-intake vulnerability by disentangling three questions: (1) whether an image passes an automated technical quality check, (2) whether an origin classifier flags it as synthetic, and (3) whether a trusted provenance record supports the claimed clinical acquisition. In this study, a submitter uses Qwen3.8-27B to select up to 20 candidate radiographs from pools of 1,000 Cheff-identified or SynthCheX images, while a receiver evaluates image quality via MedGemma 1.5 and predicts origin using a linear probe over frozen DINOv3 features. Although quality selection increases MedGemma quality passes from 12/20 to 19/20 for Cheff-identified images and from 8/20 to 17/20 for SynthCheX, we find no evidence that candidate selection increases passage through both checks simultaneously: zero selected Cheff-identified images pass both gates, and uniform, quality-guided, and surrogate-guided selection yield 2, 1, and 2 SynthCheX passes out of 20, respectively. On unprocessed test cohorts, the origin classifier rejects 4,677 of 5,000 SynthCheX images at a nominal 5% false-alarm rate (flagging 110 of 2,297 real NIH radiographs), yet leaves 152 SynthCheX images that pass both pixel-level checks. Moreover, pixel-level screening is fragile to routine distribution shifts: JPEG compression reduces SynthCheX-versus-NIH AUROC from 0.987 to 0.841, and applying the NIH-calibrated threshold to an external genuine cohort (Open-I) triggers false alarms on 568 of 957 authentic radiographs (59.4%). Because pixel-level checks can filter submissions but cannot verify clinical acquisition claims, we argue that dataset intake pipelines must record and verify source provenance independently of image pixels.
  </details>

- **[AEON: Unifying Video and 3D World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28631)**  `arXiv:2610.28631`  
  _Weiqi Zhang, Wenyuan Zhang, Yu-Shen Liu, Junsheng Zhou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current world models for explicit world simulation typically follow two distinct pathways: (1) video generative models (e.g., Sora), which simulate realistic appearances but fail to preserve 3D consistency; and (2) 3D generative models (e.g., Marble), which generate geometrically consistent scenes but often yield static, less realistic observations. We present AEON, a unified framework that bridges video-based and 3D-based world models and combines the benefits of both. The key insight of AEON lies in a generative model that operates within the text- or image-conditioned video latent space while producing renderable 4D graphics primitives---specifically, 4D Gaussian Splatting (4DGS)---as the explicit world representation. We train a 4D reconstruction model to predict both 4DGS and camera poses from monocular videos, serving as our initialized 4D decoder. Furthermore, we design a domain adapter that translates generative video latents into reconstructive 4D latents through a distillation process on the video VAE. AEON naturally supports video-based world modeling by rendering 4DGS at predicted camera trajectories, and constructs a persistent 4D environment with scene dynamics for realistic world simulation. Consequently, AEON empowers a diverse range of world modeling applications and achieves state-of-the-art performance on standard benchmarks. Project Page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[FrameScout: Scouting Query-Relevant Frames for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23975)**  `arXiv:2610.23975`  
  _Haonan Hu, Shuhao Chen, Weisen Jiang, Lizhao Gao, James Kwok, Yu Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long video understanding requires VideoLLMs to answer queries about videos spanning tens of minutes to hours, but such long videos produce far more visual tokens than current VideoLLMs can process. To address this issue, keyframe selectors are proposed to select the most query-relevant frames by computing the similarity between frame and query embeddings. Existing selectors either encode frames independently without temporal context or process chunks in isolation with ranking objectives that are difficult to optimize. To alleviate those limitations, we propose FrameScout with two core designs: (i) a streaming selector architecture that leverages a sliding-window KV cache and successor aggregation to produce temporally-contextualized frame embeddings; (ii) a frame-query contrastive objective that aligns frame and query embeddings in a shared space, directly training the selector to distinguish relevant frames from irrelevant ones. Extensive experiments on three long-video benchmarks and six base VideoLLMs demonstrate that FrameScout consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[FutureLift: Lifting Trajectory Proposals into Bidirectional Future Graphs for Motion Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22215)**  `arXiv:2610.22215`  
  _Daniel Grimm, J. Marius Zöllner_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate motion prediction is critical for safe autonomous driving, yet even strong forecasters leave residual errors in their multimodal proposals. Existing refiners compress complete proposals or update their segments sequentially, preventing the full proposed future from serving as explicit relational context for correction. We introduce FutureLift, a backbone-agnostic refiner that represents each multimodal proposal as a heterogeneous future graph, with spatially grounded temporal nodes connected to agent histories, map elements, alternative modes, and, for joint prediction, other agents future trajectories. Bidirectional temporal edges allow later evidence to revise earlier segments in a single pass, and the updated nodes predict residual coordinate and confidence corrections. Requiring only proposals, scores, and scene context, FutureLift provides a common refinement interface for structurally different forecasting backbones. On Argoverse2 and the Waymo Open Motion Dataset, it consistently improves frozen proposals and outperforms native and specialized refiners across marginal and joint forecasting. On the WOMD motion-prediction leaderboard, FutureLift ranks first in minADE (0.5049m) and minFDE (0.9942m) among the compared standard-input methods, without LiDAR or ensembling, while achieving a competitive 0.4874 Soft mAP.
  </details>

- **[From Adults to Children: Benchmarking Foundation Models for Chest X-Ray Classification under Domain Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21809)**  `arXiv:2610.21809`  
  _Sara Inácio, Hugo Proença, João C. Neves_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Foundation models have shown significant potential across diverse fields and have been applied to medical imaging, but their robustness across patient populations remains unclear. In this work, we investigate foundation models' ability to generalize from adult to pediatric chest X-ray classification. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first benchmark to compare multiple foundation models under a common adult-to-pediatric evaluation setting while also analyzing the effect of adaptation depth. We harmonize VinDr-CXR and PediCXR into a five-label classification task and evaluate six foundation models and two CNN baselines. All models are adapted using adult data only and tested in both the in-domain and pediatric domains using linear probing, partial fine-tuning, and full fine-tuning. The benchmark reveals a clear and consistent trend: all models suffer a substantial performance degradation when transferred from adult to pediatric data, with drops exceeding 10 percentage points. While Macro-AUROC values reach approximately 92% on the adult test set, performance decreases to around 78% on the pediatric domain. Another important finding is that increasing the depth of adaptation improves pediatric performance on average, although the effect varies across models. Moreover, strong in-domain performance does not necessarily indicate greater robustness to domain shift.
  </details>

- **[AdaLoop: Thinking with the Vision Encoder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21323)**  `arXiv:2610.21323`  
  _Xinran Nicole Han, Yiyang Lu, Katelyn Gan, Todd Zickler_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most vision-language models (VLMs) treat the vision encoder as a static feature extractor: it encodes an image once, independently of the question, and leaves subsequent reasoning to the language model. Yet different questions may require different visual operations and processing depths. We present AdaLoop, a framework for question-conditioned visual recurrence in VLMs. AdaLoop adapts visual computation to the question and reuses the encoder's transformations across multiple iterations, with the pretrained vision weights frozen. We also introduce VisRO, a procedural dataset and generation pipeline for training and evaluating vision-centric reasoning. On six VisRO tasks, AdaLoop outperforms standard fine-tuning and question-conditioned single-pass baselines with matched training data and optimizer updates. These gains hold across three VLM families on both in-distribution and out-of-distribution examples. Our findings suggest that pretrained vision encoders can contribute to reasoning through task-directed, iterative visual computation.
  </details>

- **[Feed-Forward Multi-View Feature Coding for 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20590)**  `arXiv:2610.20590`  
  _Seungmi Choi, Ji-Hyoun Choi, Suhyun Jo, Tae-Gwon Park, Hui Yong Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) methods enable efficient reconstruction of 3D scenes from sparse multi-view images in a single forward pass, without scene-specific optimization. However, existing feed-forward scene-coding pipelines follow a "generate-then-compress" framework, first generating a Gaussian representation and then encoding its geometry and appearance through separate coding paths, complicating codec design and limiting the exploitation of cross-attribute redundancy. We propose multi-view feature coding for Gaussian splatting (MFGS), an end-to-end feed-forward framework for scene coding. Instead of compressing an explicit Gaussian representation, MFGS extracts multi-view features from the input images and encodes them into a compact bitstream. Through end-to-end rate-distortion training, MFGS learns a compact latent representation of multi-view features that jointly represents geometry and appearance, enabling the receiver to predict Gaussian attributes from the decoded features. We further introduce importance-guided Gaussian selection to accommodate different rendering budgets, allowing the receiver to adjust the number of Gaussians used for rendering from the same bitstream. Across RE10K, ACID, and DL3DV, MFGS achieves 99.76-99.86% bitrate savings over the generate-then-compress pipeline, as measured by BD-rate, while reducing total GMACs by 52-55% and providing an approximately 12 times end-to-end speedup.
  </details>

- **[Dynamic Quadtree Tokenization and Transformer for Adaptive Mesh PDE Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04044)**  `arXiv:2610.04044`  
  _Yilin Zhuang, Noah Zambrano, Karthik Duraisamy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The quadratic attention cost of Vision Transformers (ViTs) forces a trade-off between spatial resolution and rollout horizon, particularly for fine-scale PDEs where shocks, reaction fronts, and material interfaces occupy small, evolving regions of the domain. Conventional neural surrogates also lack mechanisms to adapt resolution dynamically. We propose WAMRViT, a ViT that tokenizes inputs as balanced quadtrees using a wavelet-inspired refinement criterion, jointly encodes position and refinement level with 3D rotary positional embeddings, and regrids in cell space during inference for stable long-horizon rollouts. A multi-scale variant retains each leaf at its native source resolution and lets the model learn across resolution levels. Unlike fixed-budget adaptive-tokenization methods, WAMRViT imposes no predetermined token count and supports fully adaptive topology throughout autoregressive rollout. To our knowledge, it is the first machine-learning surrogate to natively tokenize multi-level Adaptive Mesh Refinement (AMR) data. On uniform-grid benchmarks, uniform-patch WAMRViT improves finest-level region-of-interest VRMSE over a finest-patch uniform ViT while using substantially fewer tokens. The multi-scale variant achieves the lowest first-step full-field RMSE and VRMSE on both benchmarks and improves rollout-averaged full-field and refined-region accuracy at long horizons. With parallelized regridding, its end-to-end rollout cost lies between finest-patch and approximately token-matched coarser-patch ViTs. On a complex AMR combustion problem whose finest features cannot be represented natively by the evaluated uniform-grid baselines, WAMRViT operates on adaptive cells built from the simulator hierarchy and substantially reduces finest-level error at matched transformer capacity. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[What Do Verifiable Rewards Teach Video-Language Models About Time? A Controlled Multi-Model Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03792)**  `arXiv:2610.03792`  
  _Avyay Sadhu, Patrick Cooper_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) has produced large reasoning gains in language models, and verifiable video benchmarks make the recipe directly applicable to causal-temporal video question answering. We present a controlled study of what RLVR actually teaches video-language models about temporal structure. We fine-tune four open models with group relative policy optimization (GRPO) under three data recipes using annotated synthetic questions, self-supervised real-video pretext tasks, and a one-to-one mixture, plus a targeted real-video augmentation arm. We evaluate the model-recipe grid on 105 CLEVRER questions with one correct option and on two real-video transfer sets. The 8B ordered gain is 4.8 points (95% video-bootstrap CI [-5.6, 15.1]), while blind accuracy increases 10.5 points ([0.9, 20.6]). The 4B model gains an accuracy advantage for ordered over shuffled frames. On the real-video sets, the 8B synthetic-only checkpoint loses 26.7 and 25.2 points, with smaller regressions under mixed and augmented recipes. In a separate scene-disjoint, single-gold Qwen3-VL-8B experiment, supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and GRPO each complete 1,200 updates at three fixed seeds. GRPO minus SFT is -3.89 points in ordered accuracy (nominal 97.5% video-bootstrap CI [-7.36, -0.45]) and -2.78 points in ordered-minus-shuffled accuracy ([-6.60, 0.87]). Transfer differences are sensitive to parseability. Intervals quantify test-video uncertainty conditional on the fitted runs. The results show why benchmark accuracy, transfer, and presented-order sensitivity need to be evaluated separately.
  </details>

- **[SoL-Refiner: Speed-of-Light One-Step Refinement for High-Resolution Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37969)**  `arXiv:2609.37969`  
  _Haozhe Liu, Tian Ye, Shuchen Xue, Sensen Gao, Yitong Li, Junsong Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-resolution video generation is expensive, as its cost grows rapidly with the number of spatiotemporal tokens. A practical alternative first generates a lower-resolution video and then applies a refiner, but conventional multi-step refinement introduces a second sampling bottleneck. We present SoL-Refiner, a one-step video refiner that transforms low-resolution model outputs into 4K videos with a single denoising step. Our three-stage recipe combines high-resolution continual training, reinforcement learning (RL) post-training, and a final one-step distillation. We introduce Refiner-Bench, a video refinement benchmark constructed from the outputs of different video generators, and use a shared-input protocol to compare refiners at approximately 2K output resolution. With LTX-2.3 Refiner as the base model, the one-step SoL-Refiner outperforms all external refiners at 2K on the VBench and UniPercept averages, while at $3840\!\times\!2176$ it improves both metrics over the three-step LTX-2.3 Refiner. With the complete acceleration stack, SoL-Refiner achieves an $8.91\times$ speedup in refinement latency over the same baseline in our 2K latency setting. With MiniMax H3 as the base model, SoL-Refiner produces 2K videos in three refinement steps and is preferred over the official MiniMax H3 2K API in 87% of user-study judgments.
  </details>

- **[Marathoner: Ultra-Long-Horizon Autonomous Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34378)**  `arXiv:2609.34378`  
  _Ruiyang Zhang, Jinpeng Ou, Yifan Xie, Jingang Zhou, Lirui Pan, Qingpei Guo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans naturally possess the ability to work persistently toward long-term goals. Given a challenging task, humans can continuously work for months or even years to accomplish a specific objective. In this paper, we propose Marathoner, an autonomous agentic model possessing the ability of ultra-long-horizon execution. Specifically, we propose a comprehensive post-training pipeline to instill this critical capability into base model. For Ultra-Long-Horizon Task Synthesis, we leverage major release PRs containing 1000+ lines of new code from diverse GitHub repositories as the primary source for synthesizing challenging task-level data. Additionally, we introduce Multi-Task Chaining, which chains multiple generated tasks into a single more challenging task, enabling the synthesis of tasks with frontier-level difficulty. For rejection sampling finetuning, we combine strong teacher model with diverse harnesses to generate trajectories on our synthesized tasks and conduct supervised finetuning on base model with rejection sampled trajectories. For reinforcement learning, cold-started model performs real-world execution through harnesses in independent sandboxes during rollout process, effectively facilitating the acquisition of genuine ultra-long-horizon execution capability. We further propose a novel reward strategy, Later Stage Bonus Reward, which explicitly encourages model to perform meaningful maneuvers during later stages of execution. Through extensive evaluation on 5 benchmarks containing ultra-long-horizon tasks, Marathoner achieves consistent and substantial performance improvements over base model and even surpasses performance of strong proprietary model. Further analysis shows that Marathoner can consistently work for 10+ hours and conduct 1000+ tool calls on highly challenging tasks.
  </details>

- **[MM-VeriAgent: Learning to Use Extensive Tools to Verify Multimodal Misinformation with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30698)**  `arXiv:2609.30698`  
  _Peipei Li, Shuhan Xia, Shengyang Liu, Zekun Li, Ran He_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world multimodal misinformation often involves mixed forgery sources, requiring sample-specific detection strategies. Existing tool-augmented methods rely on predefined workflows or inference-time planning, limiting adaptability or increasing inference cost. To address this issue, we introduce \textbf{MM-VeriAgent}, which learns to verify mixed-source multimodal misinformation with tools. We first build \textbf{MM-VeriTools}, a specialized toolkit for misinformation detection agents. By benchmarking various candidate models and methods on the sub-tasks required by mixed-source detection, we select the strongest for textual, visual, and cross-modal forgery analysis and encapsulate them as callable tools with a unified interface. On top of this toolkit, we train the LVLM agent with reinforcement learning to teach it how to use these tools to better solve mixed-source detection. Since many of the tools are specialized models whose online execution at every rollout severely limits RL efficiency, we further introduce \textbf{Tool-Execution Cache}, which pre-executes candidate tool calls and reuses their cached outputs during training. This preserves multi-step rollouts while reducing online tool execution, largely improving the trainingthis http URLon MMFakeBench demonstrate substantial accuracy gains over the base model without explicit tool search at inference time. Ablation and efficiency analyses further validate the learned tool-use policy and show that Tool-Execution Cache reduces online tool executions during training.
  </details>

- **[MMAP: Multimodal Missing-Aware Pretraining for Longitudinal Alzheimer's Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26617)**  `arXiv:2609.26617`  
  _Fiona Kekwick, Matthew Baugh, Bernhard Kainz, Paul M. Matthews, Wenjia Bai_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical decision making heavily relies on predicting the disease progression trajectory by seeking to understand patient's health status which is characterised by multimodal medical data. AI holds great potential for learning useful representations from multimodal medical data to predict disease progression and aid clinical decision making. However, development of predictive AI models is constrained by missing modalities and incomplete tabular data frequently occurring in medical datasets. In addition, disease labels alone may only provide limited supervisory signals for learning representations from high-dimensional multimodal data. Here, we present MMAP, a novel Multimodal Missing-aware Alignment Pretraining method for learning image-tabular representations from incomplete data. An image encoder is pretrained with efficient sigmoid contrastive learning combined with generative reconstruction. A tabular encoder is built upon a tabular foundation model. A missing token generator enables the two encoders to take incomplete data as input, enabling the model to be robust against missing modalities, either with missing images or missing tabular data. We evaluate the clinical usefulness of the learnt multimodal representations on two challenging longitudinal clinical tasks for Alzheimer's disease: predicting disease stage conversion and predicting amyloid status. The proposed method outperforms strong multimodal and unimodal baselines.
  </details>

- **[VicEdit: Learning to Edit Videos from Visual In-Context Examples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16745)**  `arXiv:2608.16745`  
  _Yuji Wang, Teng Hu, Yuheng Chen, Ran Yi, Han Feng, Weijian Cao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite remarkable progress in instruction-based video editing, textual descriptions remain limited in expressing fine-grained appearance, spatial transformations, and temporal dynamics. We introduce Visual In-context Editing, a new paradigm that shifts video editing from text-only instructions to visual demonstrations, where users specify desired edits through heterogeneous visual references including single images, image pairs, and video pairs. To establish this paradigm, we curate VicEdit-400K, the first large-scale dataset for visual in-context video editing, containing 400K high-quality samples across ten editing tasks with comprehensive semantic and visual consistency filtering. Building upon this foundation, we propose VicEdit, a unified framework that enables effective reasoning over diverse visual contexts and textual instructions. Specifically, we introduce Modality-Adaptive Semantic Distillation to extract modality-aware visual editing semantics and Dual-Context Injection to align visual contexts with textual editing intents. Extensive experiments on VicEdit-Bench demonstrate that VicEdit achieves state-of-the-art performance on both conventional instruction-based editing and visual in-context editing. Further controlled studies and human evaluations verify that VicEdit effectively leverages heterogeneous visual references, establishing visual in-context editing as a promising paradigm for controllable video editing.
  </details>

- **[RA-ClipScore: Making Generative Model Evaluation More Interpretable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12088)**  `arXiv:2608.12088`  
  _Yifan Lu, Taras Kucherenko, Hedvig Kjellström, Judith Bütepage_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative models can produce images nearly indistinguishable from real data, yet rigorous and interpretable evaluation remains challenging. Conventional metrics such as FID provide only scalar scores with limited diagnostic insight. Widely adopted CLIP-based metrics enable semantic evaluation beyond simple training class labels, but inherit limitations from CLIP's training paradigm that restrict attribute-wise analysis. We propose RA-CLIPScore, a novel metric that mitigates these issues and extends CLIP-based evaluation to spatial distribution alignment, measuring whether generated objects adhere to the positional priors found in the training data. RA-CLIPScore introduces dual prompts to decouple competing attributes and leverages local patch tokens to capture fine-grained regional semantics. We evaluate image generative models on their ability to match both attribute and spatial distributions of the training data. Extensive experiments show that RA-CLIPScore provides more robust and interpretable evaluations than prior methods, particularly under distribution misalignment or partially irrelevant textual attributes. We further demonstrate how it reveals spatial biases in generative models. User evaluations confirm that Regional Single Attribute Divergence based on our RA-CLIPScore aligns more closely with human perception of visual diversity than existing semantic metrics.
  </details>

- **[Fast and Memory-Efficient Wavelet Convolutions via I/O-Aware Reformulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10805)**  `arXiv:2608.10805`  
  _Amit Aflalo, Shahaf E. Finder, Roy Amoyal, Eran Treister, Oren Freifeld_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wavelet convolution (WTConv) has emerged as an increasingly popular drop-in replacement for standard convolutions, expanding a network's receptive field exponentially with the number of decomposition levels while keeping the parameter count linear. However, its reference implementation is severely memory-bound due to excessive data movement through high-bandwidth memory (HBM). We develop an I/O model of WTConv to characterize this bottleneck and use it to guide three algebraic reformulations: (1) recomputing the inexpensive Haar analysis butterfly on chip, (2) collapsing the multi-level synthesis cascade into a single closed-form pass indexed by output-coordinate bits, and (3) folding learned per-channel scales into the convolution weights. Together, these reformulations enable an I/O-aware fused implementation that substantially reduces HBM traffic. We evaluate the WTConvNeXt configuration across decomposition levels and a broad range of tensor shapes. Despite performing comparable arithmetic, the reference WTConv is substantially slower than the depthwise convolution it replaces. Our reformulation reduces modeled HBM traffic by approximately $2.55\times$, yielding up to a $4.35\times$ training speedup over the reference while roughly halving peak memory usage. Thus, our reformulation preserves the benefits of WTConv while substantially reducing its execution time and memory footprint, removing the systems overhead that previously limited its practical efficiency.
  </details>

- **[A Diffusion-Based Kinematics and Dynamics Model for 3D Sign Language Production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14836)**  `arXiv:2607.14836`  
  _Emanuele Colonna, Moises Diaz, Gennaro Vessio, Miguel Angel Ferrer, Giovanna Castellano_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sign Language Production (SLP) generates continuous signing from a linguistic input and drives accessibility technologies such as signing avatars. Current generators, diffusion models included, regress joint coordinates as an unstructured vector, so nothing ensures that the generated body respects anatomy or the mechanics of movement. Physics has entered SLP only through geometric loss terms, whose benefit for intelligibility has not been verified, and the dynamics of the signing body has not been modelled. We present PIDiffSign, a diffusion model informed by physics at two levels. For kinematics, a forward-kinematics head outputs bone directions and lengths and composes them into an anatomically exact skeleton. For dynamics, a rigid-body model of the upper body with anthropometric parameters supports two training priors. A physical-plausibility suite that includes two laws of motor control evaluates the generated signing. Under a controlled protocol, the head lowers back-translation word error rate by about five points on PHOENIX14T and CSL-Daily, whereas geometric penalties have no measurable effect; a control experiment traces the gain to the kinematic-chain representation and not to bone-length conservation. The dynamics priors gain about one further point over matched non-physical controls on PHOENIX14T, but not on CSL-Daily. Generated signing follows the laws of motor control as closely as real signing but carries markedly less kinetic energy. The exact skeletons can drive rigged avatars directly, and the suite offers a physical benchmark for future SLP systems.
  </details>

- **[AbsoluteDegradation: A Physics-Inspired Synthetic Film-Degradation Pipeline and Archival Film Restoration Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02131)**  `arXiv:2607.02131`  
  _Mikołaj Jastrzębski, Dawid Glinkowski, Dawid Zieliński, Daniel Borkowski, Wojciech Kozłowski, Kamil Adamczewski_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Restoring archival film remains a fundamentally challenging problem due to the absence of paired training data and the lack of standardized evaluation benchmarks. Pristine versions of deteriorated footage are physically unrecoverable, requiring supervised methods to rely on synthetic data that often fail to capture the complex, temporally coherent nature of real film degradation. At the same time, existing real-world datasets are limited in scale, quality, and accessibility, hindering reliable evaluation and fair comparison across methods. We address both limitations with AbsoluteDegradation, a physics-inspired, modular pipeline for synthesizing realistic film degradations, and a new large-scale archival benchmark. The proposed pipeline models the analog-to-digital process as a structured composition of artifact families, incorporating signal-dependent grain, parametric scratches, and temporally coherent camera motion, enabling controlled generation of diverse degradation regimes. In parallel, we introduce a curated dataset of 81,576 high-resolution frames sourced from real archival footage, designed for consistent evaluation under real-world conditions. Together, these contributions provide a unified framework for training and benchmarking restoration models. Experiments across six restoration architectures, evaluated with no-reference image quality metrics, clip-level temporal quality assessment, and a blinded human preference study (93 raters, 1,395 trials), show that models trained with AbsoluteDegradation generalize better to real-world footage: human raters preferred them in 64.7% of comparisons and for every evaluated architecture. The proposed benchmark also reveals systematic failure modes of current methods and of no-reference metrics. We hope this work establishes a foundation for reproducible and domain-authentic evaluation in archival film restoration.
  </details>

- **[Where the Real Clips Came From: Corpus Identity in AI-Generated Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31004)**  `arXiv:2606.31004`  
  _Mert Onur Cakiroglu, Zhihe Lu, Mehmet Dalkilic, Hasan Kurban_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmarks for detecting AI-generated video take their real clips from a few curated collections, so a clip's label is collinear with where it came from. We call a score's dependence on this origin corpus identity, and we measure it on four benchmarks and six real collections. Three probes read a file's declared frame types, the motion vectors its codec stores, and the frames' appearance. Frame-type counts alone separate real from generated at AUC 0.999 on GenVidBench, and re-encoding every clip with one setting at a fixed length still leaves 0.61 to 0.63 on three benchmarks. We introduce a fixed frame-type pattern that removes this leak from every clip of at least 2 s. The same re-encode lowers the ReStraV detector on GenVidBench from 0.85 to 0.60, just above the 0.59 its features reach between two real collections, because its 8 fps repeats frames in real clips. Motion vectors alone separate real collections from one another at AUC 0.51 to 0.98, and only one benchmark ships the two labeled real sources needed to measure this separation within it. In deployment, with each threshold set to flag 5% of its training benchmark's real clips, the motion-vector and appearance probes and linear readouts on ReStraV and WaveRep features each flag 15% or more of another real collection. Choosing the threshold on real videos from the target collection brings the rate back to about 5%, at the cost of catching fewer generated clips. Clearing the real-vs-real level does not guarantee replication: of the six feature sets whose GenVidBench score clears it by 0.15 or more, only WaveRep's two keep their score on AIGVDBench. A benchmark score is therefore best read together with that level, a score on a second benchmark, and false-positive rates on other real collections. The toolkit and the clip lists of every evaluation set are atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Bricker to BRACE: A Bracket Exposure RAW Dataset and Restoration Model for Flicker-Banding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29845)**  `arXiv:2606.29845`  
  _Zihan Zhou, Libo Zhu, Jue Gong, Zhiyi Zhou, Jiezhang Cao, Yong Guo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flicker-Banding (FB) arises from temporal aliasing between a camera's rolling shutter and a display's brightness modulation, degrading screen-captured image readability with color shifts and jagged patterns. Existing single-frame methods with simplified parametric stripe models cannot reliably distinguish these artifacts from genuine texture. To address this, we conduct a systematic analysis of complex FB morphologies and reveal their significant variation across exposure settings, motivating a multi-frame bracketed RAW restoration paradigm. We construct Bricker, a synthetic-real bracketed RAW dataset built via ray-tracing-based physical simulation and an automated multi-exposure capture tool. We further propose BRACE: Bracketed RAW Flicker-Banding Removal, a multi-frame restoration model that utilizes a frequency-aware banding prior and a multi-scale spatial cross-attention modulator (MSCAM) for cross-exposure spatial fusion. We also introduce the Stripe Frequency Consistency (SFC) metric to evaluate banding removal. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on both synthetic and real benchmarks. Our dataset and code are available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[VCS-SLAM: Visibility-Consistent Semantic Fusion for 3D Gaussian SLAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29494)**  `arXiv:2606.29494`  
  _Raman Jha, Shuaihang Yuan, Yi Fang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Semantic 3D Gaussian SLAM commonly fuses 2D semantic priors into a persistent 3D map using uniform optimization weights. However, such priors are not equally reliable in online mapping: occlusions, unsupported semantic boundaries, and ambiguous ray geometry introduce persistent semantic artifacts into the global Gaussian map. We propose VCS-SLAM, a visibility-consistent semantic fusion framework for RGB-D 3D Gaussian SLAM. Instead of treating all semantic priors as uniformly valid signals, VCS-SLAM evaluates their geometric reliability through visibility consistency, surface-supported boundary evidence, and ray-level conflict uncertainty. The resulting reliability-aware objective suppresses occluded semantic updates, reduces unsupported semantic bleeding, and delays premature label assignment in ambiguous regions. Experiments on Replica show improved semantic consistency, boundary preservation, and reconstruction quality, while ScanNet results confirm competitive tracking under real RGB-D input. Under structured corruption of the 2D priors, VCS-SLAM degrades at little more than half the rate of a comparable semantic Gaussian SLAM baseline and outperforms naive gating, smoothness, and entropy-weighting variants of its own modules, indicating that the gains follow from the specific formulation rather than from reweighting alone. Code is available at \textit{this https URL}
  </details>

- **[DomainShuttle: Freeform Open Domain Subject-driven Text-to-video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26058)**  `arXiv:2606.26058`  
  _Nan Chen, Yiyang Cai, Weinan Jia, Zhuowei Chen, Wen Zhou, Wenhan Luo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open domain subject-driven text-to-video (S2V) generation has drawn significant interest in academia and industry. Open domain S2V mainly involves two scenarios: in-domain, which requires retaining the reference subject features as much as possible, and cross-domain, which preserves the intrinsic features of the subject while allowing subject-irrelevant properties to vary flexibly according to the text prompt. Existing methods primarily focus on maximizing subject fidelity in in-domain scenarios, which limits their editability and adaptability in cross-domain scenarios, such as novel styles, semantic combinations, or domain attributes. In this study, we propose that an ideal S2V method should flexibly shuttle between different domains, achieving strong performance in both in-domain and cross-domain scenarios. To this end, we propose DomainShuttle, which could achieve high fidelity and generative flexibility for open domain video personalization. Specifically, we introduce Domain-MoT, which decouples videos and reference features and introduces the domain-aware AdaLN for domain-specific modeling of reference images. We then introduce the Video-Reference DualRoPE scheme, which places reference image tokens and video tokens in separate RoPE spaces to enable precise subject-level spatial modeling, and Cross-Pair Consistent Loss, which aims to extract intrinsic subject features unaffected by irrelevant features. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DomainShuttle achieves significant performance improvements over existing methods, exhibiting high subject fidelity and generative flexibility across diverse open domain application scenarios.
  </details>

- **[3D-PLOT-LLM: Part-Level Object Tokens for 3D Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19828)**  `arXiv:2606.19828`  
  _Jintang Xue, Xinyu Wang, Yixing Wu, Jingwen Chen, C.-C. Jay Kuo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D multimodal large language models (3D MLLMs) describe a 3D object as a whole but cannot address, name, or reason about its parts. Prior part-aware attempts add segmentation decoders, heavier 3D encoders, or bounding-box grammars at substantial parameter cost. We take a fundamentally different path: we reorganize the input token stream so that parts become directly addressable through the LLM's own vocabulary. Our model, 3D-PLOT-LLM, partitions the frozen point encoder's patches into K locally coherent regions and inserts, before each region's patch tokens, a learnable per-region marker and a reserved vocabulary token <part_k>; a Marker-Space Refinement (MSR) module then conditions each marker on its region's spatial statistics and adjacency neighbors. The model thus cites parts in its output and follows prompts that refer to parts by token, a capability absent from prior object-level 3D MLLMs. To probe this interface, we construct PartVerse-QA, a vocabulary-level part-QA benchmark adapted from PartVerse mesh annotations (77K training pairs and 588 held-out queries on disjoint object splits), on which 3D-PLOT-LLM reaches caption-to-slots Jaccard 0.459 and Exact-match 13.78%, with a slot-to-caption GPT-4o judge of 44.68. On the text-description track of the 3DCoMPaT-GrIn part-aware grounded description benchmark, 3D-PLOT-LLM outperforms PointLLM, Kestrel, PARIS3D, and SegPoint on every reported text metric, and ShapeLLM on 3 of 4, with up to +3.03 GPT-4o judge over PointLLM. On Objaverse whole-object captioning, adding PartVerse-QA at Stage 2 yields +0.65 SBERT and +1.85 GPT-4o over PointLLM, and tops PointLLM-PiSA on 4 of 5 traditional metrics despite its part-grounded objective. All with under 1M new trainable parameters on a frozen point encoder, an order of magnitude below prior part-aware 3D MLLMs, and no learned part detector, segmentation decoder, or bounding-box head.
  </details>

- **[POTATR: A Lightweight Image-to-Graph Model for Page-Level Table Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09788)**  `arXiv:2606.09788`  
  _Brandon Smock, Libin Liang, Maxim Sokolov, Amrit Ramesh, Valerie Faucon-Morin, Tayyibah Khanam, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale document processing requires contextually aware table extraction (TE) that is both accurate and efficient. Yet current approaches require billions of parameters, hundreds of autoregressive steps, or costly API inference. Motivated by this, we introduce the Page-Object Table Transformer (POTATR), a lightweight 29M parameter image-to-graph model that extends the Table Transformer (TATR) for contextualized page-level TE. POTATR outperforms all models tested on the PubTables-v2 Single Pages benchmark -- including frontier MLLMs -- achieving $\textrm{GriTS}_\textrm{Con}$ of 0.964 while running over 130$\times$ faster at 300$\times$ lower cost. Further, POTATR's output is spatially grounded: every recognized element has a bounding box, enabling visual verification and geometric text assignment. As a result, POTATR performs unified page-level TE while composing with other models, enabling extension to scanned documents via external OCR and to full-document TE via techniques like cross-page merging. Code and models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[αDepth: Learning Scene-Level Soft Boundary Decomposition for Stereo Conversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00386)**  `arXiv:2606.00386`  
  _Xiang Zhang, Yang Zhang, Lukas Mehl, Karlis Martins Briedis, Markus Gross, Christopher Schroers_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurately modeling soft boundaries, e.g., hair and defocus blur, is a fundamental challenge in stereo conversion due to the ambiguous blending of foreground and background. Existing depth models primarily predict single-layer depth, leading to ambiguity in depth correspondence at soft boundaries. While matting techniques can capture opacity for layered modeling, they often struggle in complex scenes with multiple targets and usually require user intervention. This paper introduces {\alpha}Depth, a layered representation that decomposes soft boundaries for high-fidelity stereo conversion. Specifically, we first resolve mixed color and depth ambiguity by estimating layered color and depth values at soft boundaries. Considering complex multi-target scenes, we design a Circular Alpha Representation (CAR) that shifts the paradigm from global target extraction to local boundary decomposition. Unlike prior matting methods restricted to a single foreground/background, CAR enables efficient scene-level inference without manual guidance. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that {\alpha}Depth achieves state-of-the-art performance in stereo conversion, eliminating background bleeding and structural distortions at soft boundaries.
  </details>

- **[MONET: A Massive, Open, Non-redundant and Enriched Text-to-image dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21272)**  `arXiv:2605.21272`  
  _Benjamin Aubin, Gonzalo Iñaki Quintana, Onur Tasar, Sanjeev Sreetharan, Urszula Czerwinska, Damien Henry, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training large text-to-image models requires high-quality, curated datasets with diverse content and detailed captions. Yet the cost and complexity of collecting, filtering, deduplicating, and re-captioning such corpora at scale hinder open and reproducible research in the field. We introduce MONET, an open Apache 2.0 dataset of 103.8M image-text pairs collected from 2.9B raw pairs across heterogeneous open sources through successive stages of safety filtering, domain-based filtering, exact and near-duplicate removal, and re-captioning with multiple vision-language models covering short to long-form descriptions, and further augmented with synthetically generated samples. Each image is shipped with pre-computed embeddings and annotations to accelerate downstream use. To validate the effectiveness of MONET, we train a 4B-parameter latent diffusion model exclusively on it and reach competitive GenEval and DPG scores, demonstrating that our dataset lowers the barrier to large-scale, reproducible text-to-image research.
  </details>

- **[Distill to Think, Foresee to Act: Cognitive-Physical Reinforcement Learning for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21139)**  `arXiv:2605.21139`  
  _Yang Wu, Qiang Meng, Zhaojiang Liu, Youquan Liu, Jian Yang, Jin Xie_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current end-to-end autonomous driving models are fundamentally constrained by the behavioral cloning ceiling of imitation learning. While reinforcement learning offers a path to smarter autonomy, it demands two missing pieces of infrastructure: (1) a cognitive foundation that understands traffic semantics and driving intent, and (2) a foresighted physical environment that can anticipate the consequences of candidate actions. To this end, we propose CoPhy, a CognitivePhysical reinforcement learning framework for autonomous driving. To distill to think, we distill VLM knowledge into the BEV encoder and then discard the VLM entirely, retaining cognitive ability at zero inference cost while releasing the cognitive channel as a pluggable interface for optional human language commands. To foresee to act, we build an auto-regressive BEV world model that explicitly predicts future semantic maps conditioned on candidate actions, serving as an interpretable physical sandbox from which safety metrics are directly derived. Built upon this dual infrastructure, we optimize the driving policy via GRPO with a novel dual-reward mechanism: a physical reward derived from BEV rollouts enforces hard safety constraints, while a cognitive reward from a language-aligned scorer ensures intent compliance. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CoPhy not only achieves state-of-the-art results on NAVSIM v1 and v2 benchmarks, but also enables safer driving via cognitively informed scene compliance and flexible intent control through user-defined language instructions. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[ParaVT: Taming the Tool Prior Paradox for Parallel Tool Use in Agentic Video Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20342)**  `arXiv:2605.20342`  
  _Zuhao Yang, Kaichen Zhang, Sudong Wang, Keming Wu, Zhongyu Yang, Bo Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training large multimodal models (LMMs) via reinforcement learning (RL) to natively invoke video-processing tools (e.g., cropping) has become a promising route to long-video understanding. However, most existing agentic Video-LMMs dispatch tool calls sequentially (i.e., one per turn). Sequential tool calling can propagate localization errors, accumulate irrelevant context, and require an additional main-model generation for each successive crop. We introduce ParaVT, a multi-agent RL framework for Parallel Video Tool calling, dispatching multiple time-window crops in a single turn, allowing evidence from multiple temporal windows to be examined concurrently. Yet applying standard Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to ParaVT reveals an obstacle we term the Tool Prior Paradox: inherited tool-use priors appear to support exploration while competing with the output format learned during supervised fine-tuning. Standard RL can also settle on tool skipping. A comparison with a model with weaker tool-use priors shows stable formatting but no tool calls during RL, consistent with a trade-off between format stability and tool exploration. We propose PARA-GRPO (Parseability-Anchored and Ratio-gAted GRPO), which augments standard RL with two complementary mechanisms: (i) a format reward that encourages tag closure and penalizes unclosed reasoning blocks, and (ii) a per-prompt randomization of the overview-frame budget to create a reward advantage for tool use when the overview provides insufficient evidence. Across six long-video understanding benchmarks, ParaVT improves over the Qwen3-VL baseline by +7.9% on average, with PARA-GRPO raising training-time format reward from 0.13 to a peak of 0.64. These findings highlight the need to account for inherited tool-use priors when designing RL methods for long-video understanding.
  </details>

- **[Accelerating Rectified Flow Models via Trajectory-Aware Caching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16789)**  `arXiv:2605.16789`  
  _Xiao Liu, Kai Liu, Naiyang Guan, Hongliang Lu, Zhixin Wang, Zhikai Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion and rectified flow (RF) models generate high-fidelity images and videos, but their iterative velocity-field evaluations are computationally expensive. Existing caching methods accelerate sampling by skipping timesteps, yet their coarse approximations introduce accumulated errors over long skip intervals and degrade quality under aggressive acceleration. We propose TACache (Trajectory-Aware Cache), a training-free acceleration framework following a skip-then-compensate paradigm. TACache performs an orthogonal decomposition of discrete velocity acceleration along the RF trajectory into a parallel component and an orthogonal residual, isolating the magnitude and directional sources of per-step approximation error. The framework operates in two stages: offline, cumulative variation thresholds on the magnitude and direction indicators yield the skip schedule and bound how far each skip interval may extend; online, at each skipped step the offline statistics are combined with the sample's historical orthogonal direction to reconstruct the skipped velocity without additional model evaluations. Main comparisons on BAGEL, FLUX.1-dev, and Wan2.1-1.3B show that TACache achieves up to $4.11\times$ speedup on text-to-image generation and $2.11\times$ speedup on text-to-video generation, with consistent improvements over prior cache-based methods on most quality metrics. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When Policy Entropy Constraint Fails: Preserving Diversity in Flow-based RLHF via Perceptual Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12112)**  `arXiv:2605.12112`  
  _Xiaofeng Tan, Jun Liu, Bin-Bin Gao, Yuanting Fan, Xi Jiang, Chengjie Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RLHF is widely used to align flow-matching text-to-image models with human preferences, but often leads to severe diversity collapse after fine-tuning. In RL, diversity is often assumed to correlate with policy entropy, motivating entropy regularization. However, we show this intuition breaks in flow models: policy entropy remains constant, even while perceptual diversity collapses. We explain this mismatch both theoretically and empirically: the constant entropy arises from the fixed, pre-defined noise schedule, while the diversity collapse is driven by the mode-seeking nature of policy gradients. As a result, policy entropy fails to prevent the model from converging to a narrow high-reward region in the perceptual space. To this end, we introduce perceptual entropy that captures diversity in a perceptual space and maintains the property of standard entropy. Building upon this insight, we propose two entropy-regularized strategies, Perceptual Entropy Constraint and Perceptual Constraints on Generation Space, to preserve perceptual diversity and improve the quality. Experiments across two base models, neural and rule-based rewards, and three perceptual spaces demonstrate consistent gains in the quality-diversity trade-off; PEC achieves the best overall score of 0.734 (vs. baseline's 0.366); a complementary setting of PEC further reaches a diversity average of 0.989 (vs. baseline's 0.047). Our project page (this https URL) is publicly available.
  </details>

- **[GibbsLens: Training-Free Camera Control via Block-Conditional Gibbs Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11871)**  `arXiv:2605.11871`  
  _Yuzhu Wang, Xi Ye, Duo Su, Yangyang Xu, Jun Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training-free camera control for pretrained flow-matching video generators is a partial-observation inverse problem: a depth-warped guidance video supplies noisy evidence on a subset of latent sites, which the sampler must reconcile with the pretrained prior. Existing methods struggle to balance the trade-off between trajectory adherence and visual quality and the heuristic guidance-strength tuning lacks robustness. We propose GibbsLens, which resolves this dilemma through a structural change to the sampler: each outer hard-replacement guidance step is augmented with an inner-loop block-conditional pseudo-Gibbs refinement on the unobserved complement at the same noise level, with provable convergence to the partial-observation conditional data law. To accelerate convergence on high-dimensional video latents, we exploit their conditional locality, partitioning the unobserved complement into 3D patches, each tracked by a custom mixing indicator that adaptively freezes converged patches. On RealEstate10K and DAVIS, GibbsLens attains the best FVD against all seven training-free and training-based competitors, outperforming every training-free baseline on every reported metric. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[EPIC: Efficient Predicate-Guided Inference-Time Control for Compositional Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11722)**  `arXiv:2605.11722`  
  _Sunung Mun, Sunghyun Cho, Jungseul Ok_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent text-to-image (T2I) generators can synthesize realistic images, but still struggle with compositional prompts involving multiple objects, counts, attributes, and relations. We introduce EPIC (Efficient Predicate-Guided Inference-Time Control), a training-free inference-time refinement framework for compositional T2I generation. EPIC casts refinement as predicate-guided search: it parses the original prompt once into a visual program of object variables and typed predicates, then verifies each candidate image against this program using visual evidence extracted from that image. A candidate is judged to satisfy the prompt when all predicates pass; otherwise, unmet predicates guide the next step, routing localized failures to targeted editing and layout-level or persistent failures to resampling. On GenEval2, EPIC improves the prompt-level Soft-TIFA GM score from 34.16% for single-pass generation to 71.46%. With the same generator, editor, and image-model execution budget, EPIC outperforms the strongest baseline by 19.23 points. It also uses 31% fewer image-model executions, 72% fewer multimodal large language model (MLLM) calls, and 81% fewer MLLM tokens per prompt.
  </details>

- **[The Alpha Blending Hypothesis: Compositing Shortcut in Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10334)**  `arXiv:2605.10334`  
  _Andrii Yermakov, Jan Cech, Mario Fritz, Jiri Matas_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent deepfake detection methods demonstrate improved cross-dataset generalization, yet the underlying mechanisms remain underexplored. We introduce the Alpha Blending Hypothesis, positing that state-of-the-art frame-based detectors primarily function as alpha blending searchers; rather than learning semantic anomalies or specific generative neural fingerprints, they localize low-level compositing artifacts introduced during the integration of manipulated faces into target frames. We experimentally validate the hypothesis, demonstrating that deepfake detectors exhibit high sensitivity to the so-called self-blended images (SBI) and non-generative manipulations. We propose the method BlenD that leverages a large-scale, diverse dataset of real-only facial images augmented with SBI. This approach achieves the best average cross-dataset generalization on 15 compositional deepfake datasets released between 2019 and 2025 without utilizing explicitly generated deepfakes during training. Furthermore, we show that predictions from explicit blending searchers and models resilient to blending shortcuts are highly complementary, yielding a state-of-the-art AUROC of 94.0% in an ensemble configuration. The code with experiments and the trained model will be publicly released.
  </details>

- **[ReMoA: Recursive Modular Adaptation via Sparse Latent Refinement in Multimodal Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25299)**  `arXiv:2604.25299`  
  _Yuwei Sun, Yuxuan Yao, Hui Li, Weihao Yuan, Siyu Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion models have achieved success in high-fidelity data synthesis, yet their capacity for more complex, structured reasoning such as text following remains constrained. While advances in language models have leveraged strategies such as latent reasoning and recursion to enhance text understanding capabilities, extending these to multimodal text-to-image generation tasks is challenging due to the continuous and non-discrete nature of visual tokens. To tackle this problem, we draw inspiration from modular human cognition and propose a Recursive Modular Adaptation (ReMoA) framework integrated into joint attention layers of conventional diffusion models, iteratively refining visual tokens over multiple recursion steps while efficiently sharing parameters via sparse selection of neural modules.
