Comprehensive evaluations on both class-conditioned ImageNet generation and text-to-image generation using the GenEval and DPG benchmarks demonstrate that recursive visual latent refinement contributes to richer object details and better-aligned outputs. Additionally, visual latent trajectory and routing analyses reveal that neural modules learn to specialize across diffusion timesteps, with each module's trajectories diverging at later, low-noise steps to support context-adaptive refinement. ReMoA increases recall for DiT-XL from 0.57 to 0.62 on ImageNet, and improves the overall GenEval score for SD3-medium from 68.33% to 71.18%, without introducing additional parameters through recursion.
  </details>

- **[EXACT: an explainable anomaly-aware vision foundation model for analysis of 3D chest CT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24146)**  `arXiv:2604.24146`  
  _Xuguang Bai, Mingxuan Liu, Tongxi Song, Yifei Chen, Hongjia Yang, Kasidit Anmahapong, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chest computed tomography (CT) is central to the detection and management of thoracic disease, yet the growing scale and complexity of volumetric imaging increasingly exceed what can be addressed by scan-level prediction alone. Clinically useful AI for CT must not only recognize disease across the whole volume, but also localize abnormalities and provide interpretable visual evidence. Existing vision-language foundation models typically compress scans and reports into global image-text representations, limiting their ability to preserve spatial evidence and support clinically meaningful interpretation. Here we developed EXACT, an explainable anomaly-aware foundation model for three-dimensional chest CT that learns spatially resolved representations from paired clinical scans and radiology reports. EXACT was pre-trained on 25,692 CT-reports pairs using anatomy-aware weak supervision, jointly learning organ segmentation and multi-instance anomaly localization without manual voxel-level annotations. The resulting organ-specific anomaly-aware maps assign each voxel a disease-specific anomaly score confined to its corresponding anatomy, jointly encoding lesion extent and organ-level context. In retrospective multinational and multi-center evaluations, EXACT showed broad and consistent improvements across clinically relevant CT tasks, spanning multi-disease diagnosis, zero-shot anomaly localization, downstream adaptation, and visually grounded report generation, outperforming existing three-dimensional medical foundation models. By transforming routine clinical CT scans and free-text reports into explainable voxel-level representations, EXACT establishes a scalable paradigm for trustworthy volumetric medical AI.
  </details>

- **[Towards Visual Query Segmentation in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08898)**  `arXiv:2603.08898`  
  _Bing Fan, Minghao Li, Hanzhi Zhang, Shaohua Dong, Naga Prudhvi Mareedu, Xiaoqiong Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we introduce visual query segmentation (VQS), a new paradigm of visual query localization (VQL) that aims to segment all pixel-level occurrences of an object of interest in an untrimmed video, given a visual query. Compared with existing VQL locating only the last appearance of a target using bounding boxes, VQS enables more comprehensive (i.e., all object occurrences) and precise (i.e., pixel-level masks) localization, making it more practical for real-world scenarios. To foster research on this task, we introduce VQS-4K, a large-scale benchmark dedicated to VQS. Specifically, VQS-4K consists of 4,111 videos with more than 1.3 million frames, and covers a diverse set of 222 object categories. Each video is paired with a visual query defined by a frame outside search video and its target mask, and annotated with spatial-temporal masklets corresponding to the queried target. To ensure high quality, all videos in VQS-4K are manually labeled with meticulous inspection and iterative refinement. To our best knowledge, VQS-4K is the first benchmark specifically designed for VQS. Furthermore, to stimulate future research, we present a simple yet effective method, named VQ-SAM, which extends SAM 2 by leveraging target-specific and background distractor cues from the video to progressively evolve the memory through a novel multi-stage framework with an adaptive memory generation (AMG) module for VQS, significantly improving the performance. In our experiments on VQS-4K, VQ-SAM achieves promising results and surpasses existing methods, evidencing its effectiveness. With VQS-4K and VQ-SAM, we expect to inspire more future research and applications on VQS. Our benchmark and code are released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Faces in Things, Faces in Nothing: Probing Face Selectivity in Vision Models with Pareidolia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03989)**  `arXiv:2603.03989`  
  _Qianpu Chen, Derya Soydaner, Rob Saunders_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An electrical outlet can look like a face without depicting a person. Face pareidolia is thus a revealing probe of how vision models turn ambiguous visual evidence into category judgments. When a model calls such an object Human, does that answer depend on its face-like arrangement? Using the FacesInThings dataset, we test six vision models, from a vision-only ViT to CLIP and generative vision-language models, with face detectors as a reference. Each face-like region is paired with three controls from the same photograph that lack the arrangement: a background crop, the region with its tiles shuffled, and its average color. A Human answer driven by the arrangement should appear less often on these controls. We also vary how the categories are described. Three findings emerge. First, models respond to illusory faces, but not as people do: face detectors rarely return a face for these regions, yet CLIP ViT-L/14 and two generative models label roughly half of them Human when the categories are described as kinds of face. Annotators, by contrast, read these faces as non-human, such as animal or cartoon faces. For these models, a face is a human face by default. Second, similar Human rates hide opposite behavior. LLaVA-1.5 answers Human less often once the arrangement is removed; Qwen2-VL calls every average-color patch Human and describes a person in each; CLIP ViT-B/32 calls shuffled regions Human more often than real faces; SmolVLM rarely answers Human, but mostly for face-like regions. Third, rewording the categories changes whether Human answers favor the face at all, removing this preference in LLaVA-1.5 and creating it in CLIP. A Human answer is not evidence of a face: vision models can keep giving it after the face is gone.
  </details>

- **[Disentangle to create: Architecture-level disentangled diffusion model for few-shot Chinese font generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.18874)**  `arXiv:2602.18874`  
  _Jie Li, Suorong Yang, Jian Zhao, Furao Shen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-shot Chinese font generation aims to synthesize new characters in a target style using only a handful of reference images. Achieving accurate content rendering and faithful style transfer requires effective disentanglement between content and style. However, most existing approaches achieve only feature-level disentanglement and allow the generator to re-entangle these features, leading to content distortion and degraded style fidelity. To address this, we propose the Architecture-Level Disentangled Diffusion Model (ALD-Font) with an asymmetric design. The U-Net backbone takes SimSun-style glyphs as content templates, concatenated with noisy latents to construct a spatial canvas. Style features extracted from target-style images are injected as modulation signals to guide generation. Based on theoretical validation of disentanglement effectiveness, we adopt a parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) strategy that updates only the style-related modules. This allows the model to better adapt to new styles while avoiding overfitting to the reference images' content under the few-shot setting. Additionally, we train a Background Noise Removal (BNR) module in the pixel space to remove background noise in complex stroke regions. We further introduce the Grey and OCR metrics to evaluate the content quality of generated characters. Experimental results show that ALD-Font achieves significantly higher style fidelity while maintaining comparable content accuracy to existing state-of-the-art methods. The implementation is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ImagineAgent: What if Agents Could Imagine? Reinforcing Open-Vocabulary HOI Comprehension through Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11499)**  `arXiv:2602.11499`  
  _Zhenlong Yuan, Yue Wang, Dapeng Zhang, Kejin Cui, Rui Chen, Jing Tang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models have shown promising capabilities in bridging visual and textual reasoning, yet their reasoning capabilities in Open-Vocabulary Human-Object Interaction (OV-HOI) are limited by cross-modal hallucinations and limited viewpoints of images. To address this, we propose ImagineAgent, an agentic framework that integrates cognitive mapping, tool-augmented reinforcement learning (RL), and generative world modeling for robust OV-HOI understanding. Specifically, we first propose an innovative CoT dataset named hicodet-6K for supervised fine-tuning (SFT), which effectively bridges the perception-to-cognition gap by structuring perceived entities into interaction pairs for comprehensive predictions. Subsequently, we develop a multimodal tool library integrating online retrieval, image cropping, and generative modeling, enabling the agent to dynamically augment reasoning with domain-specific tools to resolve visual-semantic ambiguities and hallucinations during inference. Moreover, we incorporate a generative model to reconstruct alternative viewpoints, enabling the agent to 'imagine' under limited viewpoints. Finally, we propose a composite reward mechanism to jointly optimize prediction accuracy and tool efficiency. Evaluations on both SWIG-HOI and HICO-DET datasets demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance while requiring merely 36.7% of the training data compared to existing methods, validating our robustness, empirical effectiveness and efficiency.
  </details>

- **[PointRAFT: 3D deep learning for high-throughput prediction of potato tuber weight from partial point clouds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24193)**  `arXiv:2512.24193`  
  _Pieter M. Blok, Haozhou Wang, Hyun Kwon Suh, Peicheng Wang, James Burridge, Wei Guo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Key phenotypic traits, such as crop size, volume, and weight, can be estimated non-destructively from point clouds. In practice, however, point clouds of crops are typically incomplete due to self-occlusion, leading to systematic errors when estimating these traits. To overcome this limitation, crop traits can be inferred directly from incomplete point clouds using deep learning-based regression networks. These networks, however, may perform suboptimally, because their predictions do not incorporate prior geometric knowledge of the crop. Such geometric knowledge is often available in structured settings, such as on a potato harvester, where the fixed distance between the camera and the conveyor belt allows tuber height to be derived from the point cloud. To exploit this geometric cue, we introduce PointRAFT, a high-throughput point cloud regression network that incorporates object height through a dedicated height embedding. We hypothesized that this height embedding improves tuber weight estimation from partial point clouds. To test this hypothesis, we collected 26,688 point clouds of 859 potato tubers on an operational harvester, spanning four cultivars and three growing seasons. On a test set of 5,254 point clouds representing 172 tubers, PointRAFT achieved a mean absolute error (MAE) of 11.7 g and a root mean squared error (RMSE) of 17.1 g, outperforming six point cloud regression networks without a height embedding. Requiring only 6.3 ms per point cloud for preprocessing and inference, PointRAFT is fast enough for high-throughput weight estimation on a commercial potato harvester. Beyond potato weight estimation, PointRAFT provides a versatile regression network applicable to a wide range of 3D phenotyping and robotic perception tasks. The code, network weights, and a subset of the dataset are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PubTables-v2: A new large-scale dataset for full-page and multi-page table extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.10888)**  `arXiv:2512.10888`  
  _Brandon Smock, Maxim Sokolov, Libin Liang, Valerie Faucon-Morin, Amrit Ramesh, Tayyibah Khanam, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Table extraction (TE) is a key challenge in document understanding. Traditional approaches detect tables first, then recognize their structure. Recently, interest has surged in developing methods, such as vision-language models (VLMs), to extract tables directly in their full page or document context. However, a lack of annotated data has made progress difficult to demonstrate. To address this, we create a new large-scale dataset, PubTables-v2. PubTables-v2 unifies TE across various levels of surrounding context and, notably, is the first benchmark for multi-page TE. Our evaluations reveal that while current frontier models strongly outperform ($+0.354\ \textrm{GriTS}_\textrm{Con}$) small models on the most complex task (full-document multi-page TE), this gap can be closed or even reversed ($-0.056\ \textrm{GriTS}_\textrm{Con}$) on narrower tasks (cropped table extraction) with targeted training. Data is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ZeBROD: Zero-Retraining Based Recognition and Object Detection Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04888)**  `arXiv:2512.04888`  
  _Priyanto Hidayatullah, Nurjannah Syakrani, Yudi Widhiyasana, Muhammad Rizqi Sholahuddin, Refdinal Tubagus, Zahri Al Adzani Hidayat, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Object detection constitutes the primary task within the domain of computer vision. It is utilized in numerous domains. Nonetheless, object detection continues to encounter the issue of catastrophic forgetting. The model must be retrained whenever new products are introduced, utilizing not only the new products dataset but also the entirety of the previous dataset. The outcome is obvious: increasing model training expenses and significant time consumption. In numerous sectors, particularly retail checkout, the frequent introduction of new products presents a great challenge. This study introduces Zero-Retraining Based Recognition and Object Detection (ZeBROD), a methodology designed to address the issue of catastrophic forgetting by integrating YOLO11n for object localization with DeIT and Proxy Anchor Loss for feature extraction and metric learning. For classification, we utilize cosine similarity between the embedding features of the target product and those in the Qdrant vector database. In a case study conducted in a retail store with 140 products, the experimental results demonstrate that our proposed framework achieves encouraging accuracy, whether for detecting new or existing products. Furthermore, without retraining, the training duration difference is significant. We achieve almost 3 times the training time efficiency compared to classical object detection approaches. This efficiency escalates as additional new products are added to the product database. The average inference time is 580 ms per image containing multiple products, on an edge device, validating the proposed framework's feasibility for practical use.
  </details>

- **[AEGIS: Preserving Privacy of 3D Facial Avatars with Adversarial Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.17747)**  `arXiv:2511.17747`  
  _Dawid Wolkiewicz, Anastasiya Pechko, Przemysław Spurek, Piotr Syga_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The growing adoption of photorealistic 3D facial avatars, particularly those utilizing efficient 3D Gaussian Splatting representations, introduces new risks of online identity theft, especially in systems that rely on biometric authentication. While effective adversarial masking methods have been developed for 2D images, a significant gap remains in achieving robust, viewpoint-consistent identity protection for dynamic 3D avatars. To address this, we present AEGIS, the first privacy-preserving identity masking framework for 3D Gaussian avatars, concealing identity from automated face recognition while preserving the avatar's perceptual realism and animation usability. AEGIS applies adversarial perturbations to the base (DC) color coefficients of the Gaussian primitives, guided by an ensemble of pre-trained face verification networks, embedding the protection directly in the 3D representation so that it applies across viewpoints and animations, without retraining or modifying the avatar's geometry. Evaluated against black-box recognizers, AEGIS reduces face retrieval and verification accuracy to near-zero while maintaining high perceptual fidelity. It also preserves key facial attributes such as age, gender, and emotion, demonstrating strong privacy protection with minimal visual distortion. We further introduce a viewpoint-robust leakage metric that quantifies residual identity leakage across an avatar's rendered viewpoints, which single-view evaluation cannot capture.
  </details>

- **[Counterfactual World Models via Digital Twin-conditioned Video Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.17481)**  `arXiv:2511.17481`  
  _Yiqing Shen, Aiza Maksutova, Chenjia Li, Mathias Unberath_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models learn to predict the temporal evolution of visual observations given a control signal, potentially enabling agents to reason about environments through forward simulation. Because of the focus on forward simulation, current world models generate predictions based on factual observations. For many emerging applications, such as comprehensive evaluations of physical AI behavior under varying conditions, the ability of world models to answer counterfactual queries -- such as "what would happen if this object was removed?" -- is of increasing importance. We formalize counterfactual world models that additionally take interventions as explicit inputs, predicting temporal sequences under hypothetical modifications to observed scene properties. Traditional world models operate directly on entangled pixel-space representations where object properties and relationships cannot be selectively modified. This modeling choice prevents targeted interventions on specific scene properties. We introduce CWMDT, a framework to overcome those limitations, turning standard video diffusion models into effective counterfactual world models. First, CWMDT constructs digital twins of observed scenes to explicitly encode objects and their relationships, represented as structured text. Second, CWMDT applies large language models to reason over these representations and predict how a counterfactual intervention propagates through time to alter the observed scene. Third, CWMDT conditions a video diffusion model with the modified representation to generate counterfactual visual sequences. Evaluations on two benchmarks show that the CWMDT approach achieves state-of-the-art performance, suggesting that alternative representations of videos, such as the digital twins considered here, offer powerful control signals for video forward simulation-based world models.
  </details>

- **[EvoWorld: Evolving Panoramic World Generation with Explicit 3D Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.01183)**  `arXiv:2510.01183`  
  _Jiahao Wang, Luoxin Ye, TaiMing Lu, Junfei Xiao, Jiahan Zhang, Yuxiang Guo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans possess a remarkable ability to mentally explore and replay 3D environments they have previously experienced. Inspired by this mental process, we present EvoWorld: a world model that bridges panoramic video generation with evolving 3D memory to enable spatially consistent long-horizon exploration. Given a single panoramic image as input, EvoWorld first generates future video frames by leveraging a video generator with fine-grained view control, then evolves the scene's 3D reconstruction using a feedforward plug-and-play transformer, and finally synthesizes futures by conditioning on geometric reprojections from this evolving explicit 3D memory. Unlike prior state-of-the-arts that synthesize videos only, our key insight lies in exploiting this evolving 3D reconstruction as explicit spatial guidance for the video generation process, projecting the reconstructed geometry onto target viewpoints to provide rich spatial cues that significantly enhance both visual realism and geometric consistency. To evaluate long-range exploration capabilities, we introduce the first comprehensive benchmark spanning synthetic outdoor environments, Habitat indoor scenes, and challenging real-world scenarios, with particular emphasis on loop-closure detection and spatial coherence over extended trajectories. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our evolving 3D memory substantially improves visual fidelity and maintains spatial scene coherence compared to existing approaches, representing a significant advance toward long-horizon spatially consistent world modeling.
  </details>

- **[SSFMamba: Learning Symmetry-driven Spatial-Frequency Modeling for Physically Consistent 3D Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.03069)**  `arXiv:2508.03069`  
  _Bo Zhang, Yifan Zhang, Shuo Yan, Yu Bai, Zheng Zhang, Wu Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate 3D medical image segmentation requires a delicate balance between fine-grained local details and global contextual understanding. While spatial-domain models often struggle with long-range dependencies, existing frequency-based approaches frequently overlook intrinsic spectral properties such as Hermitian symmetry, leading to suboptimal feature integration. In this paper, we propose SSFMamba, a Mamba-based Symmetry-driven Spatial-Frequency fusion framework tailored for 3D medical imaging. Our architecture employs a complementary dual-branch design: the spatial branch models voxel sequences to preserve intricate anatomical textures, while the frequency branch operates on spectral components, each of which aggregates information from the entire volume, to capture global context. A core innovation is the 3D Multi-Directional Scanning Mechanism (MDSM), which integrates Hermitian symmetry with the causal nature of State Space Models (SSMs) to enable direction-aware global modeling. Crucially, by shifting the modeling focus to frequency-domain spectral components, SSFMamba captures the underlying structural characteristics of anatomical tissues. This leads to a highly adaptable framework that excels in both MRI and CT applications, regardless of the significant variations in intensity distributions. Extensive evaluations on the BraTS2020, BraTS2023, and BTCV datasets demonstrate that SSFMamba consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods. Notably, our approach achieves exceptional performance on low-contrast organs such as the pancreas (81.97% Dice), underscoring its potential as a unified and physically consistent perception framework for diverse 3D clinical applications.
  </details>

- **[SenseShift6D: Multimodal RGB-D Benchmarking for Robust 6D Pose Estimation across Environment and Sensor Variations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.05751)**  `arXiv:2507.05751`  
  _Yegyu Han, Taegyoon Yoon, Dayeon Woo, Sojeong Kim, Hyung-Sin Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances on 6D object pose estimation have achieved high performance on representative benchmarks such as LM-O, YCB-V, and T-Less. These datasets capture natural-occurring variations in illumination and camera setup but do not systematically explore the effects of controllable sensor configurations, such as exposure, gain, and depth-sensor modes. To bridge this gap, we introduce SenseShift6D, the first RGB-D object-pose benchmark that physically sweeps 13 RGB exposures, 9 RGB gains, auto-exposure, 4 depth-capture modes, and 5 illumination levels. For six common household objects, we acquire 198.8k RGB and 20.0k depth images (i.e., 795.4k RGB-D scenes), providing 1,380 unique sensor-lighting permutations per object pose. Experiments with state-of-the-art pretrained, generalizable pose estimators reveal substantial performance variation across lighting and sensor settings, despite their large-scale pretraining. Strikingly, even instance-level estimators-trained and tested on identical objects and backgrounds-exhibit pronounced sensitivity to environmental and sensor shifts. These findings establish sensor- and environment-aware robustness as an underexplored yet essential dimension of 6D pose estimation. To illustrate the opportunity enabled by this benchmark, we evaluate test-time sensor selection without retraining. An idealized controller yields remarkable gains of up to +16.2 pp for generalizable models, whereas a practical consistency-based proxy provides only limited improvements, highlighting substantial headroom and the need for future research on reliable sensor-aware adaptation. SenseShift6D is available atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[EgoM2P: Egocentric Multimodal Multitask Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07886)**  `arXiv:2506.07886`  
  _Gen Li, Yutong Chen, Yiqian Wu, Kaifeng Zhao, Marc Pollefeys, Siyu Tang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding multimodal signals in egocentric vision, such as RGB video, depth, camera poses, and gaze, is essential for applications in augmented reality, robotics, and human-computer interaction, enabling systems to better interpret the camera wearer's actions, intentions, and surrounding environment. However, building large-scale egocentric multimodal and multitask models presents unique challenges. Egocentric data are inherently heterogeneous, with large variations in modality coverage across devices and settings. Generating pseudo-labels for missing modalities, such as gaze or head-mounted camera trajectories, is often infeasible, making standard supervised learning approaches difficult to scale. Furthermore, dynamic camera motion and the complex temporal and spatial structure of first-person video pose additional challenges for the direct application of existing multimodal foundation models.To address these challenges, we introduce a set of efficient temporal tokenizers and propose EgoM2P, a masked modeling framework that learns from temporally-aware multimodal tokens to train a large, general-purpose model for egocentric 4D understanding. This unified design supports multitasking across diverse egocentric perception and synthesis tasks, including gaze prediction, egocentric camera tracking, and monocular depth estimation from egocentric video, and also serves as a generative model for conditional egocentric video synthesis. Across these tasks, EgoM2P matches or outperforms specialist models while being an order of magnitude faster. We will fully open-source EgoM2P to support the community and advance egocentric vision research. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[UINO-FSS: Unifying Representation Learning and Few-shot Segmentation via Hierarchical Distillation and Mamba-HyperCorrelation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.15669)**  `arXiv:2504.15669`  
  _Wei Zhuo, Zhiyue Tang, Wufeng Xue, Hao Ding, Junkai Ji, Linlin Shen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-shot semantic segmentation has attracted growing interest for its ability to generalize to novel object categories using only a few annotated samples. To address data scarcity, recent methods incorporate multiple foundation models to improve feature transferability and segmentation performance. However, they often rely on dual-branch architectures that combine pre-trained encoders to leverage complementary strengths, a design that limits flexibility and efficiency. This raises a fundamental question: can we build a unified model that integrates knowledge from different foundation architectures? Achieving this is, however, challenging due to the misalignment between class-agnostic segmentation capabilities and fine-grained discriminative representations. To this end, we present UINO-FSS, a novel framework built on the key observation that early-stage DINOv2 features exhibit distribution consistency with SAM's output embeddings. This consistency enables the integration of both models' knowledge into a single-encoder architecture via coarse-to-fine multimodal distillation. In particular, our segmenter consists of three core components: a bottleneck adapter for embedding alignment, a meta-visual prompt generator that leverages dense similarity volumes and semantic embeddings, and a mask decoder. Using hierarchical cross-model distillation, we effectively transfer SAM's knowledge into the segmenter, further enhanced by Mamba-based 4D correlation mining on support-query pairs. Extensive experiments on PASCAL-5$^i$ and COCO-20$^i$ show that UINO-FSS achieves new state-of-the-art results under the 1-shot setting, with mIoU of 80.6 (+3.8%) on PASCAL-5$^i$ and 64.5 (+4.1%) on COCO-20$^i$, demonstrating the effectiveness of our unified approach.
  </details>

- **[A Closer Look at Dynamic Scene Graph Generation In the Era of Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15846)**  `arXiv:2503.15846`  
  _Xuanming Cui, Jaiminkumar Ashokbhai Bhoi, Chionh Wei Peng, Adriel Kuek, Ser Nam Lim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic Scene Graph Generation (DSGG) aims to capture objects and their evolving relations in videos. Despite recent progress, the practicality and quality of generated scene graphs remain limited compared to the rapid advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). In this work, we revisit DSGG from two fundamental perspectives: task setup and model design. From the task setup perspective, we identify two key limitations of the current recall-oriented evaluation protocol: (i) a severe precision-recall trade-off, and (ii) uninformative and redundant relation generation. To better assess practical usefulness, we introduce five additional metrics that provide a more comprehensive evaluation of the quality of generated dynamic scene graphs. From the model design perspective, we explore directly using MLLMs for scene graph generation and establish a strong MLLM-based DSGG baseline through three design changes. First, we replace the conventional bottom-up pipeline with a top-down reason-then-locate strategy. Second, we reformulate frame-wise dynamic graphs as Temporal Relation Set (TRS) prediction, improving both efficiency and performance. Third, we introduce Importance-Aware Finetuning (IAF) to encourage more relevant and diverse relation generation. Extensive experiments on Action Genome, VidVRD, and PVSG show that our approach consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance.
  </details>

- **[EgoGen: An Egocentric Synthetic Data Generator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.08739)**  `arXiv:2401.08739`  
  _Gen Li, Kaifeng Zhao, Siwei Zhang, Xiaozhong Lyu, Mihai Dusmanu, Yan Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding the world in first-person view is fundamental in Augmented Reality (AR). This immersive perspective brings dramatic visual changes and unique challenges compared to third-person views. Synthetic data has empowered third-person-view vision models, but its application to embodied egocentric perception tasks remains largely unexplored. A critical challenge lies in simulating natural human movements and behaviors that effectively steer the embodied cameras to capture a faithful egocentric representation of the 3D world. To address this challenge, we introduce EgoGen, a new synthetic data generator that can produce accurate and rich ground-truth training data for egocentric perception tasks. At the heart of EgoGen is a novel human motion synthesis model that directly leverages egocentric visual inputs of a virtual human to sense the 3D environment. Combined with collision-avoiding motion primitives and a two-stage reinforcement learning approach, our motion synthesis model offers a closed-loop solution where the embodied perception and movement of the virtual human are seamlessly coupled. Compared to previous works, our model eliminates the need for a pre-defined global path, and is directly applicable to dynamic environments. Combined with our easy-to-use and scalable data generation pipeline, we demonstrate EgoGen's efficacy in three tasks: mapping and localization for head-mounted cameras, egocentric camera tracking, and human mesh recovery from egocentric views. EgoGen will be fully open-sourced, offering a practical solution for creating realistic egocentric training data and aiming to serve as a useful tool for egocentric computer vision research. Refer to our project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Exploring Sequence Feature Alignment for Domain Adaptive Detection Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2107.12636)**  `arXiv:2107.12636`  
  _Wen Wang, Yang Cao, Jing Zhang, Fengxiang He, Zheng-Jun Zha, Yonggang Wen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Detection transformers have recently shown promising object detection results and attracted increasing attention. However, how to develop effective domain adaptation techniques to improve its cross-domain performance remains unexplored and unclear. In this paper, we delve into this topic and empirically find that direct feature distribution alignment on the CNN backbone only brings limited improvements, as it does not guarantee domain-invariant sequence features in the transformer for prediction. To address this issue, we propose a novel Sequence Feature Alignment (SFA) method that is specially designed for the adaptation of detection transformers. Technically, SFA consists of a domain query-based feature alignment (DQFA) module and a token-wise feature alignment (TDA) module. In DQFA, a novel domain query is used to aggregate and align global context from the token sequence of both domains. DQFA reduces the domain discrepancy in global feature representations and object relations when deploying in the transformer encoder and decoder, respectively. Meanwhile, TDA aligns token features in the sequence from both domains, which reduces the domain gaps in local and instance-level feature representations in the transformer encoder and decoder, respectively. Besides, a novel bipartite matching consistency loss is proposed to enhance the feature discriminability for robust object detection. Experiments on three challenging benchmarks show that SFA outperforms state-of-the-art domain adaptive object detection methods. Code has been made available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Context--Answer Metamodels: Forecasting LLM Activations Before the First Answer Token](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06837)**  `arXiv:2611.06837`  
  _Tommy He, Thomas Jiralerspong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Under stochastic decoding, a single context can elicit many different answers from a language model. Characterizing this variation typically requires many expensive autoregressive rollouts, each revealing only one realization. We ask whether the activations produced while reading the context can directly forecast the model's rollout activations. We train context--answer metamodels that map a context activation sequence to point or distribution forecasts of mean-pooled answer activations. We find that (i) the four metamodel families we study forecast mean-pooled answer activations with high fidelity, and the distribution metamodels also capture rollout variability; (ii) performance improves as the training set grows to 500,000 prompts, and the metamodels generalize zero-shot to out-of-domain datasets; (iii) their forecasts retain behavior-predictive signal; and (iv) context activations from Qwen2.5 predict mean-pooled answer activations from Qwen3.5 and Gemma 4, suggesting that activation forecasts can transfer across models. Together, these results suggest that context activation traces can support scalable forecasts of behaviors in LLM answers before the first answer token is generated.
  </details>

- **[Systolic Linear Attention: Co-Designing Tile-Grouped Gated Delta Attention for TPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06828)**  `arXiv:2611.06828`  
  _Gagik Amirkhanyan, Nuojin Cheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gated-delta linear attention layers such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN), Kimi Delta Attention (KDA), and GDN-2 are tuned for GPUs, where small tensor-core tiles and fast vector registers make per-channel decay rescaling and sequential in-block solves cheap. On Google TPUs, which concentrate compute in wide $256 \times 256$ systolic matrix units backed by a slower vector unit, porting these layers directly leaves half of each matrix tile empty and bottlenecks on vector operations. We present \textbf{Systolic Linear Attention (SLA)}, which reshapes gated delta attention around the matrix unit by widening keys to $256$ with grouped value heads, grouping decay gates into four $64$-channel tiles, and computing the chunk solve entirely with block matrix products in a single fused kernel. On TPU v7x, a full \texttt{SLA-R4} layer runs $1.6\times$--$2.5\times$ faster than the open-source Tokamax KDA kernel ($1.4\times$--$2.4\times$ with exact chunk inversion), driven primarily by head sharing and kernel fusion. Across a $2{,}048$-chip TPU v7x pre-training study of sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models from $6.5\text{B}$ to $63.1\text{B}$ parameters, SLA trains $11\%$--$24\%$ faster than KDA at similar perplexity, and reaches slightly lower perplexity than KDA in smaller matched-seed runs.
  </details>

- **[Transforming Integer Grids to Spatial Intuition: Perception, Not Planning, Governs ARC-AGI-3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06793)**  `arXiv:2611.06793`  
  _Harsidak Singh Banwait_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sample-efficient fluid intelligence requires agents that rapidly form working models of unfamiliar interactive environments. ARC-AGI-3 tests this across uninstructed visual games scored by Relative Human Action Efficiency, which penalizes exploratory waste quadratically. When frontier models read raw integer grids, serialization destroys spatial topology, and they spend hundreds of actions rediscovering basic boundaries and symmetries. We introduce SARBLOH, an open-weight agent built on the premise that spatial perception, not planning capacity, governs interactive reasoning on ARC-AGI-3. A zero-parameter O(HW) perception engine turns each frame, in 35 ms, into an object inventory with bipartite temporal tracking, a geometric briefing that separates proven invariants from affordance hypotheses, and an annotated canvas. Paired with a code-as-action runtime, the agent writes algorithmic hypotheses instead of low-level moves. Across eight presentations, grounded perception raises the rubric score from 3/28 to 25/28 points while cutting token use by 89.4%. On 23 held-out games, geometric invariants generalize with 100% accuracy, while single-frame affordance guesses fall to 6-50%, so active verification probes remain necessary. On the public set, Run A (briefing off) solved 143 of 183 levels in 8.2 hours at 59.4% single-pass efficiency; Run B (briefing on) reached 63.5%. Within the nine-hour budget on one Kaggle RTX PRO 6000 GPU, SARBLOH scored 16.53 on the hidden Kaggle evaluation, 13.7x the first Milestone winner.
  </details>

- **[FedFiOT: Fisher-Weighted Optimal Transport for Model Merging in One-Shot and Few-Round Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06783)**  `arXiv:2611.06783`  
  _Quan Minh Nguyen, Hoang M. Ngo, Bhakti Chowkwale, Yasaman Keshtkarjahromi, My T. Thai_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  One-shot and few-round federated learning (FL) reduce communication burden by constructing a global model within one or a few communication rounds, but often achieve lower performance than conventional multi-round FL. Without frequent synchronization, client models may drift apart, causing neurons that learn similar features to occupy different positions across clients. At the same time, local data heterogeneity can cause corresponding parameters to differ in their importance to each client's predictions. Together, these challenges cause direct averaging to combine mismatched neurons and assign equal weight to parameters despite differences in their importance. We propose FedFiOT, a Fisher-weighted optimal transport framework for one-shot and few-round FL that leverages Fisher information to quantify parameter importance and guide neuron alignment and model aggregation. FedFiOT jointly optimizes neuron correspondences and global model parameters through alternating minimization of a shared Fisher-weighted objective. Theoretically, we show that FedFiOT's optimization procedure monotonically decreases the layer-wise merging objective and establish a stationarity bound that quantifies the effects of client heterogeneity and nonuniform Fisherthis http URLacross multiple datasets demonstrate consistent improvements over competing baselines in both one-shot and few-round federated learning settings.
  </details>

- **[Continual Classification Beyond the Kernel Regime: Harnessing the Sparsity Bias of L2 Regularization in Diagonal Linear Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06759)**  `arXiv:2611.06759`  
  _Gilad Karpel, Itay Evron, Ron Meir, Daniel Soudry_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study logistic regression for continual learning with linear predictors in a high-dimensional statistical setting. Existing theoretical work has largely focused on continual linear regression with variants of L2 regularization, motivated by their analytical simplicity and connection to the kernel regime of neural networks. We first derive the optimal L2 regularization strength for continual linear classification and characterize its generalization performance, recovering behavior analogous to prior results for continual linear regression. Next, we examine the effect of L2 regularization in deeper models using a minimal two-layer diagonal linear network. In the single-task setting, L2 regularization on the weights of such models is known to promote sparsity in the resulting predictor, yielding a rich, sparse feature-learning regime beyond the kernel regime (a similar effect arises in two-layer ReLU networks). However, in continual learning, where L2 regularization is typically centered on previous-task weights, this sparsity bias is no longer preserved. We therefore propose a weighted variant of L2 regularization that extends this beneficial sparsity bias to continual learning. We show theoretically and empirically that, for a sufficiently sparse teacher, our variant converges substantially faster than directly learning a linear predictor with conventional continual L2 or L1 regularization, trading some long-horizon bias for markedly improved performance over moderate task horizons.
  </details>

- **[Symmetry Breaking at the Edge of Stability via Central Flow Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06718)**  `arXiv:2611.06718`  
  _Naima Elosegui Borras, Georgios Arvanitidis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continuous symmetries in neural networks lead to conserved quantities that are invariant under gradient flow, such as layer balancedness in linear and ReLU networks. Finite-step gradient descent need not preserve these laws, particularly at the edge of stability (EOS), where the iterates oscillate around a smoother trajectory, which can be described by the central flow dynamics (Cohen et al., 2025). We study how gradient flow conservation laws evolve under the central flow description of this regime. We show that the curvature correction induces a finite-dimensional drift determined by the oscillation covariance along critical parameter directions. We thus take the first steps to analyse which conservation laws remain and are broken at the EOS according to the central flow dynamics by analysing a scalar two-layer model, two-layer matrix factorisation, and a two-layer ReLU network, where the known conservation laws are shown to be complete (Marcotte et al., 2023).
  </details>

- **[Where It Breaks: Diagnosing Perception, Memory, and Decision Failures of a Vision-Language Agent in Pac-Man](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06704)**  `arXiv:2611.06704`  
  _Xinglu Wang, Edward Wang, Yong Zhang, Zhenan Fan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training an agent to play Pac-Man with only up, down, left and right actions raises a basic question: when performance is poor, what should be improved? Difficulty can arise from reading the screen or from choosing actions over a long sequence. We examine these problems in a Qwen3.5-9B-based agent through separate perception checks and primitive-policy reinforcement-learning studies. In the policy study, which uses textual boards to remove image transcription from the decision interface, completion on eight held-out mazes increased from 0% before this optimization to 80.2% at a selected checkpoint, with three extra lives. The perception checks show that accurate static screenshot reading can coexist with errors during interaction. A separate policy comparison also shows that further optimization can accompany worse action choices. Together, these findings motivate concrete checks for post-training: evaluate perception in use, distinguish training success from full-game validation, and examine decisions and later performance rather than relying on one selected score. The study provides empirical lessons for improving future agent training and evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Don't Replace, Complement: A Detector-Agnostic Subspace Signal for Near-OOD Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06688)**  `arXiv:2611.06688`  
  _Wonseok Shin, Songkuk Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-hoc out-of-distribution (OOD) detection is mature on far-OOD yet remains weak on near-OOD. These two settings differ in their use of class-characteristic feature directions: far-OOD inputs largely fail to activate those of their predicted class, whereas near-OOD inputs activate them much as in-distribution samples do. Because the axis separating far-OOD leaves near-OOD almost indistinguishable from ID, a score optimized for one setting is misaligned with the other; where a detector already saturates far-OOD, added near-OOD sensitivity is bought partly against it. This divergence motivates a targeted near-OOD signal designed to complement existing scores. We propose PLUS (Per-class Low-dimensional sUbspace Signal). For each class, PLUS selects the $m$ most class-active neurons from a sparse autoencoder, orthonormalizes the corresponding decoder directions to form a low-dimensional subspace, and scores an input by its per-class whitened squared Mahalanobis distance $z$ within the subspace of its predicted class. Designed as a dedicated complement rather than a standalone detector, the signal $z$ is fused additively with any calibrated base score at a single, fixed weight. This weight is a single global constant: no value is adjusted per backbone, per OOD dataset, or per deployment, and all calibration statistics are estimated from ID validation data alone. When applied to 18 existing detectors spanning diverse method families, $z$ consistently improves near-OOD AUROC in all 126 detector--backbone combinations. Far-OOD performance is broadly maintained or improved, with only marginal declines where the base score is near-optimal, making $z$ a robust, detector-agnostic complement across backbones.
  </details>

- **[SecureGARD: Dynamic Ontology-Guided Governance for Secure Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06682)**  `arXiv:2611.06682`  
  _Guoqin Xiong, Shengtao Wen, Xiang Chen, Xinyi Zeng, Zhaoxin Fan, Wei Ye, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using large language model (LLM) agents translate model outputs into consequential actions, turning indirect prompt injection into an execution-security problem. Existing defenses constrain tool calls, trajectories, or information flows, but often leave action semantics, state dependencies, and evidence relations implicit, limiting generalization across heterogeneous tools and complicating safe updates. We present \ours{}, a dynamic ontology-guided governance framework. \ours{} grounds heterogeneous calls in a shared semantic representation and combines an executable ontology with episode-level task state to derive deterministic ready, waiting, and forbidden decisions. A waiting decision identifies an unmet but recoverable obligation, enabling bounded replanning without expanding the agent's authorization boundary. \ours{} evolves its ontology offline from verified training executions. An offline LLM proposes instance-independent action--state axioms, which are accepted only if they address ontology errors exposed by training failures, preserve logical consistency and protected entailments, and pass held-out validation before the ontology is frozen. We evaluate \ours{} on AgentDojo, ASB, and AgentDyn under benign and indirect prompt-injection settings. \ours{} achieves a 0\% attack success rate (ASR) across all three benchmarks while improving task utility under attack compared with existing baselines. These results show that explicit semantic governance can strengthen execution security without indiscriminate blocking.
  </details>

- **[Do unlearning algorithms learn the right features?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06681)**  `arXiv:2611.06681`  
  _Polina Dolgova, Amartya Sanyal, Sebastian U. Stich_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine unlearning is commonly evaluated with standard post-deletion metrics that compare an unlearned model to one retrained on the remaining data. Such snapshot evaluations can conceal consequential differences in the features the two models encode. We prove that if an unlearned and a retrained model agree on their outputs but encode different features, a single gradient step on their prediction heads can break this agreement. We further show that deleting an arbitrarily small fraction of the data can cause retraining to select qualitatively different features, so matching retraining can require learning features that the full model never had. We then introduce a measure of feature recovery that separates missing from extraneous features. Across eight unlearning methods, models that perform well on standard metrics often fail to recover the retrained features. This mismatch predicts deviations from retraining under subsequent relearning and downstream adaptation, and directly correcting it systematically reduces these deviations.
  </details>

- **[Self-Directed Apple Tasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06677)**  `arXiv:2611.06677`  
  _Tanmay Devale, Reed Phillips, Arvind Ramaswami_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study the classical apple-tasting setting, a partial feedback model in which the learner observes the true label only when it predicts $1$. A combinatorial characterization for the rates of this problem in the online adversarial setting has been studied by Raman et al. (2024). We study the self-directed setting, in which the learner can adaptively choose the order of the points it predicts on. In the realizable setting, we establish a tetrachotomy on the expected minimax number of mistakes made by any learner. Depending on the VC dimension, the $\emptyset$-centered star number, and the self-directed dimension, we show that the rates could possibly be $\Theta(1)$, $O(\log n) \cap \omega(1)$, $\tilde \Theta(\sqrt{n})$, or $\Theta(n)$. In the agnostic setting, the minimax regret is $\tilde \Theta(\sqrt{n})$ or $\Theta(n)$, depending on the VC dimension.
  </details>

- **[Fingerprinting the Dispatchers behind Distributed Model Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06661)**  `arXiv:2611.06661`  
  _Snigdha Chandan Khilar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  API distillation campaigns try to hide model-stealing traffic by splitting requests across thousands of load-balanced accounts, which standard content classifiers often miss. However, request timestamps, specifically arrival time and stream completion time, reveal the underlying dispatching strategy, such as round-robin counters or key-shuffling pools. Using a 20 percent seed set, this timing analysis can forensically rank the remaining accounts of a campaign with 0.92 to 1.00 AUC within 20 minutes, even across thousands of real production accounts. While adaptive attackers struggle to hide rotating dispatchers using jitter, pinning keys successfully evades detection, though it can severely throttle throughput when quotas bind. Ultimately, timing exposes shared infrastructure patterns even when content filters fail.
  </details>

- **[From Reconstruction to Imputation: Test-Time Optimization for Missing Time-Series Channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06612)**  `arXiv:2611.06612`  
  _Björn Ludwig, Alexander Windmann, René Heesch, Oliver Niggemann_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Complete sensor-channel failures create a difficult form of time-series missingness: local interpolation is unavailable, and deploying a separately trained imputer for every failure pattern may be impractical. We ask whether a sequence reconstructor trained without artificial mask augmentation and then frozen can be converted into an imputer at inference time. Our method treats the missing entries of each test window as optimization variables, clamps all observations, and backpropagates through the frozen model to minimize reconstruction error only on observed coordinates. The resulting observed-self-consistency objective requires neither artificial mask augmentation nor full-channel dropout during reconstructor training. We characterize when this refinement is informative through the cross-Jacobian from missing inputs to observed residuals, and use an optional temporal-smoothness prior when that coupling is weak. Across five real-world datasets with exactly one channel synthetically masked per test window and four reconstruction architectures, no-prior test-time refinement reduces MAE relative to one-pass reconstruction in 19 of 20 dataset-backbone combinations. Compared with mask-aware imputers trained under either standard structured masks or matched full-channel dropout, the best refined reconstructor achieves the lowest MAE on two datasets and trails the strongest baseline on three. These gains require substantially more inference time and are sensitive to cross-channel coupling. Our results establish test-time self-consistency as a conditional inference-time adapter for frozen reconstruction models that complements mask-aware imputation without retraining the reconstructor.
  </details>

- **[Verifier-Guided Self-Consistency: Test-Time Reasoning with Imperfect Generators and Verifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06606)**  `arXiv:2611.06606`  
  _Ekaterina Fadeeva, Xueyan Li, Mrinmaya Sachan, Maxim Panov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models can solve many multi-step reasoning problems, but their reasoning remains brittle: plausible intermediate steps often lead to incorrect answers. Improving reasoning at test time requires balancing two imperfect signals: the generator, which provides diversity, and the verifier, which guides correctness. Existing approaches largely favor one side of this tradeoff. Verifier-weighted voting relies on trajectories sampled from the generator and cannot steer generation toward correct ones. By contrast, beam-style verifier-guided search can steer generation, but may over-concentrate on narrow trajectory sets and amplify verifier errors.We propose Verifier-Guided Self-Consistency (VGSC), a stochastic decoding framework that combines step-level correctness and future-outcome estimates to guide reasoning during generation. VGSC repeatedly proposes candidate next steps from the base language model and uses both signals in an acceptance/resampling procedure, while maintaining multiple trajectories for final answer-level self-consistency. We provide a probabilistic interpretation of VGSC as approximate sampling from a verifier-tilted trajectory distribution. Across multiple reasoning benchmarks, generator models, and verifier families, VGSC achieves higher average accuracy than representative baselines.
  </details>

- **[The Behavioral Rate of Fine-Tuning: Compression as a Measure of Useful Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06598)**  `arXiv:2611.06598`  
  _Joey David, Paul Caillon, Alexandre Allauzen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) adapts a frozen language model through a compact learned update, making it a natural setting for asking how much information fine-tuning needs to write. Yet adapter rank, parameter count, or numerical reconstruction error do not directly tell us how many bits are required to preserve the acquired behavior. We therefore define an operational behavioral rate $R^\star$ as the smallest serialized size of a compressed adapter that, once reloaded into the frozen base model, retains a fixed fraction of its held-out fine-tuning gain. Across LoRA fine-tuning experiments, this rate increases with distinct training content at fixed optimization budgets, but is not determined by intuitive metrics like source description length, fine-tuning gain, or weight-space reconstruction error. Most importantly, the same datasets exhibit substantially different, and even reordered, rates across frozen base models, showing that fine-tuning compressibility is a property of the dataset--receiver pair rather than of the dataset alone. However, we show that correction spectra measured on the data are able to rank these rates within a receiver. Finally, we study a striking noise-reducing effect of compression: adapters trained on deliberately corrupted datasets fall far below the frozen model, yet after compression score above it (23.7% to 82.9% on GSM8K, against 77.6% for frozen Qwen2.5-7B), consistently across tested families and sizes. The damage is unevenly concentrated in the adapter's leading singular directions, which compression appears to filter. Away from its training task, the compressed corrupted adapter also generalizes better than a clean fine-tune: on harder math it retains 97% of base accuracy, versus 44% for the clean adapter.
  </details>

- **[From Fixed Defaults to Calibrated Activation Policies for Long-Term Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06559)**  `arXiv:2611.06559`  
  _Shijie Xu, Boyan Tang, Jianghua Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term time-series forecasting supports decisions in energy, transportation, and other applications where reliable predictions are needed beyond the observed data. Although forecasting research has advanced model architectures, fixed activation configurations provide limited guidance on how activation choices should vary across datasets and prediction horizons. This study aims to establish when task-specific activation choices improve forecasting accuracy and how previous experiments can guide the selection of suitable configurations for new tasks. We develop a calibrated selection policy that learns a reliable default from prior experiments, adapts the activation function and its location to each task, and retains the default when evidence for a change is insufficient. In an evaluation supported by 1,225 Informer training runs across 17 dataset variants and 51 forecasting tasks, the policy reduces mean squared error by 7.86% on average relative to the conventional fixed-activation baseline and improves 72.55% of tasks. These findings establish activation choice and placement as practical opportunities for improving existing forecasting models and show how accumulated experimental evidence can guide configuration decisions without repeating an exhaustive search for every task.
  </details>

- **[EvoOPD: Evolving Privileged Information Strategies for Black-Box On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06542)**  `arXiv:2611.06542`  
  _Ximo Zhu, Ruiqi Liu, Yi Han, Zhengan Wu, Gengsheng Li, ZiAn Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proprietary language models exhibit strong reasoning and coding capabilities, but their output distributions are inaccessible, making fine-grained distillation methods that rely on teacher logits difficult to apply directly to black-box models. We observe that rich textual feedback from a black-box teacher can be treated as privileged information (PI) and converted, through a PI-conditioned privileged teacher, into dense supervision signals on the student's own trajectories. However, what PI should be acquired from the teacher to most effectively support student learning remains unclear. PI strategies that differ in where they focus, what information they request, and how much detail they provide can have substantially different effects on the current student, and their relative effectiveness can further change as training progresses. Based on this observation, we propose Evolving Privileged Information Strategies for Black-Box On-Policy Distillation (EvoOPD), which treats PI acquisition itself as an optimization problem and maintains a PI Strategy Bank that evolves with the student state. EvoOPD uses the measured repair utility of PI on the current student to adapt the focus, information type, and granularity of teacher feedback, allowing the acquired information to continually match the student's learning needs. We systematically evaluate EvoOPD on mathematical reasoning and code generation with Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B students. EvoOPD achieves the best overall performance in all six model-task settings, improving over the strongest baseline in each setting by an average of 2.14 percentage points in mathematics and 1.30 points in code. Further analysis shows that the relative effectiveness of PI acquisition strategies changes with student state, making a fixed strategy difficult to sustain as the best choice throughout distillation.
  </details>

- **[IDSISA: Influence-Distilled Sharded Training for Efficient Machine Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06519)**  `arXiv:2611.06519`  
  _Deeraj SK, Sadhana Devarajan, Tanmoy Hazra, Anupam Shukla_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine unlearning removes the influence of specific training examples to comply with privacy regulations. Full retraining gives strong guarantees but is computationally prohibitive at scale; approximate methods are efficient but lack formal guarantees. SISA (Sharded, Isolated, Sliced, and Aggregated training) achieves certified deletion through data sharding and independent retraining, but random partitioning scatters forget requests across most shards, and isolated retraining degrades utility. We introduce IDSISA (Influence-Distilled SISA), which uses influence functions to concentrate forget-prone samples into few shards and distillation from unaffected shards to recover utility lost to isolation. The deletion guarantee inherited from SISA's retraining step remains exact and requires no probabilistic qualification; the distillation-based utility recovery is a separately quantified, empirically bounded approximation ($\epsilon_{\text{cert}} \leq 0.015$ in expectation). We further formalize certification under sequences of repeated deletion requests, where cross-shard distillation can create dependencies between shards; we prove that a closure-based cascading retraining procedure restores the deletion guarantee to the full aggregated model under any deletion sequence. Experiments on ResNet-18/CIFAR-10, ResNet-18/CIFAR-100, BiLSTM/IMDB, TCN/IMDB, and a fully connected network on Fashion-MNIST show $14-91\%$ better selective forgetting, up to $50\%$ better utility retention in relative terms (with consistent percentage-point gains on the other architectures, where a relative baseline is undefined), and unlearning-time changes ranging from $-6.7\%$ to $+6.4\%$ depending on architecture, relative to SISA.
  </details>

- **[Nowcasting Future Performance for Population Pruning in Survival-of-the-Fittest Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06512)**  `arXiv:2611.06512`  
  _Rico Städeli, Léo Meynent, Boris Knyazev, Damian Borth_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training high-performing neural networks often involves evaluating populations of candidate models with varying hyperparameters, architectures, or random seeds. Training every candidate to completion is computationally expensive, yet stopping seemingly weaker runs early risks discarding models that would ultimately perform better, as learning curves can cross. To address this challenge, we investigate whether intermediate training checkpoints can predict future model accuracy and enable more informed pruning decisions. Our nowcasters predict test accuracy five epochs ahead or at the end of training. They are trained with accuracy and loss histories, weight statistics, and SANE representations as inputs. SANE is a transformer autoencoder that encodes a checkpoint as a sequence of latent tokens. We then use the nowcasters in Survival-of-the-Fittest (SOTF) training. SOTF repeatedly ranks runs during training by predicted accuracy and stops the lowest-ranked ones. Our results show that weight statistics work best for five-epoch forecasts, and SANE representations work best for final accuracy prediction. For evaluation, we compare SOTF with pruning the population by current accuracy. In homogeneous model zoos such as the CNN zoos, pruning by current accuracy already retains a final model only slightly worse than the best. Model zoos with a larger spread in performance, such as the ResNet-18 and ViT zoos, are more difficult to prune by current accuracy alone. In these zoos, SOTF with nowcasted final accuracy almost closes the gap to the best model.
  </details>

- **[Model Merging Does Not Require Uniform LoRA Ranks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06486)**  `arXiv:2611.06486`  
  _Zirong Xie, Zainab Afolabi, Nikita Kozodoi, Jack Butler_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model merging combines separately fine-tuned experts into a single model without retraining. Standard practice trains every expert at the same LoRA rank, which couples the rank choice across experts even though each domain has its own best rank. We show this coupling is a property of the merge method, not of merging. We train 36 experts on three domains (mathematics, instruction following, multilingual) at four ranks and three model scales, then merge with six methods at uniform ranks and at each domain's own best rank. Giving experts different ranks costs averaging and utility-based selection nothing, and gains them up to 1.8 pp of multi-domain accuracy, while the conflict-resolution methods are inconsistent and can lose up to 4 pp. The split has a simple cause. Merging keeps only a fixed share of the pooled spectral components, and methods that choose them by size discard many that would have reduced loss. They also tend to discard the same expert: the instruction adapter has the smallest singular values, so it is dropped almost entirely and IFEval falls by 16 to 25 pp. Choosing components by their effect on loss instead keeps all three experts, and leaves accuracy nearly flat across an 8$\times$ rank range (0.7 pp, against 6.7 pp for TIES at 4B scale). Rank can therefore be chosen per expert whenever the merge operator does not select by magnitude.
  </details>

- **[The Alignment Tax Is Graded: Task-Selective Capability Loss Under Length-Normalized DPO with SFT Retention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06476)**  `arXiv:2611.06476`  
  _Ye Yuan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Setting. This is an empirical characterization study; we propose no new method and run no comparisons against alternative objectives. We fine-tune Qwen3.5-4B with sparse LoRA and length-normalized DPO plus an SFT retention term (the RPO objective) on structured financial-filing tasks, and track downstream capability as the implicit reward margin grows to $\sim 7$ $\beta$-scaled units (nf4 training; $\sim 3$ under fp16 measurement on the unquantized base). Question. Does margin growth degrade capability uniformly across tasks, or selectively? Findings. In a pre-registered study (8 seeds, 6 unseen at freeze) with artifact-aware scoring and TOST equivalence testing, the alignment tax is graded and task-selective: extraction is preserved ($\Delta = -0.55$ pts, equivalent at $\pm5$ pts), json loses little ($\Delta = -3.52$ pts, equivalence fails on a clustered item-level interval), and router degrades severely ($\Delta = -30$ pts). Router's loss concentrates in a margin 1-3 damage window containing a transient format-break spike. The margin is accounted for almost entirely ($\geq 96\%$) by suppression of rejected completions, with chosen reward approximately flat, consistent with the retention term anchoring it; we do not attribute the behavioral damage to any single gradient channel. Gold-logp deflation is task-selective (extraction $-0.15$, json $-1.04$, router $-1.47$ nats/token under fp16), with gold selectively protected relative to incorrect controls on extraction (8/8 seeds). QLoRA reward margins differ up to $3.4\times$ between nf4 training and unquantized fp16 measurement, with an axis-dependent gap. Scope. Length-normalized rewards with SFT retention, sparse LoRA on 8/32 layers, one 4B model, easily-separable synthetic preferences, three structured tasks. A no-retention control and a measured format-alignment metric are left to future work.
  </details>

- **[Graph Foundation Models Can Become Effective Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06441)**  `arXiv:2611.06441`  
  _Zhuangyan Hu, Zhuoxuan Liang, Wei Li, Xiaohua Jia, Moustafa Youssef, Sumi Helal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multivariate time series anomaly detection (MTSAD) relies on modeling both temporal dynamics and inter-sensor dependencies to identify complex system faults. Although graph-based detectors explicitly model sensor topologies, they train task-specific networks from scratch on limited target-domain data, remaining vulnerable to spurious correlations and unable to reuse transferable priors. While pretrained Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) offer transferable topological priors and anomaly discriminability, directly adapting them to MTSAD is hindered by cross-modal feature-topology mismatch, unreliable pseudo-anomaly supervision, and decision distortion during node-to-window aggregation. To fill these gaps, we propose a novel adaptation framework, Gfm4Mtsad, which unleashes pretrained GFMs for MTSAD. We introduce the Structure-and-Information Contract Alignment (SICA) module to bridge continuous time series and the pretrained GFM space. This module harmonizes principal subspace token projection, normal-prior-guided graph construction, and residual recovery to suppress spurious edges while retaining fine-grained temporal details. We propose a Mechanism-Aligned Pseudo-Anomaly Screening strategy (MAPS) to steer unsupervised learning. It applies localized dependency breaks while preserving empirical residuals, selecting hard negatives near the normal boundary for targeted supervision. We develop a Tail-Gated Evidence Calibration mechanism (TGEC) to eliminate node-to-window aggregation distortion. It calibrates multi-granular graph responses against normal operational references, strictly preserving benign score rankings while selectively refining extreme anomaly candidates in the tail distribution for reliable window detection and interpretable sensor evidence. Extensive evaluations with two GFM backbones demonstrate that Gfm4Mtsad achieves superior detection performance over existing baselines.
  </details>

- **[Partially Interpretable Decision Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06428)**  `arXiv:2611.06428`  
  _Ludovic Schwartz, Federico Berton, Tim van Erven_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Partially interpretable models extend simple interpretable models with the option to defer complicated inputs to an accurate but uninterpretable black-box model. By deferring more inputs, the model gets more accurate; by deferring fewer, it gets more interpretable. Existing procedures to train such partially interpretable models build on linear models or decision rules. We propose three algorithms for decision trees: a partially interpretable extension of the existing Branches algorithm, which performs exact search to optimize the tree; a conversion algorithm that we run on top of standard CART, which is computationally more efficient; and a generalized CART whose splitting criterion takes into account the option to defer while growing the tree. Perhaps surprisingly, the performance of the three algorithms is similar across many realworld datasets. We can explain this through a formal bound on the potential gain of smart tree construction, provided the black box accuracy is sufficiently homogeneous across the domain. We also construct a synthetic dataset where the accuracy of the black box is deliberately inhomogeneous. There, our deferral-aware generalization of CART does outperform regular CART and matches the performance of exact search. Finally, we evaluate an application to concept bottleneck models. Partially interpretable decision trees can replace uninterpretable neural networks after the bottleneck. On undeferred inputs, the model is fully interpretable, and deferring preserves accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Zero-Shot Coordination among LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06409)**  `arXiv:2611.06409`  
  _Adrian Hayler, Shashank Reddy Chirra, Andrei Lupu, Johannes Forkel, Bidipta Sarkar, Siheng Feng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study zero-shot coordination (ZSC), where independently developed agents must coordinate at test time. While ZSC has been well studied in the RL literature, far less is known about the performance of LLM agents despite their increasing deployment in such settings. Existing work on LLM coordination often relies on specialised scaffolding that independently developed agents are unlikely to share in practice. Moreover, evaluations on complex environments (e.g., Hanabi) make it difficult to pinpoint the sources of coordination failure. In contrast, we focus on simple, general-purpose scaffolds in minimal environments designed to isolate specific coordination challenges. Our results show that even in these controlled settings, frontier LLM agents struggle to coordinate, largely due to limited understanding of the coordination problem and weak reasoning about their partner's beliefs. While LLMs introduce semantic information as an additional axis for coordination, they nonetheless fail to exploit this structure effectively. Towards this, we propose Coordination-friendly definitions (CFDs) as a principled approach for enabling robust coordination among LLM agents. Finally, we show that CFDs can be discovered automatically, removing the need for manual engineering. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[KolTa: A Physics-Informed Active Learning Framework for Formulation Discovery with Limited Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06394)**  `arXiv:2611.06394`  
  _Boyong Tang, Tao Wang, Lei Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Finding high-performing formulations is difficult when few have been measured and experimental budgets are limited. We propose KolTa, an active learning framework that selects batches to discover the top 5% of formulations at a target temperature under a fixed query budget. KolTa combines a three-stage predictor of component contributions, role-conditioned interactions, and anchored temperature response with task-specific soft physics constraints. Its Physics-Residual-Calibrated Upper Confidence Bound (PRC-UCB) acquisition rule combines predicted utility, ensemble disagreement, and a target-temperature physics residual, with nonnegative exploration weights fitted to out-of-fold signed errors. We evaluate the framework on three measured tasks and one simulated task covering binary-mixture viscosity, drug solubility, ionic-liquid viscosity, and electrolyte conductivity. KolTa ranks first or second in mean area under the learning curve (AULC) on every task and outperforms seven of the eight comparator strategies in paired tests, winning 75 of 78 paired comparisons across tasks and seeds against those seven strategies. Across the three measured tasks, its largest gap to the best mean AULC is 0.032, compared with at least 0.056 for every comparator. On Drug, reaching 80% average recovery of the remaining targets across drugs requires an estimated median of 348 additional formulation queries, about 18% fewer than the fastest comparator. Researchers can adapt the framework to diverse formulation problems through component roles and physical constraints.
  </details>

- **[Give Up to Get More: Priors, Not Policies, Govern Test-Time Compute Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06372)**  `arXiv:2611.06372`  
  _Jinhyung Bae_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When many inputs share a budget of repeated attempts by a large language model (LLM), a common rule gives harder inputs more. On public math, code and formal-proof samples, the best published difficulty-proportional rule captures half or more of the oracle gain on benchmarks the model mostly solves, but at most 13% on hard workloads, even given true pass rates. Because the value of one more attempt peaks at intermediate difficulty, the optimal static allocation is inverted-U and gives little to inputs that are hopeless at the current budget. In a strict survey of 221 LLM allocation papers, at least 13 give the hardest inputs the most attempts; their easier settings sit at budgets where this is near-optimal, so the cost appears on harder workloads. Acting on it requires knowing which inputs are solvable, so information, not the policy, drives the gain. With a verifier and a prior from a related model's past runs on the same inputs, Thompson Sampling raises the solved share on the hardest workload from 14% to 27%; without such a prior, no policy we tested beats round-robin. For the policies we tested, the gain vanishes between prior rank correlations of about 0.3 and 0.55, which cheap text-based priors barely reach on the weakest models, and it requires a verifier whose false acceptances are rare relative to the pass rate. On new Qwen2.5 samples the pattern holds, and tempering the prior avoids losses on easy workloads. We turn these results into a decision guide.
  </details>

- **[EEGDash Corpus: A Training-Ready Public Corpus for EEG Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06356)**  `arXiv:2611.06356`  
  _Kuber Shahi, Bruno Aristimunha, Arnaud Delorme_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Foundation models for electroencephalography (EEG) are pretrained on tens of thousands of hours of recordings, and their accuracy grows with that amount. Such data is hard to assemble. The largest sources are clinical archives that cannot be redistributed, and the public sources are spread over several archives, file formats, and licences. We release eegdash-corpus-v0, a training-ready corpus of 77 public EEG datasets, as the gated Hugging Face dataset EEGDash/eegdash-corpus-v0 with the SHA-256 of every file.
