REVE, the largest published EEG foundation model, names 89 datasets as its pretraining corpus. Twelve cannot be redistributed: four clinical archives that hold 82% of its hours, and eight under no-derivatives licences. The corpus holds the 76 others that REVE's sampling rate allows and one more from REVE's release card: 77 datasets, 14,075 hours from 7,272 subjects, 5.0 million 10-second samples and 6.21 billion tokens per epoch. Every dataset is fetched through EEGDash, converted once with REVE's recipe, and stored in its own buckets of a chunked Zarr store with its licence recorded beside it, so a model can train on the whole store or on any subset of datasets. To test that the store pretrains a model, we retrain REVE-Base from scratch twice with the authors' code, changed only in how it reads data, and score it on seven public OpenEEGBench tasks. It reaches 0.331 under linear probing and 0.273 after fine-tuning, against 0.333 and 0.325 for the released model and 0.200 and 0.205 for an untrained encoder. The converter, the conversion reports, and the loader changes are released with the corpus.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Tune with Biased Gradients: Iterative Feedback Tuning of Model-Free Control on Throttle Valves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06338)**  `arXiv:2611.06338`  
  _Félix Martinez-Jiménez, Sander Miller-Murphy, Tobie Ondoa-Essomba, Emmanuel Witrant_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Iterative feedback tuning (IFT) learns the parameters of a fixed-structure controller by stochastic gradient descent. At each iteration, two closed-loop experiments are run on the plant: the first one measures the tracking error, and the second one, driven by this error, provides the sensitivities from which the gradient of the cost is estimated. This estimate is unbiased for linear plants only. We study the biased case on automotive electronic throttle valves, whose friction and stiction vary between valves and runs, controlled by an intelligent proportional (model-free) controller, which is an incremental PI controller. We show that the mismatch of the controller state at the start of the two experiments, which stiction makes unavoidable, adds an error inversely proportional to the amplitude of the second experiment. On digital twins of ten valves, the IFT gradient is a descent direction in only 69\% of the low-cost region, friction being the cause, and all update rules plateau and then drift. On two hardware valves, the best update rule depends on the noise structure of the gradient. On the first valve, where one gradient component is buried in noise, Adam outperforms Gauss--Newton, its normalisation and its momentum each improving one parameter. On the second valve, where both components are consistent, the same normalisation amplifies a direction that Gauss--Newton rightly discounts, and Adam drifts to a controller worse than the initial one. The twins reproduce the mechanism but not this ranking.
  </details>

- **[Conformal Prediction for Distribution-to-Distribution Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06320)**  `arXiv:2611.06320`  
  _Trung-Khang Tran, Tuan A. Hoang, Viet-Hoang Tran, Tan M. Nguyen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conformal Prediction (CP) provides a model-agnostic framework for constructing finite-sample prediction sets with coverage guarantees and has become a tool for reliable decision-making in high-risk settings. Recent advances have extended Split Conformal Prediction to increasingly complex learning problems, including multilabel, high-dimensional, and functional outputs. In this work, we study CP for Distribution-to-Distribution Regression, a setting of growing interest across many application domains. We show that a naive application of Split CP yields prediction sets that, while marginally valid, are non-interpretable, e.g., lacking a well-defined volume or a tractable sampling procedure, and of limited practical value. To overcome this, we propose a new conformal framework that operates in an orthogonal basis space, producing interpretable prediction sets while preserving coverage guarantees. We further develop an adaptive variant with asymptotic conditional coverage under mild assumptions. Empirical results on synthetic and real-world datasets validate the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Split Protocol Decides the Result: Re-Evaluating Encrypted Traffic Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06290)**  `arXiv:2611.06290`  
  _Mayan Sharma, Kriti Saini, Devansh Behl_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Encrypted traffic classifiers are routinely reported at 95-99% accuracy. We show that on one of two public corpora, the same model and code score macro-F1 0.992 under the evaluation protocol standard in the literature and 0.567 under a protocol that keeps flows from one packet capture on one side of the split. Nothing changes but the partition (gap 0.426 over eight seeds, p = 0.008 after Holm-Bonferroni correction).The inflation is not a property of random splitting as such. On a month of backbone QUIC traffic, we evaluate five split protocols at eight seeds each: random, temporal and client-disjoint splitting are statistically indistinguishable from session-disjoint, while server-disjoint splitting costs 0.192 macro-F1 (d = 17.0, p = 0.008). Which axis matters depends on how the corpus was collected, in a way invisible from its label space.We give a diagnostic that identifies the right axis before any model is trained: single-identifier probes. A decision tree on the capture identifier alone reaches 0.983 under a random split and exactly 0.000 under session-disjoint splitting.Under this evaluation, a metric-trained flow embedding leads four deep baselines but does not beat gradient-boosted trees on twenty packet sizes. Five of our own pre-registered architectural claims fail: distance-based rejection of unknown applications beats softmax thresholding, but ablating every metric objective makes rejection better, because class separation correlates at r = -0.88 with rejection quality. Few-shot enrollment is flat within a corpus but, across corpora, five labelled flows per class recover 91% of the deficit left by failed zero-shot transfer.All splits, results, code and negative findings are released.
  </details>

- **[Learning Pseudo-Riemannian Manifolds for Heterophilic Graphs via Graph Signature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06265)**  `arXiv:2611.06265`  
  _Yun Young Choi, Asung Kil, Sun Woo Park, Minho Lee, Seokhwan Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Several widely used families of Graph Neural Networks carry a positive-definite geometric prior. Graph convolution read as heat diffusion needs a positive semi-definite operator, hyperbolic models place features on positive-definite Riemannian manifolds, and dot-product and GAT-style attention scores use positive-definite inner products. On heterophilic graphs, where edges often signify dissimilarity or structural repulsion, that prior is a mismatch: no positive-definite form can assign a negative squared distance to a direction the labels separate. To measure the mismatch, we introduce the Graph Signature Index ($s_{\mathcal{G}}$), the inertia of a class-compatibility matrix built from label topology alone, and we prove that no value of the edge or node homophily index in $(0,1)$ rules out a negative eigenvalue. We then propose the Pseudo-Riemannian Attention Network (PRAT), whose attention logit is a signed squared pseudo-distance under a learnable diagonal metric initialized from $s_{\mathcal{G}}$. The design is diagnose-then-learn: the signature supplies a sign structure that unconstrained training keeps outside near-zero directions, and the projection learns the rest of the metric around it. Across fifteen node-classification benchmarks PRAT outperforms or matches the strongest heterophily-adapted models. A controlled ablation ties that performance to the sign structure itself: with architecture and protocol fixed, forbidding the metric to be indefinite costs accuracy on the graphs $s_{\mathcal{G}}$ marks as indefinite and leaves the others unchanged.
  </details>

- **[Learning Noise Optimization for Reward Alignment in Few-Step Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06255)**  `arXiv:2611.06255`  
  _Hongeun Kim, Jeongsol Kim, Jong Chul Ye_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward alignment for diffusion- and flow-based generative models is often formulated over stochastic trajectories, making it less suitable for few-step deterministic generators. Initial noise optimization provides a trajectory-free alternative, but existing gradient-free methods require costly per-sample search at inference. We introduce LeNO (Learning Noise Optimization), which amortizes reward-guided initial noise optimization into a learned residual transport while keeping the pretrained generator frozen. LeNO learns an anchor-conditioned distribution over noise corrections using Local GRPO and scalar rewards, eliminating iterative test-time optimization. For Gaussian initial noise with norm-preserving projection, we derive a tractable upper bound on the KL divergence between the corrected noise distribution and the original prior, providing a principled regularization objective. We evaluate LeNO on few-step text-to-image and protein structure generation, including expensive black-box designability rewards. Across both domains, LeNO improves target rewards with a single noise correction at inference while largely preserving generation quality and diversity.
  </details>

- **[Rubric Curriculum RL: Exploiting the Generation-Verification Gap in Non-Verifiable Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06244)**  `arXiv:2611.06244`  
  _Sumeet Ramesh Motwani, Tejas Krishnan, Alistair Letcher, Charles London, Suhaas Bhat, Huitian Jiao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has led to significant gains in math and code generation. Extending these gains to open-ended domains remains challenging: ground-truth verification is unavailable, human annotation is expensive, and learned reward models are prone to reward hacking. The generation-verification gap, where judging good outputs is easier than producing them, offers a potential path to improvement. However, rubric-based judges are often imprecise and stationary, so policies can overfit or reward hack them. We introduce Rubric Curriculum RL (RcRL), which exploits this gap without new data, human annotations, or stronger teacher models. We combine pairwise preferences over rollouts with a curriculum over rubric criteria, yielding a more discriminative signal than absolute scoring and a non-stationary objective. Whereas baselines plateau within a few hundred steps, RcRL preserves output entropy and keeps improving past 1000 steps. On creative writing, RcRL achieves a 70.5% human win rate against the base model, with gains across benchmarks and judges. On HealthBench, RcRL improves over the base model by 14.6% (full set) and 25.4% (consensus subset), outperforming all training baselines, including instance-specific rubrics.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Asset Knowledge Transfer for Early Fault Detection of Data-Scarce Assets Without Target Fault Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06194)**  `arXiv:2611.06194`  
  _Zekai Wang, Yuxuan Li, Zhangyue Shi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliability management of industrial fleets depends on early fault warnings, yet lifecycle data across similar assets are highly imbalanced. A few assets accumulate run-to-failure histories, while newly installed or rarely failing assets provide only normal-condition data. This paper asks how diagnostic knowledge can be transferred from a data-rich source asset to a data-scarce target asset when no fault data have been observed on the target. We formulate cross-asset early fault detection as a zero-shot domain adaptation problem, in which zero-shot refers to the absence of target fault samples, and propose a framework that requires only normal data from the target. The framework comprises (1) a statistical process control step that labels the source asset run-to-failure data with an exponentially weighted moving average chart, reducing manual annotation to verifying flagged segments, (2) an Adapted Domain-Adversarial Neural Network (ADANN) that combines domain-adversarial training and a Maximum Mean Discrepancy penalty with an Adapted Classification-Domain (ACD) loss, which anchors the classifier normal region to the target own normal data, and (3) an ahead-of-time labeling scheme with a prediction horizon that targets incipient faults. Under an exact-classification assumption, a theoretical analysis characterizes the domain-adversarial term for a normal-only target and the alignment state in which source fault features remain separated from target normal features. On a public run-to-failure bearing dataset, ADANN attains the highest mean F1-score among four transfer baselines, with a nominally significant precision gain over the strongest baseline, and its robustness is examined under synthetic noise.
  </details>

- **[FaCoDiff: Factor-Correlated Diffusion for Multivariate Probabilistic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06166)**  `arXiv:2611.06166`  
  _Jiaxuan Li, Yao Liu, Yuewei Xue, Shijie Dong, Zixiang Jin, Xiaoyu Guo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multivariate probabilistic forecasting requires modeling cross-variable dependence alongside marginal uncertainty. The challenge is to learn this dependence while controlling marginal noise scales and avoiding dense covariance costs. We propose Factor-Correlated Diffusion (FaCoDiff), which couples a compact conditional statistical estimator with structured denoising. History-conditioned factor loadings use distinct coefficients for forecast-center correction, scale adjustment and noise dependence. Diagonal compensation preserves the estimated marginal noise variances, while low-rank operators enable residual Gaussian likelihood evaluation and noise transport without dense covariance storage or factorization. Factor-derived feature modulation explicitly conditions the denoiser, and a geometry-aware objective accounts for errors after noise transport. The same conditional geometry governs forward perturbations and reverse sampling. Across nine datasets, FaCoDiff achieves the lowest mean variogram score on eight datasets and ranks among the top two on 43 of 54 dataset-metric combinations in the reported comparisons. Matched ablations on Solar and Weather show that factor covariance improves mean Energy and Variogram scores under all four combinations of center and scale correction. Disrupting cross-variable sample pairings while preserving marginal trajectories worsens joint and aggregate scores on both datasets, demonstrating the predictive value of generated cross-variable dependence.
  </details>

- **[Selective Prediction Intervals: Jointly Learning to Predict, Bound, and Abstain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06158)**  `arXiv:2611.06158`  
  _Lidor Rubi, Gilad Katz_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many high-stakes regression decisions ask three things of a model at once: a point estimate to act on, an interval that bounds the risk of acting, and the option to abstain when neither can be trusted. Deep learning provides these in isolation: prediction-interval methods return an interval, and at best a value estimate inside it, for every input, while selective-prediction methods learn when to abstain, but only for point predictions and with no statement of how far the truth may lie from the estimate. We introduce SelPI, which trains interval, value and abstention heads jointly under a single loss with an explicit acceptance-rate budget. A natural objection is that interval width already tells us when to abstain. We show that it does not: across 18 tabular benchmarks, width predicts large point errors well (AUROC 0.72) but is anti-correlated with interval misses (AUROC 0.38), so abstaining on wide intervals discards precisely the inputs the interval got right. SelPI therefore selects with two gates, one trained on the interval loss and one on the value loss, coupled by a Fr'echet penalty that ties their separate budgets to the joint acceptance rate they deliver. With the base predictor held fixed, the learned gate beats width thresholding on a proper interval score on all 18 tabular datasets, closing a median 87% of the gap to an oracle selector, and it also beats selection by deep-ensemble disagreement on every dataset. The advantage grows with the heteroscedasticity of the task, and we report the settings in which it disappears.
  </details>

- **[Neural Stochastic Forecasting for Lateral Molecular Diffusion Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06157)**  `arXiv:2611.06157`  
  _Jacob Ede Levine, Mario Hidalgo-Soria, Chen Yun Wen, Yun Lyna Luo, Sai Chandra Kosaraju_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting the long-time diffusion of membrane proteins is challenging because molecular dynamics (MD) simulations are computationally expensive, while experimental single-particle tracking is often limited by finite observation times. Here, we introduce a neural stochastic (NS) model for forecasting the collective diffusion of membrane proteins from coarse-grained MD trajectories. The model combines a transformer-based representation of protein configurations, recurrent networks operating at multiple temporal scales, and a stochastic latent process with learned memory. A cross-attention decoder then generates correlated protein displacements while accounting for the instantaneous system geometry and periodic boundary conditions. We evaluate the model in two membrane systems: seven interacting PIEZO channels and a mixed system containing seven PIEZO and ten TREK channels. For the seven PIEZO system, the NS model reproduces the anomalous diffusion exponent measured in MD simulations $\alpha \sim 1$, substantially improving on a previously developed variational autoregressive model that exhibits superdiffusive behavior. The model also generates trajectories extending to 40,000 frames and captures the distinct diffusion characteristics of PIEZO and TREK populations in the mixed system. Comparisons of time-averaged mean-square displacements, Van Hove displacement distributions, and non-Gaussian parameters show that the model preserves key statistical features of the simulated dynamics. These results establish neural stochastic modeling as a promising approach for generating long trajectories of multi-protein membrane diffusion and extending the statistical analysis of molecular dynamics simulations.
  </details>

- **[Short-Term Forecasting of Heterogeneous Wind Power Fleets: Global Temporal Fusion Transformer Leveraging Static Covariates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06130)**  `arXiv:2611.06130`  
  _Viktor Walter, Andreas Wagner_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate short-term wind power forecasting is critical for grid stability and electricity trading. While transformer-based deep learning is state of the art, industry practice relies mostly on local models trained for individual assets. This study investigates whether a global temporal fusion transformer (TFT), trained jointly on a heterogeneous portfolio of wind farms across Germany, outperforms site-specific local models in 48-hour-ahead forecasting. Using numerical weather prediction (NWP) data from the regional ICON-D2 and the global ECMWF HRES models, we compare the global TFT against local models and a linear regression baseline. The global model leverages static covariates, such as farm age, altitude above sea level, hub height, rotor diameter and power curve parameters, to learn shared representations across sites. We evaluate on 100 synthetic wind farm profiles and a real portfolio of 11 German wind farms, across two seasonal test periods with intermediate retraining. The global TFT outperforms the local models for 87% of the synthetic and 77% of the real wind farms and clearly surpasses the linear baseline. Wilcoxon signed-rank tests confirm that these gains are significant across both seasons and datasets. Crucially, this superiority depends on static covariates: without asset-specific context, the global model fails to generalize. The TFT's variable selection network (VSN) reveals the learned feature selection. Altitude emerges as a relevant static discriminator on both datasets, while hub height is decisive on the real portfolio. For dynamic inputs, the encoder prioritizes historical power, whereas the decoder combines upper-level wind speeds from both NWP models, consistent with averaging out model-specific errors. These findings support a shift from single-site to scalable multi-site forecasting, reducing operational complexity while improving accuracy in large portfolios.
  </details>

- **[Learning When to Think Aloud in Latent Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06108)**  `arXiv:2611.06108`  
  _Zhehao Zhou, Tongrui Su, Shengyu Zhu, Da Li, Zeyu Liu, Jun Zhu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning improves efficiency by shifting intermediate computation into continuous hidden states. However, fully latent methods often struggle on precision-critical tasks such as mathematical reasoning, while existing hybrid approaches retain substantial token-generation overhead. We introduce VITAL, an importance-guided hybrid reasoning framework that selectively verbalizes informative tokens as reasoning anchors while keeping most computation latent. A lightweight gating head dynamically selects between explicit token generation and direct hidden-state feedback. VITAL constructs hybrid trajectories from explicit CoT using token- importance estimation and trains the model through curriculum-based supervised training, followed by reinforcement learning with a reward that balances correctness and conciseness. Experiments show that VITAL consistently out- performs existing fully latent reasoning methods, achieving relative average accuracy gains of 9.6% on GPT-2 and 5.1% on Llama-3.2-1B at comparable reasoning lengths. Compared with hybrid baselines, VITAL achieves higher reasoning accuracy with substantially shorter reasoning trajectories on Qwen3-1.7B.
  </details>

- **[Learning From Static Context in Federated Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06064)**  `arXiv:2611.06064`  
  _Viktor Walter, Andreas Wagner, Fabian Stricker_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series forecasting is shifting from locally trained, per-series models to global models trained across many heterogeneous series, which requires learning what sets the series apart. When the raw data cannot be centralized, such a global model is trained with federated learning (FL), one client per data owner. State-of-the-art forecasters such as the Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT) tell series apart through a static input that describes each series and does not change over time. In the special case of a federated setting where each client holds a single series, this static input is constant within each client and varies only across clients. We ask whether a federated TFT can learn from it. Per-batch FedAdam, which aggregates gradients after every batch, uses the static input almost as well as central training, whereas communication-efficient schemes with local epochs (FedAvg, FedAdam+Prox) extract far less from it and gain nothing significant on either application dataset. At the same local computation, an Adam-compatible SCAFFOLD keeps the static pathway responsive on wind power and gains significantly there. On building energy its error still drops, but the static pathway falls silent, so drift and communication frequency cannot be fully separated as the cause. Only per-batch FedAdam matches or beats the per-client local models across all datasets. We confirm the effect on building-energy meter data, a wind-power dataset, and a controlled synthetic dataset. The loss is not specific to the TFT: on the synthetic and the wind data two further architectures with different static-conditioning mechanisms show it as well.
  </details>

- **[Alleviating Limited Effectiveness and Multi-Class Collapse in Deep Graph-level Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06052)**  `arXiv:2611.06052`  
  _Xiaoyang Lin, Renchi Yang, Yuqi Chen, Xin Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Given a collection of $N$ graphs, graph-level clustering (GLC) seeks to partition the $N$ graphs into semantically meaningful groups, which serves as a fundamental task in analyzing graph-structured data. While Graph Isomorphism Networks (GINs) have become the de facto encoders for GLC, they still fail to distinguish graphs due to their neglect of global topology. On top of that, existing deep clustering frameworks suffer from severe "clustering collapse" issues, particularly in imbalanced multi-class scenarios. To address these challenges, we propose S$^3$GLC, a novel framework that integrates global topological characteristics with local neighborhood features for balanced GLC. Theoretically, we leverage the Hoffman-Wielandt inequality to construct Spectral Signature as a rigorous lower bound for graph edit distance (GED), providing a computationally efficient proxy for global topological features. We additionally propose a joint manifold-preserving sparse regression module to fuse Spectral Signature with Spatial Representation, eliminating noise while preserving the complementary information. Furthermore, to mitigate clustering collapse, we introduce a balanced clustering objective based on optimal transport, harnessing the Sinkhorn-Knopp normalization to enforce equipartition constraints on cluster assignments. Extensive experiments on 10 real-world datasets demonstrate that S$^3$GLC significantly outperforms 15 state-of-the-art baselines, achieving remarkable improvements on benchmark datasets such as REDDIT-12K and OGBG-PPA.
  </details>

- **[Act-HiMotif: Hierarchical Daily Activity Schedule Generation via Learned Activity-Structure Motifs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06038)**  `arXiv:2611.06038`  
  _Yushi Bai, Kang Liu, Ling Yin, Wei Wang, Yilin Lyu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating daily activity schedules from individual attributes (e.g., age and income) is valuable for activity-based travel modeling and human behavior simulation. The task is challenging because exact activity structures follow a highly imbalanced, long-tailed distribution, while the same structure can admit multiple duration allocations under a fixed full-day budget. Direct generation must therefore learn many exact activity structures from highly uneven observations while modeling their possible temporal realizations. However, many exact structures share recurring activity organization. Based on this observation, we propose Act-HiMotif, a hierarchical motif-based generative model that introduces learned, reusable activity-structure motifs as an intermediate representation between individual profiles and specific activity structures. Act-HiMotif first distinguishes home-only days from active days, then samples a motif from a profile-conditioned prior to guide activity-structure generation, and finally applies a budget-constrained duration allocator to generate activity durations that sum exactly to the full-day horizon. This hierarchy models home-only routing, activity-structure generation, and duration allocation at their corresponding levels, while motifs allow related structures to share statistical support without losing their distinct sequences. Experiments on large-scale travel survey data show that Act-HiMotif outperforms strong baselines in both in-distribution and spatial generalization settings, demonstrating the effectiveness of hierarchical generation and reusable activity-structure motifs for producing realistic profile-conditioned daily activity schedules.
  </details>

- **[TRACK: Multiple Instance Learning for Anti-Money Laundering in Transaction Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06035)**  `arXiv:2611.06035`  
  _Seonkyu Lim, Inwoo Tae, Gwangui Hong, Jaehoon Lee, Jeongwhan Choi, Taehoon Kim, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Anti-money laundering (AML) investigations are organized around accounts, while the suspicious evidence for an account may reside in only a few of its transactions. Yet many account-level detectors summarize transactions before learning, and message-passing models may aggregate once more over neighboring accounts. We name this discrepancy the aggregation mismatch and study it under an account-window benchmark label defined by the presence of at least one flagged transaction. We recast account-level AML detection as multiple instance learning, in which an account-window is a bag and its transactions are instances. We propose TRACK, which encodes every transaction with a shared encoder and selectively pools the encodings into an account decision. To emulate coarse supervision, we withhold the transaction labels of five AML datasets from TRACK throughout training. Under a time-ordered protocol, TRACK achieves the highest average precision among the tabular and graph baselines trained from account labels on every dataset. Despite using only account-window supervision, it remains competitive with edge classifiers trained directly from transaction labels. Its instance scores also rank flagged transactions well above chance and approach or exceed directly supervised ranking performance across datasets.
  </details>

- **[Practical Estimation of the Bayes Optimal Fairness-Accuracy Tradeoff with Soft Labels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06027)**  `arXiv:2611.06027`  
  _Mohit Sharma, Okan Koç, Amit Deshpande, Takashi Ishida, Gang Niu, Rajiv Ratn Shah, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  There is a fundamental limit to the best prediction error of any model for a given data distribution. In classification, the Bayes error quantifies this limit and serves as both a benchmark for trained models and a criterion to detect overfitting. The state-of-the-art Bayes error estimation techniques for binary classification use only soft labels (positive class probabilities) and require no input features or auxiliary training. We extend such notions to constrained classification problems, such as fairness. Fair classification is an important case of constrained or multi-objective classification. It requires a model not only to be accurate but also to yield fair outcomes (equal or near-equal true positive rates) across different demographic groups (e.g., race and gender). This leads to an inherent fairness-accuracy tradeoff or a Pareto frontier that is used to compare fair classifiers against each other. The focus of our work is to estimate the fundamental limit for this fairness-accuracy tradeoff. We derive an explicit expression for the fair Bayes error rate (i.e., best achievable error rate subject to given fairness constraints), and construct consistent, instance-free estimators for it using only soft labels and group information. We provably bound the bias and the sample complexity of our estimators. Empirically, our method produces a robust, low-variance estimate of the optimal fairness-accuracy tradeoff curve, even when the soft labels are noisy and derived from a classification model on hard labels.
  </details>

- **[Decouple to Reuse: Fine-Grained Knowledge Augmentation for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05993)**  `arXiv:2611.05993`  
  _Yuqian Wu, Qingxiang Liu, Yuxin Guo, Weifeng Su, Jian Li, Pengfei Xu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series forecasting is critical for demand prediction and resource management in modern Web services, yet applying pretrained Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) to downstream tasks remains challenging due to domain shifts. Existing retrieval-augmented approaches typically retrieve coarse-grained window contexts and may introduce context-specific noise. To address this problem, we argue that temporal patterns should be reused at a decomposition level, as effective trend and seasonal dynamics may originate from different contexts. We propose \method, a two-stage \underline{F}ine-grained \underline{K}nowledge \underline{A}ugmentation framework that decouples and consolidates pre-training knowledge into compact, task-shared residual codebooks to enhance frozen TSFMs. Specifically, Stage I constructs dual-branch residual codebooks for decoupled trend and seasonal signals, while Stage II dynamically retrieves and merges these fine-grained patterns into target representations via task-adaptive aggregation and gate fusion. Extensive experiments across eight real-world benchmarks with four representative TSFM backbones demonstrate the superior effectiveness and computational efficiency of \method, while further validating the strong transferability of the residual codebooks across different backbones. The source code is available at the anonymous repository:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Reward Budgeting Reduces Premature Convergence in Reinforcement Learning for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05942)**  `arXiv:2611.05942`  
  _Mengni Jia, Mengyu Zhou, Xiaoxi Jiang, Guanjun Jiang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RL for LLM reasoning often reaches an early performance plateau as the policy distribution becomes increasingly sharp. Recent work has studied policy entropy as both a diagnostic and a control signal for this behavior. In our experiments, however, increasing entropy alone does not reliably change the eventual plateau. We analyze RL through its implicit target policy and derive a sharpness factor $\kappa$, together with an effective counterpart $\kappa_{\mathrm{eff}}$, that describes how strongly reward or advantage gaps are translated into probability-ratio gaps. Motivated by this view, we introduce \emph{reward budgeting}, a plug-in advantage construction that assigns a fixed total reward budget to each prompt. For binary rewards and independent on-policy rollouts, its unclipped score-function estimator follows the gradient of an exact finite-$G$ concave objective with diminishing returns, while its advantage gap decreases as more rollouts become correct. For general rewards, a shifted log-mean interpretation holds asymptotically under the stated regularity conditions. These results motivate, rather than establish exact objective equivalence for, the clipped implementations. On Qwen3-8B and Llama3.2-3B-Instruct, reward budgeting consistently improves a range of RL baselines, with gains up to +12.50 on AMC23 and +10.00 on AIME24 and AIME25. Overall, our results support an update-dynamics view of premature convergence and show that strong gains can be obtained without explicitly preserving entropy.
  </details>

- **[BAM: Bivariate Additive Models for Interpretable Tabular Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05940)**  `arXiv:2611.05940`  
  _Atsushi Koike_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generalized Additive Models with pairwise interactions (GA$^2$Ms) are regarded as glass-box models, since each pairwise shape function can be visualized as a contour plot. In practice, the shape functions learned by methods such as EBM or NODE-GAM are so complex (irregular contours and sharp local fluctuations) that a practitioner cannot meaningfully interpret them or check them against domain knowledge. Auditability in this stronger sense requires substantially simpler shape functions. We argue that such simplification can match the accuracy of standard GAMs if the right feature pairs are captured. We propose Bivariate Additive Models (BAMs), a class of GA$^2$Ms in which every pairwise shape function is constrained to a simple parametric form, instantiated as an asymmetric bivariate Gaussian. Each component is visualizable as a unimodal contour map, and its feature pair is selected automatically during training. Experiments on six tabular benchmarks show that BAM attains predictive accuracy comparable to standard GAMs and approaches that of GA$^2$Ms. We also show the trade-off between accuracy and the number of components to inspect.
  </details>

- **[Deep Meets Classical: A CPU-Efficient Decision-Fusion Model for Automatic Modulation Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05937)**  `arXiv:2611.05937`  
  _Zhiyi Yin, Zhizhao Wen, Ruoxin Zhang, Chao Wang, Puyang Xu, Yifan Jiang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automatic modulation classification (AMC) underpins spectrum sensing and non-cooperative signal identification in cognitive radio, yet the two standard CPU-feasible approaches to it, a compact convolutional neural network (CNN) on raw in-phase and quadrature (I/Q) samples and a classical higher-order-cumulant feature classifier, are rarely compared against each other conditioned on signal-to-noise ratio (SNR). We show that they are, in fact, complementary: the CNN is markedly more robust at low SNR, where hand-crafted statistics collapse toward chance, while the cumulant classifier is more accurate at high SNR, where higher-order statistics cleanly separate dense constellations. We exploit this complementarity with FuseAMC, a lightweight decision-fusion model that trains a logistic-regression meta-combiner over the concatenated class-posteriors of an approximately 198k-parameter residual CNN and a gradient-boosted-tree cumulant classifier, on a validation split disjoint from test. On RadioML 2016.10a (5 seeds, 44k-frame held-out test set), FuseAMC reaches 55.8% overall accuracy, improving on the best cumulant-feature baseline by 5.1 points and on the CNN alone by 3.2 points (paired-bootstrap 95% confidence-interval lower bounds of 5.0 and 3.1 points, both positive), while matching the best classical baseline in its own strong high-SNR regime and the CNN in its strong low-SNR regime. With about 0.2 million parameters, a model footprint of a few megabytes, and roughly 3 MFLOPs per frame, the whole pipeline trains and runs on one CPU core, targeting general-purpose and embedded processors without hardware acceleration.
  </details>

- **[MIGONet: a multiple-input graph neural operator with solver-free discrete manufactured-solution training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05876)**  `arXiv:2611.05876`  
  _Muhammad Hasnain, N. K. Anand, Arun Srinivasa_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surrogate modeling of transport requires operators that capture the combined influence of spatially distributed sources and Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions, together with training datasets that can be generated at low computational cost. This work presents MIGONet, a multiple-input graph neural operator, and a discrete method of manufactured solutions (MMS) that trains it without solving a partial differential equation, using steady heat conduction as the model problem. In the discrete MMS procedure, temperature fields are prescribed a priori, and the source terms and Dirichlet or Neumann boundary data are evaluated with the same discrete operators as the reference finite-difference method (FDM), so every training sample satisfies the discrete governing equations by construction. Because the boundary data are derived from each field, a Dirichlet or Neumann condition can be assigned to every boundary segment of every sample, and the boundary specification becomes an operator input. MIGONet supplies representations of the complete source field and boundary data to every node before local message passing. On the rectangular domain, it is compared with a version without the complete-source encoder (MIGONet-local) and with the multiple-input operator network MIONet, using the same training data. MIGONet achieves mean relative temperature errors of 10.31% and 10.64% for curved-wire and Gaussian-spot heating, compared with 45.97% and 48.34% for MIONet and 72.25% and 67.82% for MIGONet-local. For 10,000 samples, discrete MMS reduces data-generation time from 486.65 s to 3.15 s relative to matched iterative FDM labeling. Separately trained MIGONet models are also evaluated on L-shaped, disc-shaped, and ring-shaped domains.
  </details>

- **[PEARL: Prediction Error-Adaptive Regularized Learning for Selective Forgetting under Partial Preference Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05842)**  `arXiv:2611.05842`  
  _Rashmi Gangadharaiah, Balakrishnan Narayanaswamy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  User preferences shift as needs, context, and tastes evolve. This matters for personalized recommendation, LLM response generation, and RLHF-trained reward models, which can become miscalibrated as preferences drift. Existing non-stationary bandit methods forget uniformly across all dimensions, but real preference shifts are typically partial: some dimensions go stale while others remain valid. Neural contextual bandits accumulate confidence monotonically via the Gram matrix, with no mechanism to direct uncertainty where predictions have gone wrong. We propose PEARL (Prediction Error-Adaptive Regularized Learning), which reverses the Gram update along feature directions where predictions are surprisingly wrong, while leaving confidence intact elsewhere. We prove that (i) any non-adaptive policy suffers linear regret after a preference shift, (ii) no fixed exploration rate achieves low regret in both stationary and non-stationary regimes, and (iii) PEARL's cumulative regret scales with the number of shifted users N_s, not the total population N. On PRISM, a personalized LLM response selection benchmark (1,500 users, 21 LLMs), PEARL beats greedy by +4.0 points and the best neural bandit baseline by +2.5 points (p<0.001), while performing on par with greedy when preferences are stable. It beats sliding-window and discounted approaches by 3 to 10 points, showing that selective forgetting outperforms uniform forgetting. The selection policy is a ~100K-parameter MLP that runs in milliseconds on CPU, leaves LLM weights frozen, and works with any API-based model.
  </details>

- **[Optimizing Recourse Under Predictive Multiplicity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05774)**  `arXiv:2611.05774`  
  _Shahin Jabbari_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Algorithmic recourse identifies modifications that can change an unfavorable model prediction into a favorable one. Under predictive multiplicity, similarly performing models can disagree on individual predictions, so recourse valid for one model may fail for another. We formulate robust recourse under predictive multiplicity as a budget-constrained minimization of the worst-case validity loss across a competing set of models, which can include models with different architectures or from different hypothesis classes. We propose an oracle-efficient algorithm called ORPM and prove sufficient conditions for its convergence. We evaluate ORPM on one synthetic and five real datasets, and competing sets consist of linear, neural, mixed, and decision-tree models. Our experiments show decreasing optimization-gap bounds and high validity in many tested settings, with favorable performance against baselines designed for specific model classes or architectures. We also quantify how requiring a common action across competing models changes recourse success at a given budget and examine disparities in this success across demographic groups.
  </details>

- **[Self-Supervised Learning of Universal Latent Spaces for Operator Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05735)**  `arXiv:2611.05735`  
  _Sepehr Mousavi, Levi Lingsch, Siddhartha Mishra, Laura De Lorenzis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural operators fit their representation of functions to the task they are trained on, so every new task rebuilds a reduced space from labeled data. Following the self-supervised pre-training that made representations shareable in natural language processing and computer vision, we decouple representation learning from operator learning. A functional autoencoder is pre-trained on analytic functions synthesized on the fly, with no solver and no labels, to represent functions on arbitrary point clouds, and the latent space it induces is frozen and reused across tasks. The representation is exactly equivariant to translation, dilation, and amplitude, and reconstructs solutions of partial differential equations (PDEs) and natural images on geometries it never saw. Being task-agnostic, the latent space can be interpreted in its own right. The whole space is used and decodes to valid fields, it preserves the ordering of distances between physical fields, its geodesics interpolate physical evolutions, and edit directions transfer across fields. With frozen representation maps, a dataset is encoded once and a neural operator is trained entirely on cached embeddings, which saves memory and training time. On 28 tasks in fluid dynamics, solid mechanics, acoustics, and the Helmholtz and Poisson equations, this paradigm can exceed the accuracy of state-of-the-art end-to-end architectures, with up to an order of magnitude higher throughput and memory savings that fit models nearly two orders of magnitude larger on consumer-grade GPUs. At inference, latent rollouts run up to an order of magnitude faster than regular rollouts, and chunked inference processes functions of over half a billion points on a single consumer-grade GPU.
  </details>

- **[Give the Drafter a Second Chance to Guess: Parallel Reconsideration for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05724)**  `arXiv:2611.05724`  
  _Hoang Anh Duy Le, Huibing Dong, Alireza Khadem, Ziteng Sun, Xiang Song, Yizheng Huang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference with a small drafter, whose per-guess acceptance rate determines the achievable speedup. Feature-level drafters share a design choice: every token, easy or hard, is drafted under a single forward pass. Many of the tokens the target rejects are ones the drafter is itself unsure about yet the target answers decisively. To address this, we introduce Parallel Reconsideration: guided by its own uncertainty, the drafter reconsiders its hesitant guesses with extra passes that run in parallel with the one-pass draft, adding negligible latency. A co-designed training recipe and serving-time drafting algorithm append these reconsidered guesses to the original draft tree as refined candidates. Across models and benchmarks, on average, Parallel Reconsideration improves over the baselines by 5.3% to 10.2% on acceptance length and by 3.5% to 9.2% on speedup.
  </details>

- **[CoReFlow: Consistency-Relaxed Flow Maps for Probabilistic Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05715)**  `arXiv:2611.05715`  
  _Pengnan Chi, Zhiguo Zhang, Xiaoliang Ma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Probabilistic traffic forecasting is essential for reliable Intelligent Transportation Systems (ITS), as it captures uncertainties in complex road networks. Recent diffusion- and flow-based generative models have facilitated uncertainty modeling through a multi-step sampling process. However, beyond slow inference, this process is designed to capture complex multimodal distributions, which misaligns with the limited complexity of the conditional traffic data distribution, causing models to underperform deterministic baselines. To address this, we introduce the \underline{Co}nsistency-\underline{Re}laxed \underline{Flow} Map (CoReFlow) model, presenting the first effort to leverage the one-step generation capability of flow maps, known as solution operators of probability-flow ordinary differential equations. Besides, we improve accuracy using a concise training objective that supervises the model via interpolation endpoints over finite time intervals. Unlike existing methods (e.g., self-distillation and MeanFlow Identity) that maintain trajectory consistency to model multimodal distributions, our objective leverages conditional mean to restrict the consistency, thereby achieving higher accuracy in the probabilistic traffic forecasting task. Extensive experiments on four real-world datasets show CoReFlow achieves state-of-the-art forecasting accuracy. It surpasses recent baselines, provides competitive uncertainty modeling, and reduces inference costs compared to existing generative baselines.
  </details>

- **[Revisiting Critic Estimation in Soft Actor-Critic Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05666)**  `arXiv:2611.05666`  
  _Jan Felix Kleuker, Koen Ponse, Aske Plaat, Thomas M. Moerland_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) is a widely used deep reinforcement learning algorithm that combines entropy regularization with an actor-critic architecture. Standard implementations rely on one-step temporal-difference learning of Q-functions, yet alternative critic design choices remain underexplored. In this work, we revisit three such choices: learning soft advantages instead of soft Q-values, the use of multi-step return targets, and the training of multi-step critics from replay buffer data. Keeping the policy objective fixed, we compare the six critic variants these choices generate: (i) advantage and (ii) dueling-advantage critics learned from one-step transitions, and $\lambda$-return and soft Generalized Advantage Estimation (GAE) critics learned either (iii, iv) on-policy or (v, vi) from replayed trajectory segments. Each variant is evaluated across an entire random hyperparameter search on fourteen tasks, treating the hyperparameters as part of the algorithm. Learning soft advantages instead of soft Q-values does not improve performance, and the soft GAE critic adds nothing over the simpler $\lambda$-return critic. What does improve performance, most clearly on continuous control, is training a $\lambda$-return critic on replayed trajectory segments, even without importance weighting or trace cutting. This extends earlier evidence that uncorrected multi-step targets work well under replay to the full soft $\lambda$-return, entropy terms included. By design, the underlying ideas studied here extend beyond SAC, and our empirical findings aim to encourage their application to a broader class of reinforcement learning algorithms.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive Domain Decomposition for Finite Basis Physics-Informed Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05659)**  `arXiv:2611.05659`  
  _Sarah Helfert, Wanxin Chen, Amanda A. Howard, Panos Stinis, Alexander Heinlein_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work, we present adaptive domain decomposition finite basis physics-informed neural networks (ADD-FBPINNs). The proposed method integrates Partition of Unity Networks (POUNets), a data-driven approach for learning domain partitions, with finite Basis physics-informed neural networks (FBPINNs) to perform more efficient domain decomposition and achieve more accurate approximations. FBPINNs improve upon the accuracy of standard physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) through user-defined subdivisions of the computational domain. By adapting the domain decomposition based on the solution's features using a separable POUNet, the framework can automatically learn partitions that are stable and can be geometrically interpretable. We test this approach on the two-dimensional Helmholtz equation, two-dimensional Poisson equation with multiscale features, and viscous Burgers equation and observe improved accuracy compared with FBPINNs with fixed domain decomposition.
  </details>

- **[When Are Two SAE Latents the Same Feature?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05655)**  `arXiv:2611.05655`  
  _Alexandra Suvorikova, Ilgam Latypov, Anton Trukhan, Yuriy Dorn_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Comparing sparse autoencoders (SAEs) requires connecting feature correspondence, differences between whole dictionaries, and uncertainty from finite evaluation data. We develop a framework that connects these tasks through an energy-weighted optimal-transport plan under a specified similarity geometry. The same plan defines a dictionary-level discrepancy, decomposes it into latent contributions, and quantifies how each latent's mass is distributed across counterparts. We characterize relationships between decoder, activation, and first-order causal similarities; idealized models clarify the interpretation of splitting and absorption. For fixed trained SAEs, we derive joint asymptotic inference for transport costs and fractions, accounting for activation similarities and both energy marginals estimated from the same documents. A joint document bootstrap gives asymptotically valid simultaneous confidence bands. Experiments across four language models locate cross-seed discrepancy and separate context-dependent changes.
  </details>

- **[LACS: Distribution-Sensitive Label-Agnostic Confidence Selection for Dynamic Data Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05616)**  `arXiv:2611.05616`  
  _Heejin Choi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training on large datasets motivates selecting which examples receive expensive training computation. Fixed quotas determine the amount of pruning in advance, while absolute confidence cutoffs depend on the score scale. We study a population-relative alternative that adapts the threshold and retained count together. We introduce LACS (Label-Agnostic Confidence Selector), a training-time rule based on maximum predictive confidence, requiring neither labels at selection time nor per-example gradients. LACS removes examples at or above a log-mean-exp reference plus a margin. We characterize its sensitivity to score gaps and mass, and derive an exact no-pruning condition. For $0<\rho<1$, the removed fraction is at most $\rho$ and at least one example remains. The unsigned score permits both confidently correct and confidently wrong examples to be removed. Seven evaluation settings include neural training, language modeling, and boosting. Neural execution studies reduce backward-active rows by 10-35%; on CIFAR-10, 29% fewer backward rows accompany accuracy means of 89.77% versus 89.82% for full CE. At a matched backward budget and number of optimizer updates, LACS is 1.0 pp above small-batch training and 1.2 pp above random selection over five seeds. Across 14 tabular datasets, quadratic boosting reduces mean test error from 23.36% to 22.96%. Its full training replays use about 30% fewer fitting rows; these counters span a different horizon from validation-selected accuracy. In one RBF-SVM case study, elapsed time falls by 37% with slightly lower error. These findings separate selective-work reduction from realized acceleration: the current exact-current-score neural implementation uses an additional scoring forward, so its backward sparsity does not yet translate into wall-clock acceleration. This overhead is an execution choice rather than a requirement of the selection rule.
  </details>

- **[On the Generalization Limitation of On-Policy Distillation for Post-RL Consolidation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05609)**  `arXiv:2611.05609`  
  _Yichen Wang, Yufa Zhou, Siqi Zhu, Haozheng Luo, Jingzhi Bao, Zhichao Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) extends on-policy distillation (OPD) to consolidate capabilities from multiple teachers. Although prior work reports near-perfect in-distribution (IND) distillation performance, we find that this can mask incomplete capability consolidation beyond the supervised distribution. A preliminary study on synthetic reasoning shows that OPD does not fully transfer teachers' generalization capabilities. We then extend this investigation to real-world tasks, evaluating extrapolative and compositional generalization across mathematical reasoning, coding, agent, and long-context capabilities using Qwen3 models. Across extrapolative settings, OPD and MOPD reach 96.04-102.73% of their teachers' IND scores. Yet their out-of-distribution (OOD) scores remain 2.45-16.25% below the base model's. Across three compositional settings, MOPD improves IND performance over the base model, but its performance on composed tasks as OOD remains 18.19-24.34% below that of the best teachers. To understand these gaps, we examine teacher capability and data coverage as two potential bottlenecks. Jointly alleviating these bottlenecks eliminates OOD forgetting in our experiments and suggests the potential for generalization beyond the teacher. (M)OPD also reduces OOD forgetting on average relative to its reinforcement learning (RL) baselines. Analysis of the associated update geometry reveals smaller and more spectrally concentrated updates on average than those of the RL baselines, along with greater shared update structure under MOPD. Motivated by these findings, we propose geometry-based patching and base-preserving replay to improve OOD performance while largely preserving IND performance. Our findings identify a blind spot in capability consolidation via (M)OPD, highlighting the need for algorithms that explicitly support OOD retention and generalization.
  </details>

- **[C-GRPO: Conformal Group Relative Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05591)**  `arXiv:2611.05591`  
  _Arya Fayyazi, Seyedarmin Azizi, Mehdi Kamal, Massoud Pedram_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning-oriented language model training increasingly relies on multiple on-policy rollouts, but standard Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) uses a fixed group size, wasting compute on easy prompts and under-exploring hard ones. Existing adaptive-budget methods alleviate this inefficiency, yet their stopping rules are heuristic and do not provide a finite-sample calibration guarantee. We propose CGRPO, which replaces the fixed rollout budget of standard GPRO with calibrated conformal thresholds over task-specific nonconformity scores, combining Adaptive Prediction Set (APS)-style calibration for closed-form reasoning, a first-success score for execution-based code, automatic miscoverage selection, and periodic re-calibration. We prove finite-sample coverage for the resulting stopping rule and show that, under monotone policy improvement, recalibration induces a non-increasing expected training budget. Across six benchmarks spanning mathematics, graduate-level science, and code generation, CGRPO reduces mean training rollouts by 49-70% relative to fixed-budget GRPO while retaining strong fixed-budget accuracy, achieves the strongest pass@1 among evaluated static and dynamic baselines on different datasets, and transfers across model families with up to 82% rollout savings.
  </details>

- **[FedSharp: Byzantine Robust Decentralized Federated Learning Under Noisy Channel](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05546)**  `arXiv:2611.05546`  
  _Ojaswi Sinha, Amin Farajzadeh, Animesh Yadav, Melike Erol-Kantarci_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decentralized federated learning (DFL) eliminates the central aggregation server but remains vulnerable to data heterogeneity, Byzantine attacks, and channel noise. We show that noise accumulates as models propagate across nodes, becoming increasingly significant as the learning rate decays. Moreover, accumulated noise enables Byzantine attackers to exploit loss-based model selection by mimicking honest model averages. To address these challenges, we propose screened, hop-aware, ranked, proximal averaging, a robust DFL framework (FedSharp). In this approach, each node maintains models from its 2b predecessors, ensuring at least b honest candidates when at most b nodes are Byzantine. FedSharp screens suspicious models using coordinate-wise median deviations, averages the b lowest-loss candidates to reduce fresh channel-noise variance by a factor of b, and applies noise-adaptive gradient-norm regularization and proximal training to mitigate accumulated noise and non-IID drift. Experiments on a 100-node ring with 33 Byzantine nodes show that FedSharp achieves 93.5-94.9% accuracy on MNIST, compared with 29.4-38.1% for a state-of-the-art single-model-selection benchmark, and 32.8-48.8% on CIFAR-10, compared with 10.0-30.5%, across Gaussian and hidden attacks under IID and non-IID data. On MNIST, FedSharp also achieves accuracy within approximately one percentage point of the benchmark operating without channel noise.
  </details>

- **[Recasting Visual Language Models as EEG Foundation Models for Clinical Neurodiagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05538)**  `arXiv:2611.05538`  
  _Xin Zhou, Elena Sergeeva, Nan Bi, Wenyu Song, Lijun Yin, Hao Deng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Historically automated electroencephalography (EEG) systems was done using task-specific supervised models trained on purposely annotated data. A more recent modeling paradigm relies on creating a unified representation, or a 'foundation model', for multiple modality-specific tasks through large-scale domain-specific pretraining on the unannotated EEG signal. The quality of this unified representation directly depends on the availability of the modality-specific datasets. At the same time, generalist Vision Language Models (VLMs) exhibit broad few-shot learning capacities as well as good fine-tuning adaptability to different visual and language tasks. Can VLMs learn how to read and interpret EEG visually, emulating to a degree the way human clinicians inspect the EEG patterns? In this work we make a case that they can: we show that generalist VLMs without large-scale EEG pretraining perform competitively across a collection of clinical neuro-diagnostic tasks, can adapt to different tasks in a few-shot learning setting better than specialist foundation models, and exhibit better performance with model scaling when modality-specific models fail to exhibit.
  </details>

- **[TSFM Meets LLM: Context as Covariates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05515)**  `arXiv:2611.05515`  
  _Hemanth Ram, Sai Shankar Narasimhan, Shubhankar Agarwal, Sandeep Chinchali_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Textual information is ubiquitous in real-world time series datasets, appearing as news, reports, and annotations that hold key predictive signals not deducible from numerical history alone. Large Language Models (LLMs) interpret text but lack numerical precision, while Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) are quantitatively accurate but cannot reason about the effects of text. Moreover, the lack of large-scale paired multimodal datasets limits progress in bridging this gap. In this work, we address this challenge by providing a scalable approach for generating realistic multimodal datasets by annotating real-world time series with LLMs. Building on this, we propose Context as Covariates (CoCo), a forecasting framework in which an LLM distills its textual reasoning into numerically grounded forecast and confidence covariates that guide a TSFM backbone, thereby exploiting the strengths of both LLMs and TSFMs. We align the two models in stages, first fine-tuning the LLM via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with a reward tied to covariate-induced improvement in forecasting performance, and then fine-tuning the TSFM on covariates generated by the aligned LLM. Our experiments show that models trained on our synthetic multimodal corpus generalize to real benchmarks spanning finance, economics, weather, traffic, and security. Notably, CoCo with Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 as the LLM and Chronos2 as the TSFM achieves up to 12% MAE improvement over state-of-the-art TSFMs and LLM forecasters, such as GPT-4o. Code, checkpoints, and the evaluation datasets are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[Objective-Conditioned Active Preference Learning for Multi-Objective Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05506)**  `arXiv:2611.05506`  
  _Duc Anh Vu, Thong Nguyen, Hoang Pham, Ponhvoan Srey, Luu Anh Tuan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aligning large language models (LLMs) with several objectives, such as helpfulness, harmlessness, and honesty, typically relies on preference judgment for each objective, growing the annotation cost linearly with the number of objectives. Active preference learning can reduce this cost by selecting the comparisons whose labels are expected to change the policy most. Existing remedies either lack an objective-aware query selection mechanism for the multi-objective setting or regard conflicting preferences as harmful, therefore excluding them from the training data. We introduce OGC-DPO (Objective-conditioned Gradient Coverage DPO), an active learning framework that treats each (response pair, objective) combination as a separate query and measures its informativeness through the policy's own implicit gradient, without relying on an external reward model. OGC-DPO then selects the queries that most expand each objective's gradient coverage. Empirical experiments across varying numbers of objectives demonstrate the effectiveness of OGC-DPO over existing active preference learning methods under the same annotation cost. Further analysis demonstrates that informative conflicts, rather than being uniformly harmful, provide a useful policy training signal.
  </details>

- **[Matched but Not Better: An Equivalence-Tested Benchmark of Quantum Machine Learning on Six Clinical Datasets and the QML-Health-READY Reporting Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05442)**  `arXiv:2611.05442`  
  _Mohammed Siraj B, Gousia Habib, Ruban S_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Background: Quantum machine learning (QML) is promoted for clinical prediction, but comparisons with classical models are rarely paired or tested for equivalence. Methods: We compared a quantum-kernel support vector machine (QSVM; 4 qubits, tuned bandwidth) and a variational quantum classifier (VQC) with five tuned classical models across six public clinical datasets. All models used identical four-component inputs and 25 cross-validation splits. Paired AUROC differences were assessed using a corrected resampled t-test, Holm-adjusted equivalence tests (margins: +/-0.02 and +/-0.05), and random-effects pooling. Ablation, noise, finite-shot, and implementation-fault analyses were conducted. We propose QML-Health-READY, an eight-domain reporting framework. Results: Neither quantum model significantly outperformed any classical comparator. Pooled across datasets, QSVM differed from the kernel-matched radial basis function (RBF) SVM by -0.006 AUROC (95% CI: -0.017 to +0.004), equivalent to +/-0.02. VQC differed from the best classical model by -0.007 (95% CI: -0.019 to +0.004). QSVM was equivalent to RBF SVM within +/-0.05 in four of six datasets but was significantly worse than the best classical model on NHANES 2017-18 (-0.076). A classically trivial product-state kernel performed as well as the entangling kernel. With depolarizing noise (p = 0.01) and 1,024 shots per kernel entry, QSVM AUROC decreased by 0.054 (95% CI: 0.036 to 0.071). Untuned bandwidth produced near-chance AUROC (0.48-0.59). Conclusions: Correctly configured quantum classifiers approached but did not exceed tuned classical models. Finite-shot estimation, rather than gate noise, was their main practical cost, while configuration errors could reverse conclusions. QML-Health-READY defines reporting requirements for credible claims.
  </details>

- **[Constraint-Preserving Flow Matching for Spectral Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05436)**  `arXiv:2611.05436`  
  _Anita Yang, Ken-ichi Kawarabayashi, Yohan Jung_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spectral graph generation models graphs through the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of their Laplacians, providing a compact and continuous alternative to direct graph construction. However, these eigensystems must retain the structural properties of graph Laplacians. Existing approaches preserve eigenvector orthonormality but do not enforce the Laplacian nullspace constraint, potentially producing eigensystems inconsistent with Laplacian structure. To address this, we introduce Laplacian-Constrained Stiefel Flow Matching (LaSFM), which preserves the nullspace condition alongside eigenvector orthonormality and positive eigenvalues during generation. We characterize the underlying constrained Stiefel manifold and formulate flow matching on it. We further extend LaSFM to property-guided generation, steering samples toward desired graph properties while maintaining these constraints. Experiments demonstrate improved performance on both spectral and graph-level metrics, along with better alignment with target properties.
  </details>

- **[Stabilizing On-Policy Targeted Guidance in Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05266)**  `arXiv:2611.05266`  
  _Kevin Frans, Alexander M Rush, Sergey Levine, Pieter Abbeel, Ashvin Nair_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Naive on-policy RL is often bottlenecked by the capabilities of the base model --- insufficient coverage can result in an inability to learn from certain rewards. At the same time, rewards in modern post-training systems often come from rich textual sources, e.g. judge models with a rubric of criteria. Can we utilize such structures to provide a rich directional signal that enables improving on otherwise difficult to optimize rewards? In this work, we examine a framework we refer to as targeted guidance that learns from per-turn textual feedback via an on-policy self-distillation objective. Our focus is not only on directly improving hard-to-optimize behaviors, but on how to do so throughout the training process without degrading baseline RL performance. Over a range of settings across an agentic multi-turn software engineering environment, we find success by combining targeted self-distillation with a per-criteria RL reward, and by limiting feedback to turns where the policy specifically fails. We further examine the design choices that lead to stable entropy and reduce oscillatory behavior throughout training.
  </details>

- **[PAC Learning with Multiple Correct Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05133)**  `arXiv:2611.05133`  
  _Steve Hanneke, Farnam Mansouri, Alireza F. Pour, Amirreza Shaeiri, Kun Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study PAC learning when each example comes with a set of at most $\ell$ correct labels and a prediction is correct if it belongs to this set. Training may reveal the full set, only whether a proposed label is correct (bandit feedback), or one correct label chosen by a demonstrator (demonstrations). Our main finding is that demonstrations can be much harder than full sets, and even than bandit feedback, both in which classes can be learned and in how many examples are needed. For finite label spaces, a generalized Natarajan dimension characterizes learnability from full sets and from bandit feedback, in both the realizable and the agnostic setting. From demonstrations, however, a class is realizably learnable if and only if the intersections of at most $\ell+1$ of its hypotheses are learnable from full sets. Already with three labels, this condition can fail for a class learnable from a single full-set example or even a single bandit answer. When the condition holds, we show that $\widetilde O(\varepsilon^{-\ell})$ demonstrations suffice, and the exponent $\ell$ cannot be improved in general, even for classes learnable from $\widetilde O(1/\varepsilon)$ full sets or bandit answers. For countable label spaces and $\ell \geq 2$, our packed dimension, which asks for many disjoint groups of at most $\ell+1$ hypotheses with no common label, characterizes full-set learnability, and demonstrations still reduce to intersections. Agnostic learning from demonstrations, already for finite label spaces, is not determined by the dimensions of the intersection class that characterize realizable learning. Finally, we show that the uniform bound $\ell$ on the number of correct labels is essential. Without this bound, it is impossible to characterize learnability by any dimension determined by finite subclasses.
  </details>

- **[Sample-Grained Approximate Unlearning with Provable Per-Sample Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04501)**  `arXiv:2611.04501`  
  _Yaxin Xiao, Yulin Jin, Li Tang, Haoyang LI, Huadi Zheng, Qingqing Ye, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine unlearning aims to erase the influence of a designated forget set from a trained model while preserving strong utility on the retain set. Approximate unlearning methods efficiently achieve this goal without full retraining, but their effectiveness is primarily assessed using dataset-level aggregate metrics. These aggregate metrics can mask deviations from retraining at the level of individual samples. In this work, we focus on per-sample unlearning effectiveness. We introduce SALA (Sample-grained Likelihoods ALignment), a plug-in optimization framework that enables approximate unlearning methods to operate at the level of individual samples by steering each example toward its correct target distribution. To quantitatively assess this behavior, we further propose the sample-grained distribution gap, a metric that captures per-sample discrepancies between approximate unlearning and exact retraining output distributions. We provide theoretical guarantees showing that SALA contracts this gap and yields a bounded per-sample discrepancy to retraining. To make this framework practical, we develop a low-cost estimation method for the member and non-member target distributions. Extensive experiments on classification and generation tasks demonstrate that SALA reduces the sample-grained distribution gap by up to $86.67\%$ when paired with existing approximate unlearning baselines, while also improving robustness to unlearning hyperparameters.
  </details>

- **[Aligning Training and Rollout Policies for End-to-End FP4 RL on Mixture-of-Experts LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04376)**  `arXiv:2611.04376`  
  _Hei Yi Mak, Hoang Le, Mehran Taghian Jazi, Yunke Peng, Huan Lin, Zhengzhi Tang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work, we study end-to-end FP4 reinforcement learning (RL) post-training for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) large language models. Recent work on FP4 RL has introduced effective recovery recipes that restore accuracy under aggressive low-precision quantization for dense models, but we find that the same approach yields no improvement over the naive FP4 baseline in the MoE setting. We conduct an in-depth study to understand why. Our analysis identifies the root cause in the routing mechanism unique to MoE: because the router makes discrete expert-selection decisions, training-inference policy alignment becomes acutely sensitive to whether the same recovery technique is applied symmetrically on the training and inference sides. When an asymmetric recipe is used-including the dense-model approach, which corrects only the rollout-training-inference alignment deteriorates sharply, as measured by the Pearson correlation coefficient between training and inference probabilities, and router selection matching rates. Building on this finding, we propose MoEResQ, a quantization-recovery recipe for MoE RL post-training that enforces symmetry between the training- and inference-side corrections. MoEResQ restores training-inference alignment and substantially closes the accuracy gap to full precision, establishing symmetric recovery as the key principle in FP4 RL for MoE models.
  </details>

- **[Separating Common and Unique Directions for Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04108)**  `arXiv:2611.04108`  
  _Shiting Wang, Yingjie Zou, Ke Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model merging combines multiple fine-tuned models without additional training, and arithmetic methods have emerged as a simple yet effective way to merge models without data. However, existing arithmetic methods treat task vectors in parameter space and apply a single element-wise rule to directions that can differ in mergeability. In a shared directional basis, we find a consistent two-regime structure: a low-dispersion common bulk shared across tasks and a high-dispersion task-dominated tail that carries disproportionate energy. These findings motivate Common--Unique Subspace Decomposition (CUSD), a data-free pre-merging method that separates task vectors into common and unique directional components, merges the common components with a downstream arithmetic operator, and retains unique residuals separately. Experiments across $11$ base models, four model families, and four arithmetic-based merging operators show that CUSD improves 42 of 44 model--operator combinations and achieves up to 22.5% improvement over the corresponding baselines. Our code is publicly available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Pretraining Data Scale Reverses the Muon-AdamW Fine-Tuning Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03633)**  `arXiv:2611.03633`  
  _Catherine Li, Jingchu Gai, Andrej Risteski, Jacob Mitchell Springer, Aditi Raghunathan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Muon has recently emerged as a promising optimizer for pretraining large language models, offering a speedup over AdamW in minimizing pretraining loss. However, it remains unclear whether Muon's advantage persists after fine-tuning. In this paper, we study the downstream behavior of Muon and AdamW and uncover a surprising trend: beyond a sufficiently large pretraining data budget, AdamW outperforms Muon after fine-tuning. Moreover, as model size increases, AdamW overtakes Muon at progressively smaller pretraining token-parameter ratios. To explain this reversal, we view fine-tuning as a perturbation of the outputs and introduce the margin-to-spread ratio, which measures how far the correct logit lies above the wrong ones relative to how widely the wrong logits are spread. In language models, Muon has the larger margin-to-spread ratio at small data budgets and AdamW at large ones, mirroring the fine-tuning trend. In a linear associative memory, we prove that after one update the margin-to-spread ratio of gradient descent gains on that of Muon as the data budget grows, and simulations show that this trend persists after extended training.
  </details>

- **[NorthCUA Technical Report: Turbocharging Computer-Use Agents through Experience at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02102)**  `arXiv:2611.02102`  
  _Chaoyun Zhang, Bingxuan Li, Pinxue Guo, Yangfan Gao, Zikun Cai, Tianci Xie, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling computer-use agents from individual actions to complex software work requires experience that connects decisions to complete outcomes. We present NorthCUA, an integrated system for turbocharging computer-use agents through experience at scale. Its Experience Flywheel supplies diverse executable tasks to the NorthCUA Harness, which sustains long interactions, records decisions, and checks task outcomes. The Flywheel uses these records to curate verified trajectories. For unsolved tasks, recursive self-improvement evolves reusable skills and preserves verified milestones so later attempts build on earlier progress. Staged Learning combines this experience with grounding data from the Flywheel to develop GUI competence through mid-training and long-horizon supervised fine-tuning. The policy then learns from fresh attempts in the same Harness through joint desktop-web reinforcement learning, closing the loop between experience production and policy improvement. The resulting Nano, Mini, and Pro family achieves state-of-the-art results among open-source models across GUI grounding, desktop execution, and Web interaction under the reported evaluation settings.
