For more responsive Web workflows, NorthCUA-Ultrafast pairs direct action selection with open-ended generation on pre-RL Nano, offering a measured quality-latency tradeoff. Together, these advances establish NorthCUA as a high-performing model family and a reusable foundation for agents that carry complex software work from intent to completion.
  </details>

- **[TensorSSM: Memorizing Spatial Position in Dynamic State Space for Tensor Core Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40871)**  `arXiv:2610.40871`  
  _Sheng Zhang, Xinyi Wang, Maolin Wang, Xiangyu Zhao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-dimensional tensors are ubiquitous in real-world applications but lead to massive storage overhead. Functional tensor decomposition addresses this by employing neural networks to map element coordinates to tensor cores, significantly reducing the parameters for data compression. However, existing functional methods typically flatten high-order tensors into 1D sequences. This folding operation causes highly overlapping local coordinate sequences and struggles to capture complex cross-mode spatial dependencies, severely limiting their expressiveness on diverse datasets. To tackle these challenges, we propose TensorSSM, a novel Implicit Neural Representation (INR) for functional decomposition method that memorizes spatial positions within a dynamic State Space Model (SSM). Specifically, TensorSSM first decomposes mode-wise coordinates into sequences spit by K-ary rule, encoding spatial tokens from coarse to fine, which effectively captures spatial variations. Furthermore, we extend the SSM's state space to explicitly map cross-mode spatial dependencies, allowing inter-mode interactions to enhance the updates of each mode. By integrating cross-mode information from previous hidden states into the current state transitions, TensorSSM effectively expands tensor representation along two dimensions: the sequence dimension for coarse-to-fine spatial tokens, and the state dimension for cross-mode memorization. This design effectively integrates spatial information into the state space and the tensor core, substantially advancing the state adaptability and enhancing the expressiveness of the data compression. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TensorSSM achieves significantly high reconstruction accuracy and storage efficiency, exhibiting robust expressiveness across diverse tensor datasets without relying on strict spatial assumptions.
  </details>

- **[Communication-Efficient Clustered Federated Learning in Non-IID Wireless Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38970)**  `arXiv:2610.38970`  
  _Martin Isaksson, Rickard Cöster, Šarūnas Girdzijauskas, Seif Haridi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current and emerging mobile networks face increasing privacy, scalability, and performance challenges as distributed learning becomes essential for advanced 6G services. These challenges are amplified by the presence of non-IID data, naturally arising in heterogeneous wireless environments, that degrade the performance of standard Federated Learning (FL) approaches. To address non-IID data across clients, we propose a Clustered Federated Learning (CFL) framework that assigns clients to specialized models during training. Data-domain clustering initializes model-based clustering, enabling identification and exploitation of client similarities without sharing raw data while reducing communication cost.Motivated by mmWave beam selection, we evaluate our methods on a wireless simulation dataset (DeepMIMO) and complement it with two non-IID benchmarks (FEMNIST, Rotated CIFAR-10) to isolate different non-IID characteristics. Our best configurations improve accuracy over IFCA by 4.8 pp on DeepMIMO and 10.6 pp on FEMNIST while reducing downlink data volume by 83.1 % on FEMNIST compared to IFCA.We demonstrate that different clustering strategies suit different non-IID characteristics. These results show that our CFL methods enable resource-efficient, and high-performance distributed learning in future 6G networks.
  </details>

- **[A Hybrid Framework for Explainable Aviation Risk: Causal Chain Extraction from Safety Reports with Physics-Based Failure Quantification and Interventional Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35658)**  `arXiv:2610.35658`  
  _Yutian Pang, Yifang Tian, Xinyu Zhao, Jueming Hu, John-Paul Clarke, Hans-Arno Jacobsen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aviation authorities hold decades of accident reports whose narratives record how investigators reconstructed each accident. Computational work on these archives has mostly sorted reports into fixed taxonomies. In this work, we propose a hybrid framework in which a text-based corpus identifies the leading factors of a failure mode. Physics-based models then quantify how likely that failure is at a stated operating point. Ordered causal chains extracted from 56{,}202 American accident reports with a local large language model aggregate into a causation knowledge graph. On an evaluation sample of 2{,}000 reports, the chains recover 90.8\% of investigator-flagged causes. Each physical factor whose drivers the record carries receives an occurrence model calibrated to the accident population. A leaky noisy-OR fitted to the corpus combines the factors into a failure mode probability that supports intervention. For engine power loss, the carburetor icing model separates 1{,}205 icing accidents from other accidents using temperature and dewpoint alone, reaching 0.61 in area under the receiver operating characteristic curve against a density ratio ceiling of 0.64. Routine weather hours give no valid control, because flying concentrates in fair weather. On later years the physics adds 0.03 to that area for occurrence and nothing for severity, so its value lies in interpretability. On four named accidents, abduction gives a probability of necessity of 0.29 to 0.50 for the heat omission, under monotonicity. The Fast Causal Inference algorithm does not license the largest backdoor adjustment on the learned networks.
  </details>

- **[Last-Iterate Stationarity of Muon with Finitely Many Newton-Schulz Iterations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34158)**  `arXiv:2610.34158`  
  _Ping Li, Vincent Y. F. Tan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Muon has demonstrated strong empirical performance in large-scale language-model training, motivating a deeper theoretical understanding of its practical implementation. Unlike the idealized polar-factor update, practical Muon employs a fixed number of quintic Newton-Schulz iterations. Existing analyses primarily establish average- or best-iterate stationarity guarantees, while last-iterate convergence remains less understood. In this work, we investigate last-iterate gradient convergence of practical finite-step Muon on smooth, lower-bounded nonconvex objectives. In the deterministic setting, we show that Muon without input stabilization can exhibit cyclic behavior even on a smooth convex quadratic. In contrast, with positive stabilization, we establish last-iterate gradient convergence and square-summability of gradients for any fixed momentum coefficient below one and a sufficiently small constant learning rate. In the stochastic setting, we first demonstrate that persistent bounded, unbiased noise can prevent convergence under fixed learning rates and momentum, even with positive stabilization. We then establish almost-sure and mean-square last-iterate gradient convergence under bounded-variance stochastic gradients by combining diminishing learning rates with increasing momentum. Finally, we construct a smooth counterexample demonstrating that neither convergence theorem implies a pointwise polynomial convergence rate. Nevertheless, we establish density-one convergence rates, guaranteeing polynomial gradient decay for all but an asymptotically vanishing fraction of iterations.
  </details>

- **[Reactive Optimizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33826)**  `arXiv:2610.33826`  
  _Xingjian Bai, Jia-Hao Liang, Jayden Teoh, Vincent Sitzmann_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study a reactive optimizer that uses a pretrained language-model agent to inspect training dynamics and rewrite executable optimizer code during a single training run. The agent can change update rules and schedules for individual parameter groups, without optimizer-specific meta-training or memory transferred from other runs. We evaluate this approach, OPAL, on language modeling and image generation when a recipe has not been tuned for the target setting. Across the evaluated settings, it matches or improves on selected fixed recipes. Analysis of its decisions shows changes to learning-rate schedules, optimizer structure, numerical precision, and momentum. Controlled experiments also show adaptation to repeated data corruption and reduced overfitting. We report these behaviors together with the agent's computational cost and implementation requirements.
  </details>

- **[Bridging the Data Gap: Source-Informed Task-Space Transport for Few-Shot Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31323)**  `arXiv:2610.31323`  
  _Jinjian Jiang, Jing Peng, Rehan Deen, Yu Cao, Ziquan Liu, Muhammad Salman Haleem, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When an object category is well represented in one domain but scarcely observed in another, adapting a diffusion model to the target can be unreliable. Yet the same source-target relationship may have been observed for other categories. We ask whether this recurring relation can be learned and reused for a new semantic task. We introduce Meta-Diff, which embeds source and target distributions in a shared low-dimensional task space, learns an affine transport map across paired training tasks, and generates from the transported source coordinate using a shared diffusion backbone. At deployment, it requires source examples and the identity of a previously learned relation, but neither target images nor target-specific optimisation. Gaussian-mixture experiments examine how transfer depends on task information retained by the encoder, relation predictability and the size of the source--target shift. On held-out CIFAR-100 validation tasks, correct-source transport achieves 13.9% lower mean sliced Wasserstein distance than wrong-source transport. In the one-target final-test comparison, it achieves 14.7% lower mean distance than target-only adaptation and 32.7% lower than full fine-tuning under validation-selected settings. These gains have clear limits: directly applying the known corruption to clean-source generations performs better on CIFAR, and requested-class identity remains weak. Office-Home shows domain alignment without reliable object identity; clinical stress tests show no resolved source-specific advantage. The results characterise when reusable task-space relations help and where their generative limitations remain.
  </details>

- **[Sparse Federated Echo State Networks for Time-Series Classification via Smooth-L1 Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21742)**  `arXiv:2610.21742`  
  _Gengcheng Lyu, Manuel Röder, Frank-Michael Schleif_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Echo State Networks (ESNs), as lightweight implementations of reservoir computing, provide efficient solutions for temporal data processing in resource-constrained environments. While standard ESNs offer computational efficiency, their dense readout layers inflate the stored model and the per-sample inference cost. This paper introduces a Smooth-L1 regularized ESN (SL1ESN) that promotes sparsity in the readout layer while preserving differentiability for gradient-based optimization. The method employs a twice-differentiable Smooth-L1 approximation compatible with Newton's method, which we exploit both for centralized training and for a second-order (Newton-step) federated aggregation scheme. Under a single fixed shrinkage threshold shared by every dataset and both settings, experiments on seven time-series classification benchmarks show that SL1ESN learns readouts that are 77-95% sparse in the centralized setting and 75-95% sparse in the federated one. On five of the seven benchmarks, the mean accuracy of the sparse readout over ten seeds differs from that of the stronger dense baseline by at most 0.7 points in the centralized setting and 1.6 points in the federated one, differences of the same order as the seed-to-seed variation, while on the remaining two it trails by 2.4-5.8 points. The second-order federated update converges in only a few rounds. The resulting sparsity compresses the stored model, the per-sample inference cost, and the server-to-client broadcast by a comparable factor. These results demonstrate the practical value of sparse readouts for edge computing and IoT deployments.
  </details>

- **[Fractional Power-of-Two Quantization for Efficient and Effective Multiplier-Free LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21257)**  `arXiv:2610.21257`  
  _Sunghyun Wee, Geunjae Choi, Hyeonjin Kim, Suyoung Kim, Nojun Kwak_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) demand efficient inference, where the multiply-accumulate (MAC) operations in linear projections dominate compute and energy cost. Post-training quantization (PTQ) reduces this cost by mapping weights and activations to low-bit integers. Power-of-Two (PoT) weight quantization promises efficient multiplier-free LLM inference via shift-add logic, but suffers significant accuracy degradation at low bit-widths due to its coarse base-2 quantization grid. We introduce Fractional PoT (FPoT), a base-$\sqrt{2}$ (half-octave) fractional PoT grid that achieves practical 4-bit weight quantization while preserving a fully integer shift-add datapath. The method combines (i) the base-$\sqrt{2}$ weight grid with uniform integer activations, (ii) a calibration-time grid-alignment refinement absorbed into the static per-channel scale, and (iii) a lightweight 1-adder $\sqrt{2}$ approximation whose error is absorbed during weight calibration via approximate-grid quantization, yielding a processing element (PE) with significantly fewer gates than a 4-bit integer multiplier. On Llama-3-8B with 4-bit weight and activation quantization, FPoT improves zero-shot commonsense reasoning performance by 8.72% relative to the PoT baseline, while maintaining performance close to the uniform integer baseline, with only a negligible 0.4%p degradation. These results hold across various configurations spanning seven models and three bit-widths. Furthermore, FPoT can be integrated with state-of-the-art LLM PTQ methods, demonstrating framework-agnostic applicability.
  </details>

- **[Revisiting the 16x Pretraining Compute Gap between Diffusion and Autoregressive Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17590)**  `arXiv:2610.17590`  
  _Guanghan Wang, Bing He, Zhan Shi, Benoit Dumoulin, Hanqing Lu, Volodymyr Kuleshov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (DLMs) have attracted wide attention for their fast, parallel decoding, but they are believed to be far less efficient to train than autoregressive language models (ARMs): a widely cited study reports that a DLM needs roughly 16x the training compute of an ARM to match its likelihood, a figure that has made pretraining DLMs from scratch look prohibitive at scale. We find that the methodology behind this figure has fallen out of step with current DLM practice: it scores the model with its evidence lower bound (ELBO) and trains a full-sequence, decoder-only model. We therefore revisit the scaling laws of DLMs, adopting the exact likelihood under the model's own decoding order, block diffusion training, an encoder-decoder architecture, and a sweep over the block sizes used in practice. Under this new protocol, we study across ten model variants, about 1,700 models in total, and find that pretraining DLMs is far cheaper than previously thought. Concretely, scoring with the exact likelihood reduces the compute multiple to 12.8x; block diffusion training and an encoder-decoder architecture further reduce it to 7.8x at block size 32, and smaller block sizes narrow it to 4.6x at block size 8 and 3.0x at block size 4. We complement these scaling analyses with an empirical rule for the optimal decoder depth of encoder-decoder block diffusion, a held-out compute budget beyond the fitting range at which the gap between variants persists, and downstream benchmark results where likelihood improvements carry over to accuracy. Overall, the 16x figure substantially overstates the pretraining cost gap between DLMs and ARMs, and pretraining DLMs from scratch is far more viable than previously assumed.
  </details>

- **[Cite What You Explore: Budget-Aware LLM Reasoning over Medical KGs with Verifiable Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07739)**  `arXiv:2610.07739`  
  _Chen Chen, Dongjie Wang, Mei Liu, Zijun Yao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-discharge risk prediction from electronic health records (EHRs) is difficult because many dependencies that link discharge-time observations to downstream complications, such as comorbidity cascades and drug-disease interactions, are absent from the record. External medical knowledge graphs (KGs) can supply these missing dependencies, but tracing them in a clinical setting demands three properties. KG exploration must remain cost-bounded, retrieved evidence must be differentiated by source quality, and the resulting rationale must be citable for review. Large language models (LLMs) can plan and verify over structured evidence, making them natural candidates for KG reasoning, but existing LLM-based methods do not satisfy these three properties jointly. In this paper, we propose BAR, a Budget-Aware LLM Reasoning framework over medical KGs with three contributions. First, BAR refines the raw KG into disease-specific evidence graphs whose edges carry support scores and provenance records, turning the KG into a quality-annotated reasoning space rather than a static feature source. Second, an LLM reasons over this graph through a plan-navigate-verify loop that decomposes the question into steps, retrieves evidence under a patient-specific budget, and revises when verification fails. Third, a reasoning policy is trained with a reward that compares predictions with and without acquired evidence, combined with acquisition cost and citation-integrity terms. Across 8 diseases and 3 prediction horizons on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV, BAR achieves higher performance and citation precision than strong baselines while using fewer LLM tokens. Its gain over EHR-only prediction grows at longer horizons, and its advantage carries over to an external benchmark.
  </details>

- **[Backward-Consistent Diffusion Sampling for Sparsely Observed PDE Inverse Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04624)**  `arXiv:2610.04624`  
  _Yida Pan, Muhammad H. Ashiq, Chanyong Jung, Yixuan Jia, Jonah M. Miller, Qing Qu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recovering Partial Differential Equation (PDE) coefficient fields from extremely sparse observations is a severely ill-posed inverse problem for which generative machine learning methods (e.g., diffusion models) have become a leading way to encode the prior. Recent state-of-the-art diffusion solvers lift these priors to function spaces, finding a physics-consistent reconstruction in the output space of the diffusion denoiser. We prove that, in a discontinuous PDE setting, output space methods can result in failure to appropriately minimize the unobserved error with the correct coefficient field. Consequently, we propose Function Space Backward-Consistent Sampling (FunBCS), an input space optimization approach for solving PDE problems which aims to find the best input such that the denoiser reconstruction is physics-consistent. We then prove that FunBCS appropriately minimizes the unobserved error, unlike output space optimization methods. Per our theoretical analysis, we also provide insights on how to dynamically allocate the number of input space optimization steps used throughout the sampling process. Our evaluations, across four PDE inverse problems (including the discontinuous Darcy flow), demonstrate that FunBCS reduces the reconstruction error by $27$-$64\%$ while running $1.4$-$2.1\times$ faster when compared to the current state-of-the-art.
  </details>

- **[PatchKV: Weight-Space Compensation of KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39329)**  `arXiv:2609.39329`  
  _Chanryeol Lee, Chanhyuk Lee, Yeonwoo Choi, Donggyun Kim, Seunghoon Hong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context inference with Large Language Models (LLMs) is bottlenecked by the linearly growing memory of the key-value (KV) cache. Existing compression methods reduce the cache through token eviction or approximation, but degrade sharply at aggressive compression budgets. We propose PatchKV, a training-free framework that compensates KV cache compression methods by carrying part of the context in the model's weights. PatchKV pairs an off-the-shelf compressed KV cache with a context-specific weight patch, which is computed once at context-loading time and served for downstream queries for the context. The weight patch is derived in closed form via ridge regression, by aligning the block-wise activations of context-derived reference query tokens under the full cache and the compressed cache. Once merged into the model, the patch leaves the forward graph and per-query inference cost unchanged in the single-context, multi-query setting. Across long-context QA (SCBench with up to 170K tokens, SQuAD, NIAH) and math (GSM8K) benchmarks on three model architectures, PatchKV consistently improves cache compression methods, suggesting an alternative direction to compensate them at aggressive budgets.
  </details>

- **[From Grey-Box to Green-Box: When can Physics-Informed Machine Learning Reduce Carbon Footprints in Structural Health Monitoring?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33387)**  `arXiv:2609.33387`  
  _Daisy R. Bradley, Nathan A. Hinchliffe, Daniel J. Pitchforth, Matthew R. Jones, Elizabeth J. Cross_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning plays an increasingly vital role in engineering, but the corresponding increase in compute time is not without environmental cost. Physics-informed machine learning or "grey-box" models have been developed to overcome some of the limitations of traditional black-box learners, utilising the physical insight that an engineer would have about the structure they are modelling and have shown promising results in the structural engineering field among many others. This work explores whether an additional advantage could be a reduced environmental impact, considering the relationship between training data quantity and training time, linking this duration to carbon emissions from computing.In a structural health monitoring context, four physics-informed machine learning approaches - spanning Gaussian processes and neural networks - are evaluated: residual modelling, input augmentation, hybrid modelling, and constrained learning. The emissions for training each of the models to reach a given error threshold is compared, and in most examples, shown to be lower for the physics-informed models (with input augmented models being an exception). This reduction in training emissions further compounds the environmental savings achieved by collecting and storing less data. Although promising results, we cannot expect a silver bullet and the case studies demonstrate that a trade-off is needed between the increased complexity that comes from introducing physics into a machine learner, against the gain from reduced training data requirements.
  </details>

- **[What Should Data Teach? Moving Bottlenecks Across Circuit, Store, and Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32991)**  `arXiv:2609.32991`  
  _Yixiao Chen, Ke Cheng, Yue Liu, Jun Zhang, Jie Jiang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  What should data teach a language model at a particular point in training? A circuit view reveals three distinct bottlenecks: forming a computation, making its required content available, and selecting among available routes. A shared diagnosis-to-data principle connects them: localize the missing operation, preserve its causal relation, vary shortcut-bearing context, and re-audit the residual. Formation-sensitive selection and prerequisite ordering accelerate a binding-matching-transport path; a brief early prefix from the same training multiset retains a validation advantage through 100B tokens. Availability counterfactuals then distinguish writing content from invoking available memory, while paired supervision and context-opportunity ranking improve matched route decisions and long-context answer likelihood. A continuous 350M-model experiment connects the three interventions on the same facts: early circuit training improves subsequent learning, and the complete sequence outperforms stage-replacement controls on facts withheld from Use teaching. Independent query surfaces and opposed-source decisions expose conditional arbitration as the remaining frontier. Together, these results show why a change in the limiting operation calls for a change in supervision, not merely a new ranking of difficult examples.
  </details>

- **[Robust Successor Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31016)**  `arXiv:2609.31016`  
  _Erik Nikulski, Yamen Habib, Vicenç Gomez, Anders Jonsson, Rubén Moreno-Bote, Javier Segovia-Aguas_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generalization in Reinforcement Learning (RL) refers to the ability to execute close-to-optimal policies in unseen tasks after the agent has been trained on a different set of tasks. Building on the seminal work of the successor representation and further adaptations with function approximation, Transfer in RL has traditionally focused on generalizing to tasks that only differ in the reward function. A decade after the introduction of the successor representation, Robust RL emerged simultaneously from several articles in the field of operations research. In Robust RL, the transition kernel is unknown, and the goal is to maximize the expected reward under this uncertainty. Our work unifies these two paradigms through robust successor features, which generalize across both the reward function and the transition kernel, under the assumption that tasks are linear Markov Decision Processes. We derive a bound on Generalized Policy Improvement (GPI) that explicitly quantifies how performance degrades with the mismatch between transition kernels, recovering existing successor-feature guarantees when dynamics are shared. Finally, the generalization capabilities of robust successor features are validated on several grid-based benchmarks and compared to previous alternatives that focus solely on either the reward or the transition kernel.
  </details>

- **[Decoupling Knowledge and Privacy: Post-Task Self-Distillation Replay for LLM Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29711)**  `arXiv:2609.29711`  
  _Shengtao Wen, Yunying Yang, Xiang Chen, Lingbing Guo, Yu Tian, Sheng-Jun Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Privacy-preserving continual learning (PPCL) must reduce the reproduction of sensitive content while retaining useful knowledge across sequential tasks. Formal privacy guarantees characterize randomized mechanisms, whereas operational output control concerns whether a trained model selectively reduces the likelihood of sensitive content in its outputs. In this work, we investigate the latter together with continual-learning utility under realistic task evolution. Retention and privacy correction operate at different granularities: task acquisition requires broad preservation of current- and old-task behavior, whereas privacy correction targets sparse annotated positions. Joint optimization leaves the current-task preservation target continually changing. We propose SPARK, a retention-correction decomposition that first freezes the learned post-task distribution and then applies selective correction around this stable reference. Self-Distillation Replay learns the current task while distilling behavior from previous tasks, and Post-Task Privacy Correction reduces annotated-PII likelihood while anchoring current- and old-task non-PII behavior to the resulting checkpoint. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that SPARK achieves effective selective PII suppression while preserving strong continual-learning utility and knowledge retention across diverse settings. Code and data will be released upon publication.
  </details>

- **[Robust Policy Optimization via Adversarial Importance Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13044)**  `arXiv:2609.13044`  
  _Amine Andam, Jamal Bentahar, Mustapha Hedabou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Significant progress has been made in safeguarding Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) policies against input perturbations. Developing robust DRL involves three main stages: algorithm design, implementation, and evaluation. In this work, we identify and address a key limitation at each stage. First, we introduce Adversarial Importance Sampling (Advis), a method that estimates and optimizes worst-case returns directly from trajectories already collected during standard training. Advis satisfies three desirable criteria that prior work does not jointly achieve: it requires no additional environment interactions, no auxiliary networks, and captures long-term robustness. Second, we introduce advrl, a modular PyTorch library that provides clean, single-file implementations of existing robustness methods and adversarial attacks, enabling rapid prototyping and reproducible evaluations. Third, we revisit evaluation under learned adversaries and show that optimal adversarial hyperparameters do not transfer across agents, which can lead to an overestimation of robustness when using a limited set of attacker configurations. Accordingly, we evaluate policies against a large and diverse set of attackers, using 6 to 14 times more configurations than prior work. Finally, experiments on continuous control environments show that Advis achieves stronger worst-case performance and greater training efficiency than existing baselines.
  </details>

- **[LongCounsel-8: A Benchmark Suite for Longitudinal Depression Tracking from Multi-Session Counseling Dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03507)**  `arXiv:2609.03507`  
  _Jiayi Li, Bingsheng He, Zhaomin Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to assess depression in counseling conversations, where psychology evidence suggests that tracking change across sessions matters more than any single score. However, existing dialogue benchmarks score each session on its own, and an accurate score for every session does not guarantee that a method follows the changes between sessions. We introduce LongCounsel-8, a benchmark for tracking depression across counseling sessions. Its systematic pipeline takes client profiles from real counseling cases, depression scores from real longitudinal data, and turn length and form from real counseling dialogues, yielding 7,749 five-session trajectories from three LLMs. Every session is generated to express a preset score on the PHQ-8, a standard eight-symptom depression questionnaire. Validation shows that the labels are self-consistent and the dialogues close to real counseling. Following clinical research on treatment monitoring, we propose a trend-based evaluation protocol reflecting real-world tracking needs: changes between sessions, crossings of the screening cutoff, trajectory types, and symptom-level changes. Extensive experiments show all five existing methods tested fall short in tracking trends: none estimates changes better than a no-change baseline, at most 40% of cutoff crossings are detected, and adding earlier sessions lowers change error but causes more missed improvements.
  </details>

- **[Continuous Adversarial MeanFlow Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19540)**  `arXiv:2608.19540`  
  _Yara Bahram, Zahra Dehghani, Mélodie Desbos, Eric Granger, Pablo Piantanida, Mohammadhadi Shateri_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training fast generators on new domains with limited data remains challenging for two reasons. First, adapting a pretrained diffusion or flow model leaves its costly multi-step sampling intact, and existing acceleration methods are tied to the source parameterization (x, epsilon, v, or u), so heterogeneous pretrained models share no common acceleration target. Second, while adversarial post-training sharpens generation quality, existing criteria each cover only one end of the step range. Discrete criteria compare jump endpoints and lose their signal as the jump shrinks, while continuous criteria score the instantaneous velocity and misjudge the large jumps of few-step sampling. Neither fits MeanFlow (MF) models, which predict average velocities over intervals of any length. We propose MeanFlow-Transfer (MF-T), which adapts a source domain model of any parameterization and compresses it into a few-step MF generator through a common velocity map. We then introduce Continuous Adversarial MeanFlow (CAMF), which scores the change of a learned potential along each predicted interval jump of MF. CAMF needs no gradient penalty or Jacobian-vector product (JVP). We also prove it stays well-conditioned for every interval length and recovers the instantaneous criterion as the interval vanishes. Adapting four ImageNet-based source models (DiT, SiT, JiT, iMF) to five target domains, MF-T + CAMF approaches or exceeds the fine-tuned many-step teacher in FID and FDD at up to 125x fewer Neural Function Evaluations (NFEs). Further, CAMF improves MF-T's few-step FID by about 30% on average, outperforming prior adversarial post-training on iMF at every NFE.
  </details>

- **[Do Tabular Foundation Models Agree with Themselves?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06004)**  `arXiv:2608.06004`  
  _Christian Klötergens, Vijaya Krishna Yalavarthi, Lars Schmidt-Thieme, Tom Hanika_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tabular Foundation Models (TFMs) are the state of the art for many tabular prediction tasks. They are transformers that approximate the Bayesian posterior predictive distribution under a pretraining prior. TFMs become multivariate predictors autoregressively, by sampling one target and appending it to the features. This paper is the first to evaluate whether this setup induces a valid stochastic process. Informally, we ask: *Do we get the same answer to the same question asked in two different ways?* The standard Kolmogorov consistency conditions cannot be checked on TFMs directly, since TFMs are unable to model certain conditional distributions. We therefore propose two adapted, testable requirements. Marginalization consistency demands that marginalized conditionals equal directly predicted marginals, and factorization consistency that both factorization orders yield the same joint. Since the data-generating distribution satisfies both, a perfect TFM would as well, and every violation lower-bounds how far the model's predictions are from the ground truth. Every TFM we evaluate violates both requirements, for classification and regression, on all datasets.
  </details>

- **[EvolveNet: Collaborative Harness Evolution for Agent Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04968)**  `arXiv:2608.04968`  
  _Jun Nie, Yonggang Zhang, Qianshu Cai, Yiu-ming Cheung, Xinmei Tian, Bo Han_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An LLM agent can acquire persistent improvements by evolving its harness---the executable program governing model calls, tool use, verification, and recovery---without updating model weights. When the same harness serves users with different workloads, isolated evolution limits the sharing of useful discoveries, while centralized adaptation can introduce conflicting task-specific changes. We introduce EvolveNet, a framework for collaborative harness evolution that connects local specialization with shared capability accumulation. Clients evolve the same broadcast harness on their local workloads in parallel, and a server composes their adaptations into an updated shared program. Redistribution makes accepted discoveries available to all clients, allowing subsequent local evolution to build on improvements accumulated across the collaboration. EvolveNet supports this process through evidence-guided, scope-typed program aggregation and behavioral validation. Our theoretical analysis provides sufficient conditions for retaining and combining complementary improvements despite program interactions. Experiments across five settings show improved shared-harness performance, with ablation and behavioral analyses supporting the contribution of composition beyond specialist selection alone.
  </details>

- **[Sedentary Behavior Classification for Wearable Sensors with a CNN-BiLSTM Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02946)**  `arXiv:2608.02946`  
  _Yuliang Chen, Weiwei Shi, Rong Zablocki, Animesh Kumar, Jordan A. Carlson, Sheri J. Hartman, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate detection of sedentary behavior is important for studying health risks related to prolonged sitting, but posture-based classification remains challenging with wearable sensors, especially at the wrist. We study whether a deep learning model trained on hip-worn accelerometer data can transfer to wrist-worn accelerometer data for sitting versus non-sitting classification. We use CHAP, a CNN-BiLSTM model originally developed for hip accelerometers, and evaluate its zero-shot performance on wrist data as well as its adaptation through finetuning with varying amounts of labeled wrist data. Experiments are conducted on the iWatch dataset with ground-truth posture labels derived from wearable cameras. The hip-trained model performs strongly on hip data without retraining, but accuracy drops on wrist data due to sensor placement shift. Finetuning CHAP provides consistent advantages over transformer models trained from scratch. These findings suggest that hip-based pretraining provides a useful starting point for wrist deployment, while highlighting the need for wrist-specific adaptation to handle higher signal variability.
  </details>

- **[DP-IVON-Gradsq: Differentially Private Squared-Gradient Improved Variational Online Newton](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23649)**  `arXiv:2607.23649`  
  _Nour Jamoussi, Ikram Dridi, Giuseppe Serra, Marios Kountouris_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Differential privacy provides formal privacy guarantees for training neural networks on sensitive data, while Bayesian deep learning offers a principled framework for uncertainty-aware prediction. Combining these two objectives remains challenging, as privacy noise can interact with the stochasticity introduced by Bayesian posterior sampling. In this work, we investigate differentially private variational Bayesian learning through the Improved Variational Online Newton (IVON) optimizer. We introduce DP-IVON-Gradsq, a private variant of IVON. The proposed method constructs its curvature estimate from the privatized gradient using a noise-corrected squared-gradient estimator, reducing the direct interaction between posterior-sampling noise and privacy noise while preserving the Adam-like computational efficiency of IVON. We evaluate DP-IVON-Gradsq on CIFAR-10 against the standard private optimizers DP-SGD and DP-Adam over a range of privacy budgets. The results show that DP-IVON-Gradsq is competitive under weak-to-moderate privacy constraints, i.e., large-to-moderate values of $\varepsilon$, while degrading under strong privacy. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Unbiased Open World Regularization for Fair Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22149)**  `arXiv:2607.22149`  
  _Léo Nicollier, Marc Pic, Pablo Musé, Enric Meinhardt-Llopis, Gabriele Facciolo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite recent advances, self-supervised learning (SSL) models and Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) remain susceptible to spurious correlations. Many anti-collapse regularizers constrain the global representation distribution to match a prescribed target, such as a multivariate Gaussian or the uniform distribution on the sphere. However, global matching does not prevent groups defined by a bias attribute from occupying distinct regions of the representation space. We show that existing encoder-only debiasing regularizers, including EnD and FSCL, can be interpreted as restricted forms of conditional distribution matching. Building on this interpretation, we introduce Unbiased Open World (UOW), which matches the representation distribution within each observed bias group to a common target distribution. At the population optimum, using a discriminative conditional discrepancy guarantees independence between the representation and the targeted attribute. We instantiate UOW in Gaussian and spherical spaces and evaluate it across controlled synthetic settings and real-world fairness benchmarks. Across Synthetic Engraving, Colored MNIST, CelebA, and Waterbirds, conditional matching substantially reduces bias compared with global regularization while preserving useful downstream representations. Jointly conditioning on multiple bias attributes further reduces intersectional disparities missed by separate marginal constraints. Finally, experiments on CelebA show that models can be trained from random initialization using UOW as a pretraining objective, provided checkpoints are selected according to fairness-aware validation criteria.
  </details>

- **[Revisiting data-driven dynamic security assessment with a tabular foundation model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16031)**  `arXiv:2607.16031`  
  _Olayiwola Arowolo, Maosheng Yang, Jochen Cremer_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data-driven pre-fault dynamic security assessment (DSA) rapidly evaluates the dynamic risk of credible contingencies on a power system using machine learning (ML). Existing approaches face two limitations. First, they require a large labelled database for training, with a separate model trained, tuned, and maintained for each contingency in a potentially long list of credible contingencies. Second, the trained models generalize poorly to unseen contingencies. This work addresses these limitations by using a tabular foundation model (TFM) that assesses stability through in-context learning, and requires no retraining or hyperparameter optimization. We use a single TFM to assess many contingencies at once, removing the need to train one classifier per contingency. We also characterize when using electrical distance coordinates (EDC) as continuous features enables the TFM to generalize to unseen contingencies and when it does not, showing how a few labelled samples can reliably improve generalization. Through comprehensive case studies on the IEEE 68-bus system, we show that a single TFM attains an average Macro F1 score of about 90\% with only 120 labelled samples per contingency, outperforming both classical ML and deep learning baselines, without any model retraining or hyperparameter tuning. For held-out contingencies, we show that using just 10 labelled samples of the new contingency with a modified EDC encoding matches the balanced accuracy of the best achievable transfer learning oracle model, which requires fully labelled data and is not deployable in practice. Overall, this initial study paves the way for developing and deploying foundation models for power system operations, with possible applications across multiple operational tasks.
  </details>

- **[Optimal and Efficient Contextual Combinatorial Semi-bandits with General Function Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13686)**  `arXiv:2607.13686`  
  _Hao Qin, Chicheng Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study the contextual combinatorial semi-bandit (CCSB) problem, where at each round, the learner observes a context, selects a combinatorial action consisting of a subset of basic arms, and receives the reward of each selected arm; its goal is to maximize its cumulative reward over time. While many previous works focus on CCSB problems with linear reward function approximation, the design of efficient algorithms for the CCSB problem with general reward function approximation is limited. In this work, we study the realizable setting where the learner has access to a reward function class $\mathcal{F}$ that contains the true reward function, and propose the $\mathrm{SquareCB{.}Comb}$ algorithm. We prove that $\mathrm{SquareCB{.}Comb}$ achieves a minimax optimal regret bound of $O(\sqrt{m A T \log |\mathcal{F}|})$, where $A$ is the number of arms, $m$ is the maximum number of arms in a combinatorial action, and $T$ is the time horizon. In addition, $\mathrm{SquareCB{.}Comb}$ is computationally efficient when we have an online regression oracle that can efficiently predict rewards of all arms given contexts, and the combinatorial action set admits either efficient linear optimization or efficient sampling given any target marginal probability on arms. We empirically verify the effectiveness of our algorithm in webpage recommendation and contextual shortest path problems.
  </details>

- **[Distributed Sketching on Data Partitions for OLS Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07888)**  `arXiv:2607.07888`  
  _Luyuan Yang, Brayden Garner, Shayan Shafaei, Chao Lan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper studies a scalable approach for ordinary least squares (OLS) regression based on distributed sketching. It distributes small sketches of a large data set across multiple workers, has each worker construct a local OLS estimator from its sketch, and averages the local estimators into a global one. Prior studies assume each sketch is generated from all data, but this sketching step has high computation cost, sometimes even higher than standard OLS. Instead, we propose to generate each sketch from a partitioned subset of the data, which allows the sketching cost to decrease with more workers. Under the fixed design setting, we characterize the exact excess loss of the global estimator. This loss is higher than the loss established in prior studies, but remains close to it when the average Burg divergence between subset covariances is small. Since more workers tend to increase the divergence under our partition design, this reveals a tradeoff between the computational and statistical efficiency of our estimator.
  </details>

- **[Hyperparameter Transfer in Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05017)**  `arXiv:2607.05017`  
  _Gage DeZoort, Boris Hanin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The performance of deep learning models crucially depends on the settings of hyperparameters like learning rate, initialization scale, and weight decay. Hyperparameter transfer aims to make near-optimal hyperparameter settings consistent across model scale, so that large models can be optimized by proxy tuning their smaller, cheaper-to-optimize counterparts. While transfer principles are well-studied in the context of dense neural networks in language and vision tasks, they remain comparatively under-explored for graph neural networks (GNNs). We develop and validate a transfer parameterization for GNNs trained with SGD, Adam, and AdamW. Through theoretical scaling analyses and controlled experiments, we show that the proposed parameterization yields stable feature updates, learning rate transfer, and improved performance as width and depth increase. For SGD, we identify graph-dependent first-layer correction factors and show that their use can accelerate early training in graphs with sparse bag-of-words inputs. For Adam, we explore how different message passing normalizations affect early- and late-training transfer behavior, illustrating the importance of message passing normalization and advocating for an associated hyperparameter. For AdamW, we adapt a parameterization that allows for the joint transfer of weight decay and learning rate. Together, these results provide a practical recipe for scaling GNNs across a variety of learning tasks and training scenarios.
  </details>

- **[Batch-Invariant Spectral Intelligence for Robust and Explainable Insect Authentication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26757)**  `arXiv:2606.26757`  
  _Majharulislam Babor, Giacomo Rossi, Annalisa Altavilla, Oliver Schlüter, Marina M.-C. Höhne_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Edible insects offer an efficient source of alternative protein, requiring less land and water and emitting less greenhouse gas than conventional livestock. However, their successful integration into the food supply chain demands reliable species authentication to control allergen exposure, prevent adulteration, and meet regulatory standards. Near-infrared spectroscopy provides a rapid analytical tool, but its performance drops when applied to production batches unseen during training due to batch-to-batch variation in spectral measurements. We introduce the Batch-Invariant Spectral Network (BISN), an end-to-end framework that combines a learnable preprocessing module, initialised with Savitzky-Golay filtering, with an unsupervised, entropy-regularised auxiliary branch connected through a gradient reversal layer. The branch uses no batch labels and supplies opposing entropy gradients to the auxiliary head and preprocessing module. Using 2,700 spectra from three species (Acheta domesticus, Hermetia illucens, and Tenebrio molitor) collected across three independent production batches, BISN achieves a mean leave-one-batch-out accuracy of 0.93 (standard deviation 0.04), outperforming the strongest baseline by four percentage points (p=0.002). Further insights gained by using explainable AI confirm that model decisions consistently rely on the lipid and protein absorption regions across all folds, connecting predictive performance to known insect biochemistry. BISN addresses both cross-batch robustness and biochemical interpretability for automated insect species authentication in a controlled benchtop setting. The source code and dataset are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Decomposing one-class support vector machine into an ensemble of one-data support vector machines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16002)**  `arXiv:2606.16002`  
  _Toshitaka Hayashi, Dalibor Cimr, Hamido Fujita, Richard Cimler_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  One-class Support Vector Machine(OCSVM) is a variant of support vector machine(SVM) for one-class classification (OCC). However, OCSVM has scalability issues with large datasets. This paper proposes an acceleration strategy for OCSVM. The idea is to decompose the dataset into samples and train OCSVM models for single data points. Subsequently, ensemble learning is applied to combine all models to compute the OCSVM model for the dataset. In addition, further acceleration is achieved through a support vector selection strategy with an OCSVM model trained on the average of the training samples. The experiment compared the proposal and traditional OCSVM using the Python package. The proposed strategy is faster than traditional OCSVM, while achieving similar classification results. Moreover, the proposed strategy can create a one-to-one correspondence between samples and models. Source code is uploaded atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Diffuse AI Control on Fuzzy Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08892)**  `arXiv:2606.08892`  
  _Mikhail Terekhov, Caglar Gulcehre, Vivek Hebbar, Joe Benton_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI models deployed in critical domains, such as AI safety research, may subtly sabotage our efforts due to misalignment. Diffuse AI Control is a subfield of AI safety concerned with mitigating risks from AI sabotage distributed over long deployment horizons (diffuse threats). These risks are particularly pernicious on fuzzy tasks, i.e. tasks which are hard to grade or require intuition. To understand diffuse threats on fuzzy tasks, we introduce a framework that considers AI control as an adversarial game between a blue team and a red team. The blue team uses a weak trusted model to construct a weak score against which they would train a strong, potentially subversive model to remove the subversion propensity if it were present. The red team then tries to find model behaviors that are rated highly by the weak score, and thus might not be trained out, but actually correspond to poor performance. We test our framework on the task of writing experimental proposals for research questions from recent ML papers. We use a language model with access to the original paper as a proxy "ground-truth" scorer. Our red team discovers subversive behaviors using multi-objective evolutionary prompt optimization. We show that Opus~4.6 can write proposals that are worse according to the ground truth proxy than those of GPT-OSS-20B, while the weak scorer rates them as highly as the best proposals from Opus 4.6. We then propose an adversarial optimization algorithm for the blue team that discovers more robust prompts for the weak model. This algorithm produces a blue team prompt that our red team optimization fails to exploit. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SALT: When More Rollouts Don't Help in Group-Based Policy Optimization and How to Make Them Matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05800)**  `arXiv:2606.05800`  
  _Powei Chang, Jinpeng Zhang, Chaoqun Sun, MiniWell Tsao, Lianrui Li, Jianxiang Xiang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) often adopts GRPO-style group-relative updates, sampling multiple rollouts per prompt to construct normalized learning signals. However, merely increasing the number of rollouts does not reliably strengthen learning: under GRPO-style group normalization, per-rollout policy-gradient features can concentrate into a low-rank, signed geometry, causing substantial cancellation during aggregation and weakening the effective update. We address this failure mode with SALT, a Subspace-Adaptive geometry pLug-in componenT that uses sample-wise gradient geometry to reweight the coefficients of group-relative updates. SALT estimates a dominant shared subspace from the mini-batch Gram geometry, decomposes group-relative coefficients into shared and residual channels, and adaptively amplifies the residual channel when signed cancellation is severe. Across diverse reasoning-oriented RLVR benchmarks and model scales, SALT improves effective update geometry and performance without modifying the reward model or the rollout sampling procedure.
  </details>

- **[Denoise First, Orthogonalize Later: Understanding Momentum in Muon via Spectral Filtering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03899)**  `arXiv:2606.03899`  
  _Xianliang Li, Zihan Zhang, Weiyang Liu, Han Bao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Muon has recently demonstrated strong empirical performance in large language model training, but the theoretical role of momentum in Muon remains unclear. Existing analyses of Muon either remove momentum to study spectral updates in isolation, or retain momentum without explaining why it improves empirical performance. Our work bridges this gap by showing momentum in Muon acts as a spectral filter. Under a structured signal-plus-perturbation gradient model, we prove that momentum suppresses perturbations while preserving the dominant signal, thereby enlarging the spectral gap between them. This enlarged gap stabilizes the singular subspaces of the matrix passed to Muon's orthogonalization step, making the resulting update more reliable. We further show that applying momentum before orthogonalization achieves provably stronger alignment with the signal component of the gradient than either reversing this order or simply removing momentum. Experiments across diverse tasks, including LLM pretraining, support our theoretical analysis. More broadly, our theory offers a starting point for understanding the benefits of momentum in other matrix-based optimizers.
  </details>

- **[EmoTrack: Clinical-Semantic Modeling for Text-Based Depression Severity Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22286)**  `arXiv:2605.22286`  
  _Zhaomin Wu, Jiayi Li, Bingsheng He_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-based counseling is widely adopted across online mental health platforms, where automatically estimating depressive symptom severity from transcripts is vital for client safety and clinical monitoring. We therefore study prediction of the standard eight-item Patient Health Questionnaire (PHQ-8) total score directly from counseling sessions. Existing clinical-based methods obtain structured assessments through large language model inference but summarize away individual client turns. Training-based methods preserve turn semantics but must learn clinical structure from limited labels. Integrating these representations requires preserving clinical-feature identity and selecting evidence relevant to each symptom. We propose EmoTrack, which jointly encodes clinical-feature tokens and frozen client-turn embeddings, then uses PHQ-8 item queries to estimate total severity. Optional gated memory incorporates evidence from the preceding session. To support controlled evaluation in the multi-session setting, we introduce CounselArc, a synthetic counseling dataset with session-level PHQ-8 supervision for predicting current-session severity with optional prior-session context. Across real DAIC-WOZ interviews, transfer to E-DAIC, and three longitudinal datasets, EmoTrack achieves the lowest normalized aggregate error. It reduces DAIC-WOZ mean absolute error by 12.5% and aggregate error by approximately 8.6%, each relative to the strongest baseline for that measure.
  </details>

- **[Fast and Stable Triangular Inversion for Delta-Rule Linear Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21325)**  `arXiv:2605.21325`  
  _Aleksandros Sobczyk, Gioele Gottardo, Christos K. Matzoros, Mirko De Vita, Filip Skogh, Anastasios Zouzias, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linear attention has emerged as a cornerstone for efficient long-context architectures, as evidenced by its integration into state-of-the-art open-source models including Qwen3.5/3.6, Kimi Linear/K3, and RWKV-7. Models that incorporate linear attention layers with the so-called Delta-Rule involve the inversion of triangular matrices as a core sub-routine. This operation often forms a performance bottleneck, and, due to its high-sensitivity to numerical errors, it can significantly deteriorate end-to-end model accuracy if it is not carefully implemented. This work provides a systematic analysis of both direct and iterative triangular inversion algorithms, targeting methods that are rich in matrix products, and, therefore, have the potential to efficiently utilize modern hardware. To that end, our analysis covers a broad spectrum of mathematical and practical aspects, with a heavy focus on numerical stability, computational complexity, and, ultimately, hardware efficiency and practical considerations. We provide a rigorous experimental evaluation to verify these properties in practical scenarios, and in low-precision floating-point representations, highlighting the strengths and limitations of each method. Performance benchmarks on NPUs reveal up to $4.3\times$ speed-up against the state-of-the-art implementations of SGLang for triangular matrix inversion, leading to significant performance improvements on the entire layer level, while maintaining end-to-end model accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Learning POMDP World Models from Observations with Language-Model Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13740)**  `arXiv:2605.13740`  
  _Valentin Six, Frederik Panse, Mathis Fajeau, Lancelot Da Costa, Mridul Sharma, Alfonso Amayuelas, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whether navigating a building, operating a robot, or playing a game, an agent that acts effectively in an environment must first learn an internal model of how that environment works. Partially-observable Markov decision processes (POMDPs) provide a flexible modeling class for such internal world models, but learning them from observation-action trajectories alone is challenging and typically requires extensive environment interaction. We ask whether language-model priors can reduce costly interaction by leveraging prior knowledge, and introduce Pinductor (POMDP-inductor): an LLM proposes candidate POMDP models from a few observation-action trajectories and iteratively refines them to optimize a belief-based likelihood score. Despite relying on strictly less information, Pinductor achieves comparable performance and sample efficiency to LLM-based POMDP learning methods with privileged access to hidden states. Moreover, it is up to 330 times more sample-efficient than standard POMDP baselines such as DreamerV3, at comparable computational cost. Further results show that performance scales with LLM capability and degrades gracefully as semantic information about the environment is withheld. Together, these results position language-model priors as a practical tool for sample-efficient world-model learning under partial observability, and a step toward generalist agents in real-world environments. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Data-Driven and Cycle-Agnostic Covariate Selection for Nonparametric Causal Effect Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06385)**  `arXiv:2605.06385`  
  _Ana Leticia Garcez Vicente, Gijs van Seeventer, Saber Salehkaleybar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Estimating causal effects from observational data requires identifying valid adjustment sets. This task is especially challenging in realistic settings where latent confounding and feedback loops are present. Existing approaches typically assume acyclicity or rely on global causal structure learning, limiting applicability and computational efficiency. In this work, we study a local, data-driven method for covariate selection based on conditional independence information under a pre-treatment assumption. While this method is known to be sound and complete in acyclic causal models, its validity in the presence of cycles has remained unclear. Our main contribution is to show that these guarantees extend to cyclic causal models. In particular, our result relies on the invariance of conditional independence assertions under $\sigma$-acyclification. These findings establish a unified, cycle-agnostic perspective on covariate selection, which allows for causal effect estimation using any consistent estimator. Therefore, showing that the method applies across cyclic and acyclic settings without modification. Empirically, we validate this on extensive synthetic data, showing reliable performance in cyclic causal models.
  </details>

- **[LINC: Decoupling Local Consequence Scoring from Hidden Matching in Constructive Neural Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06332)**  `arXiv:2605.06332`  
  _Shaofeng Qin, Li Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Constructive neural routing solvers usually score the next action by matching a decoder context against candidate embeddings computed once per instance from static node features such as coordinates, demands, and time windows; deterministic one-step consequences such as travel, waiting, slack, and capacity changes are not provided as inputs and must be inferred implicitly by the model. We propose LINC, a decoder-side candidate decision architecture that decouples computing these consequences from learning how much they matter. The environment computes the consequences explicitly at each step; candidate-level consequences are scored by a state-conditioned shared linear comparator, while feasible-set summaries modulate the decoder context. This keeps the cached embeddings and standard global matching while giving the scoring function direct access to deterministic transition quantities. The Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem with Time Windows (CVRPTW) serves as the main constrained-routing testbed, and the same interface extends to the Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem (CVRP) and Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP). Across external benchmarks and no-retraining scale-transfer settings, LINC consistently improves strong neural baselines, with the advantage becoming more pronounced as test size moves further beyond the training scale, especially on constrained routing problems.
  </details>

- **[In-Context Black-Box Optimization with Unreliable Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06187)**  `arXiv:2605.06187`  
  _Nicolas Samuel Blumer, Julien Martinelli, Samuel Kaski_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Black-box optimization in science and engineering often comes with side information: experts, simulators, pretrained predictors, or heuristics can suggest promising candidates. Such feedback can accelerate search, but can also be biased, input-dependent, or misleading. Feedback-aware Bayesian optimization methods typically handle tasks independently, limiting adaptation across feedback sources. In-context optimizers enable cross-task adaptation, but usually rely only on optimization history at test time. We study feedback-informed in-context black-box optimization (FICBO), where a pretrained optimizer conditions on both the observed history and cheap auxiliary feedback for the current candidate set. We introduce structured priors over how auxiliary feedback relates to the objective, and use them to pretrain a feedback-aware transformer across regimes ranging from highly informative to misleading. At test time, the model estimates source reliability in context by comparing observed objective values with auxiliary signals, improving query selection. On synthetic and real-world tasks, FICBO effectively exploits informative feedback while remaining robust to weak or misleading sources. Empirical investigations further illustrate how the model perceives test-time sources, offering insights into its interpretability and decision-making process.
  </details>

- **[Hyper-Dimensional Fingerprints as Molecular Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27810)**  `arXiv:2604.27810`  
  _Jonas Teufel, Luca Torresi, André Eberhard, Pascal Friederich_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Numerical molecular representations underpin virtual screening, property prediction, and materials discovery. Conventional fingerprints are efficient and deterministic but lose structural information through hash-based compression at low dimensionalities, whereas graph neural networks recover expressiveness through compute-intensive, task-specific training. Here, we introduce hyperdimensional fingerprints (HDF), which compose the chemical attributes of atoms and their multi-hop environments through algebraic operations on high-dimensional vectors, producing deterministic representations without any training. Across diverse property-prediction benchmarks, HDF ranks consistently among the best representations across datasets and models. Crucially, HDF encodes structure more efficiently: at low dimensionalities, its distances correlate with graph edit distance more strongly than Morgan fingerprints', yielding effective similarity metrics and competitive nearest-neighbor regression at just 64 components. In Bayesian optimization, HDF-based surrogates are more sample-efficient than Morgan fingerprints with a Tanimoto kernel for most of the tested objectives. These results establish HDF as compact, training-free representations for property prediction, similarity search, and sample-efficient optimization.
  </details>

- **[MemReward: Graph-Based Experience Memory for LLM Reward Prediction with Limited Labels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19310)**  `arXiv:2603.19310`  
  _Tianyang Luo, Tao Feng, Zhigang Hua, Yan Xie, Shuang Yang, Ge Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning improves large language model (LLM) reasoning using reward signals on sampled rollouts. When reward labels are available for only a subset of rollouts, restricting policy updates to that subset leaves the remaining rollouts unused for policy optimization. Semi-supervised learning propagates labels from labeled to unlabeled samples, but existing methods either diffuse labels with fixed similarity weights or treat each sample independently, ignoring the relations among queries, thinking processes, and answers. We propose MemReward, a graph-based experience memory that propagates rewards to unlabeled rollouts during online policy optimization. MemReward stores queries and the rollouts (thinking processes and final answers) of an initial LLM policy as nodes of a heterogeneous graph connected by similarity and structural edges, and warms up a GNN on labeled rollouts to propagate rewards over this graph. During online RL fine-tuning, unlabeled rollouts are attached to the graph by query similarity, and the policy is updated with ground-truth rewards on labeled rollouts and GNN-predicted rewards on the rest. We evaluate MemReward on 13 benchmarks in mathematics, question answering, and code generation with verifiable rewards, and further with LLM-judge, human-preference, and continuous rewards. With ground-truth rewards on only 20% of rollouts, MemReward achieves 96.6% of the performance of fully supervised training (Oracle) on Qwen2.5-1.5B and 97.3% on Qwen2.5-3B, where it also outperforms label propagation and FixMatch-style self-training. It carries over to Llama backbones up to 8B and closely approaches Oracle on out-of-domain tasks.
  </details>

- **[Manifold-Matching Autoencoders: Embedding Data into Chosen Geometric Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16568)**  `arXiv:2603.16568`  
  _Laurent Cheret, Vincent Létourneau, Isar Nejadgholi, Chris Drummond, Hussein Al Osman, Maia Fraser_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Manipulating the structure of data in the latent space of an autoencoder is a long-standing goal of representation learning. We propose a simple regularization, Manifold Matching, that imposes an explicit geometric structure on that latent space. The idea is to take a reference representation R of the data, which need not be the raw input X fed to the network, and to minimize, batch-wise, the mean squared error between the pairwise distances in R and those in the latent space Z. Although the autoencoder still learns to reconstruct X, the shape of its latent space comes to mimic that of R. The objective requires no labels, only reference distances, and because those are scalars, the dimensionality of R is decoupled from that of Z: any reference can guide any bottleneck. Using this single instrument we study how different geometric structures can be imposed on a latent space, comparing against autoencoders built on geometric or topological regularizers. Across fifteen datasets under a shared protocol with statistical testing, the resulting Manifold-Matching Autoencoder (MMAE) matches the global-structure quality of persistence-based autoencoders, statistically tying RTD-AE on distance correlation and triplet accuracy, and stays competitive on the local and topological metrics that other methods lead, at the computational cost of a plain autoencoder: it uses only a batched distance loss, with no persistence computation. We frame MMAE not as a universal winner but as an efficient and controllable regularizer whose reference selects which structure the latent inherits, and which turns any fixed embedding into a reusable out-of-sample encoder. All experiments use a two-dimensional bottleneck.
  </details>

- **[PREBA: Surgical Duration Prediction via PCA-Weighted Retrieval-Augmented LLMs and Bayesian Averaging Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.13275)**  `arXiv:2603.13275`  
  _Wanyin Wu, Kanxue Li, Baosheng Yu, Haoyun Zhao, Yibing Zhan, Dapeng Tao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate prediction of surgical duration is pivotal for hospital resource management. Although recent supervised learning approaches, from machine learning (ML) to fine-tuned large language models (LLMs), have shown strong performance, they remain constrained by the need for high-quality labeled data and computationally intensive training. In contrast, zero-shot LLM inference offers a promising training-free alternative, but it lacks grounding in institution-specific clinical evidence, making its predictions clinically misaligned and prone to instability. To address these limitations, we present PREBA, a retrieval-augmented framework that integrates PCA-weighted retrieval and Bayesian averaging to ground LLM reasoning in institution-specific evidence without requiring task-specific fine-tuning. Specifically, PREBA first encodes heterogeneous clinical features into a unified representation. It then performs PCA-weighted retrieval, weighting each dimension by its contribution to the variation across institutional cases, instead of treating them as equally informative, and conditions the LLM to generate multiple candidate predictions. Finally, Bayesian averaging consolidates these predictions with population-level statistical priors, yielding a calibrated estimate that remains stable and clinically plausible. We evaluate PREBA on four real-world datasets spanning different institutions and languages, using three state-of-the-art LLMs, including Qwen3, DeepSeek-R1, and HuatuoGPT-o1. PREBA consistently outperforms zero-shot inference, reducing MAE by up to 64.9% and raising R2 to as high as 0.63. It further attains accuracy competitive with, and on several datasets superior to, fully supervised ML methods, while offering greater interpretability through retrieved exemplars and explicit priors. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning as Compression: Unifying Budget Forcing via the Conditional Information Bottleneck](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08462)**  `arXiv:2603.08462`  
  _Fabio Valerio Massoli, Andrey Kuzmin, Arash Behboodi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting improves LLM accuracy on complex tasks but often increases token usage and inference cost. Existing ``Budget Forcing'' methods reduce cost via fine-tuning with heuristic length penalties, suppressing both essential reasoning and redundant filler. We recast efficient reasoning as a lossy compression problem under the Information Bottleneck (IB) principle, and identify a key theoretical gap when applying naive IB to transformers: attention violates the Markov property between prompt, reasoning trace, and response. To resolve this issue, we model CoT generation under the Conditional Information Bottleneck (CIB) principle, where the reasoning trace $Z$ acts as a computational bridge that contains only the information about the response $Y$ that is not directly accessible from the prompt $X$. This yields a general Reinforcement Learning objective: maximize task reward while compressing completions under a prior over reasoning traces, subsuming common heuristics (e.g., length penalties) as special cases (e.g., uniform priors). In contrast to naive token-counting approaches, we introduce a semantic prior that measures token cost by surprisal under a language model. Crucially, the prior is queried only for token-level log-probabilities, adding negligible overhead to the training loop. Empirically, our CIB objective prunes reasoning redundancy while preserving fluency and logic, improving accuracy at moderate compression and enabling aggressive compression with minimal accuracy drop. These gains generalize across model families and task domains, confirming CIB as a domain-agnostic CoT compression framework.
  </details>

- **[SEAR: Sample Efficient Action Chunking Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01891)**  `arXiv:2603.01891`  
  _C. F. Maximilian Nagy, Onur Celik, Emiliyan Gospodinov, Florian Seligmann, Weiran Liao, Aryan Kaushik, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Action chunking improves exploration and accelerates value propagation in long-horizon reinforcement learning, but naively applying off-policy methods to the temporally extended action space at reduced decision frequency offsets these gains, leading to poor sample efficiency. Existing online action chunking methods address these issues through computationally expensive critic-only approaches or by relying on offline data. We introduce SEAR, a sample-efficient off-policy algorithm that enables online reinforcement learning with action chunks by addressing both challenges. To handle high-dimensional action sequences, SEAR employs a causal transformer critic trained with multi-horizon targets that provide a training signal for every prefix of an action chunk, effectively increasing the useful gradients per sample. To restore decision frequency, SEAR operates with a receding horizon and random replanning, ensuring uniform state coverage while combining the fast value propagation of large chunks with the reactivity of small ones. SEAR outperforms state-of-the-art online reinforcement learning methods including SimbaV2 on challenging Metaworld manipulation tasks. Beyond online RL, applying SEAR to the offline-to-online method QC improves its performance on the demanding OGBench cube-triple tasks.
  </details>

- **[High-Dimensional Robust Mean Estimation from Doubly Corrupted and Heterogeneous Batches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20698)**  `arXiv:2602.20698`  
  _Maryam Aliakbarpour, Vladimir Braverman, Yuhan Liu, Junze Yin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern data systems often require aggregating information from multiple sources: some might be entirely untrustworthy, while the remaining presumably reliable sources may still suffer from a small amount of corruption. We study robust mean estimation under this practical scenario, which is formulated through the double corruption model: samples are divided among $N$ users, each with $n$ samples. $\varepsilon$ fraction of the users could be arbitrarily corrupted, while each of the remaining ``good'' users still have $\alpha$-fraction ($\alpha>0$) of corrupted samples. The goal is to estimate the unknown mean $\mu$ with small $\ell_2$ error. This is a challenging scenario because we must handle two types of adversarial corruption: user-level corruption and sample-level corruption. When the unknown distribution has covariance matrix bounded by the identity matrix, we design a sum-of-squares (SoS) algorithm that achieves an $\ell_2$ error of $\sqrt{\alpha}$ with probability at least $2/3$ as long as the total number of samples $nN=\widetilde{\Omega}(d/\alpha)$. The estimation error and sample complexity are min-max optimal under the mild condition. For comparison, treating all $nN$ samples as $(\varepsilon+\alpha)$-corrupted and applying standard robust mean estimation only achieves an error of $\sqrt{\varepsilon+\alpha}$. Thus, our new algorithm removes the adverse effect of untrustworthy users and achieves the optimal error as if only sample-level corruption is present. Also, our SoS algorithm extends to the heterogeneous setting where each user's local mean could be additionally shifted by $\sqrt{\beta}$ in $\ell_2$ distance. We show that an error of $O(\sqrt{\alpha+\beta})$ can be achieved with probability at least $2/3$ when $nN=\widetilde{\Omega}(d/(\alpha+\beta))$.
  </details>

- **[Golden Layers and Where to Find Them: Improved Knowledge Editing for Large Language Models Via Layer Gradient Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20207)**  `arXiv:2602.20207`
